# Political Theory — Topic 10: State, Civil Society, Nation and Internationalism (Live Learning Session)

## The question this topic exists to answer

Topic 09 ended by showing that political analysis must borrow its evidence from history, economics, sociology, psychology and philosophy. This topic asks the question that borrowing forces on you next: **if politics has to be studied through so many other disciplines, is there anything left that is distinctively its own?**

The chapter's answer is a single word, and it is the word that some political scientists wanted to delete: **the state**. ⚠️ The uncomfortable part is that the chapter opens by reproducing, at full strength, the argument for abandoning that word — and only then explains why it survived.

🔑 The whole topic is built on one move, repeated five times. Take the state, place a near-neighbour beside it — **society**, **civil society**, **government**, **nation**, and finally the **world** — and show exactly where the two separate. ❌ Every weak answer on this topic collapses one of those five pairs. ✅ Every strong answer can state, for each pair, what is shared, what is not, and what is lost politically when the distinction is dropped.

A second trap sits beside the first. Because the chapter is called *Concept of the State*, candidates write answers that are lists of four elements and nothing more. ❌ Four nouns earn almost nothing. What earns marks is the ability to say, for each element, *what work it does, what the chapter refuses to add to the list, and what follows politically when the element is missing*. This session is built around that four-part move and repeats it for every element, every distinction and every historical form the chapter names.

A third demand is added by the second source. ⚠️ The chapter was written in 2009 and has nothing to say about **secularism** or **multiculturalism**, yet the examined record for this subject area is dominated by exactly those two words. ✅ A separate supplied source carries them, and the last five lessons teach them on their own terms, clearly marked as a different source with a different voice.

## Lesson 1 — The central theme, and the three behavioural objections

Progress: 1 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — why the concept survived

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §2 and §4.1, with the Advanced companion's §3.1 and §6, read against the chapter's unnumbered opening page (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.147 = printed 132), which supplies the two traditional definitions, the three lettered behavioural objections and Easton's four-way ambiguity passage.
CA search: "concept of the state abandoned behavioural political science Easton 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on a 1950s methodological dispute reported in a 2009 textbook. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### 1. Start with the embarrassment

Most candidates begin this topic by reciting four elements. The chapter begins somewhere far less comfortable: with the claim that the word **state** should be deleted from political science altogether.

⚠️ That is not a rhetorical device. It is the chapter's real starting point, and it controls everything that follows. If you do not know what the objection was, you cannot explain why the four elements are worth listing — because you will not know what the list is answering.

### 2. The traditional position, in two sentences

✅ The chapter opens by establishing how central the concept once was, and it does so through two named authorities:

> The concept of the state has figured as the central theme of traditional political theory. R.G. Gettel (*Political Science*; 1949) defined political science as 'the science of the state', while J.W. Garner (*Political Science and Government*; 1928) claimed that 'political science begins and ends with the state'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.147 (printed 132)

| Authority | Work and year | The claim | What kind of claim it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ R.G. Gettel | *Political Science*, **1949** | Political science is **'the science of the state'** | ⚠️ A definition of the discipline by its object |
| ✅ J.W. Garner | *Political Science and Government*, **1928** | Political science **'begins and ends with the state'** | ⚠️ A boundary claim — nothing before, nothing after |

⚠️ Garner's formula is the stronger of the two, and it is the one worth memorising. "Begins and ends" is not a description of emphasis; it is a statement that the state exhausts the subject. ❌ A discipline that accepts it has no room for a politics that happens outside the state's formal organisation.

### 3. The objection, and why it has exactly three limbs

✅ The chapter then reports that **some exponents of the behavioural approach** suggested **abandoning the concept of the state altogether**. Their main objection is that the concept **does not help in understanding political reality or the political process**, and it is set out in three lettered parts:

```text
   (a) FORMALISM
       'state' refers to a formal concept
       while real politics TRANSCENDS the
       formal organization of the state
                 |
   (b) THE SLIDE INTO ENDS
       the 'state' is usually conceived in
       terms of the 'ends' of the state
       -> drags us to the realm of MORAL
          PHILOSOPHY, far removed from the
          real world of politics
                 |
   (c) THE EXCLUSION PROBLEM
       the concept postulates a particular
       TYPE of organization, which excludes
       top organizations of certain societies,
       real or imaginary
       -> introduces 'pre-state' societies
          and 'stateless' societies
       -> political organization is NOT a
          universal phenomenon
```

⚠️ Read the three limbs as three different kinds of failure, because that is how an examiner will test them.

- ⚠️ **(a) is an empirical failure.** The word points at an institution; politics happens partly outside it. Parties, pressure groups, movements, bureaucratic bargaining and voting behaviour are political and are not the formal state.
- ⚠️ **(b) is a methodological failure.** Defining the state by its *ends* turns a descriptive enquiry into an evaluative one. ➡️ This is the fact-value problem that **Topic 08** owns in full; here it appears only as a reason for distrusting one word.
- ⚠️ **(c) is a conceptual failure.** If "state" names a particular *type* of organisation, then societies organised differently have no state — and the chapter notes the consequence precisely: the assumption that **political organization is not a universal phenomenon**.

🔑 Limb (c) is the sharpest and the least quoted. It says that the concept of the state is not merely narrow but *parochial*: it generalises one historical form into the definition of politics itself.

### 4. Easton's passage, and what it actually shows

✅ The chapter then quotes **David Easton**, *Political System — An Inquiry into the State of Political Science* (1953):

> One person sees the state as the embodiment of the moral spirit, its concrete expression; another, as the instrument of exploitation used by one class against others. One author defines it as simply an aspect of society, distinguishable from it only analytically; another, as simply a synonym for government; and still another, as a separate and unique association among a large number of other associations such as the church, trade unions, and similar voluntary groups.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.147 (printed 132)

✅ Four distinct senses are named in that passage, and a fifth is implied. Set them out as a table, because the whole chapter is an answer to this list:

| # | Sense of "state" in Easton's passage | Whose position it resembles | Where the chapter answers it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ The **embodiment of the moral spirit**, its concrete expression | ⚠️ Idealist — Hegel | Lesson 18 |
| 2 | ✅ The **instrument of exploitation used by one class against others** | ⚠️ Marxist | Lessons 14, 18 |
| 3 | ✅ Simply **an aspect of society**, distinguishable only analytically | ⚠️ Sociological reduction | Lessons 11-12 |
| 4 | ✅ Simply **a synonym for government** | ⚠️ Everyday usage | Lesson 17 |
| 5 | ✅ A **separate and unique association among a large number of other associations** such as the church, trade unions and similar voluntary groups | ⚠️ Pluralist — Laski's family of positions | Lesson 4 |

⚠️ Notice what the quotation is doing. It is not a survey offered for interest; it is an argument from **ambiguity**. Easton's point is that a term carrying five incompatible meanings cannot do analytical work.

✅ The chapter records his conclusion in his own words: after dwelling on these ambiguities in some detail, Easton came to the conclusion that the word 'state' **'ought to be abandoned entirely'**.

🔑 Memorise that this is a **quotation inside a quotation** — the chapter quotes Easton quoting his own verdict — and that the verdict is total. ❌ It is not "use the word carefully"; it is *abandon entirely*.

### 5. The hidden structure: an answer is already visible in the objection

⚠️ Here is the move that lifts an answer from average to strong. Easton's five senses are not five arbitrary errors. Four of them are **collapses of a distinction**, and the chapter's whole middle section consists of restoring exactly those distinctions.

```text
   Easton's sense            ->   Collapsed distinction  ->  Restored at
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   'an aspect of society'    ->   state  = society       ->  L11-L12
   'synonym for government'  ->   state  = government    ->  L17
   'embodiment of moral      ->   state  = ideal state   ->  L18
     spirit'                      (no control mechanism)
   'instrument of            ->   state  = ruling class  ->  L18
     exploitation'                  instrument
   'unique association       ->   (the one sense the     ->  L4
     among associations'           chapter partly keeps)
```

⚠️ That is the architecture of the chapter in a single box. ✅ The chapter's reply to the charge of ambiguity is not to deny the ambiguity but to *resolve* it, pair by pair.

### 6. Comparison: why the objection came from one tradition only

✅ The chapter adds a sentence that candidates almost always drop, and it is worth marks because it locates the dispute:

> It is important to note that distaste for the term 'state' was confined to some exponents of liberal political theory, especially to some American political scientists.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

| Tradition | Attitude to the term | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Some American liberal behaviouralists | ❌ Abandon it | Formalism, moral overtones, parochialism |
| ✅ Marxist political theory | ✅ Keep and sharpen it | The term classifies historical types of political organisation |
| ✅ Empirically-oriented liberal scholars of development | ✅ Keep it as **state-building** | They needed a word for what was being built |

⚠️ The sentence does two things at once. It narrows the objection — this was never the whole discipline's view — and it prepares Lesson 2, where each of the other two columns becomes a route by which the concept returned.

### 7. The strongest objection to the chapter's own framing, with its reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter rebuts the behaviouralists by *counting users*: Marxists used the word, developmentalists used the word, Skocpol brought it back. ⚠️ But popularity is not validity. If the concept really is formal, morally loaded and parochial, then the fact that three groups kept using it shows only that three groups were comfortable with a defective instrument.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter does not rest on usage alone, though its surface narrative is a usage narrative. ✅ The substantive reply is the **Weberian redefinition** of Lesson 3: Weber defines the state **sociologically**, by its **specific means** rather than its ends, which answers limb (b) directly. ✅ And the element of **territory** plus the concession that nomadic communities have a **political system** but not a state — Lesson 6 — answers limb (c) by conceding the point and narrowing the claim: the state is a historically particular form, and the chapter never says otherwise.

⚠️ **Residual.** Limb (a) is the one the chapter never fully answers. ⚠️ It concedes that governments act through concrete institutions and that the state is **abstract**, but it does not show how a concept of the abstract state captures the informal politics the behaviouralists pointed at. ⚠️ The honest position is that the chapter secures the state as an *object of analysis* without securing it as the *whole* of politics — and it never claims the second.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Every question on the nature of the state starts here, because the opening page supplies both the traditional thesis and its strongest rebuttal in six sentences.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** A short stem — *Why was the concept of the state criticised in modern political theory?* — is answered by the three lettered limbs and nothing else. A longer stem adds Easton's five senses.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing "behaviouralists rejected the state because they preferred to study behaviour" is a restatement, not a reason. ✅ The three limbs are separately markable and are not interchangeable.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Open by naming Garner's **begins and ends** formula, then give the three limbs, then Easton's verdict. That is a complete, quotable opening paragraph that costs fewer than sixty words.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Gettel** (*Political Science*, 1949): political science is **'the science of the state'**.
2. ✅ **Garner** (*Political Science and Government*, 1928): political science **'begins and ends with the state'**.
3. ✅ Some **behavioural** exponents proposed **abandoning the concept of the state altogether**.
4. ✅ Objection (a): the term refers to a **formal concept** while real politics **transcends the formal organization**.
5. ✅ Objection (b): the state is conceived in terms of its **'ends'**, dragging enquiry into **moral philosophy**.
6. ✅ Objection (c): the concept postulates a **particular type of organization**, generating **'pre-state'** and **'stateless'** societies, and implying that political organisation is **not a universal phenomenon**.
7. ✅ **Easton**, *Political System — An Inquiry into the State of Political Science*, **1953**, lists the moral-spirit, class-instrument, aspect-of-society, synonym-for-government and unique-association senses.
8. ✅ Easton's verdict: the word **'ought to be abandoned entirely'**.
9. ✅ Distaste for the term was **confined to some exponents of liberal political theory, especially some American political scientists**.
10. ⚠️ Four of Easton's five senses are collapsed distinctions that the chapter later restores one by one.
11. ⚠️ Limb (b) is answered by Weber's means-based definition; limb (c) is answered by conceding historical particularity.
12. ⚠️ Limb (a) is never fully answered, and saying so is more accurate than pretending otherwise.
13. 🔑 Memorise the lettering: **(a) formal — (b) ends — (c) exclusion**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Behaviouralists rejected the concept of the state because it was old-fashioned and because they preferred quantitative methods." Identify precisely what has gone wrong, and restate the objection in a form that would earn marks.

**Model answer:** ✅ Neither reason appears in the chapter, and both substitute an attitude for an argument. ✅ The objection has three specific limbs: the term denotes a **formal concept** while real politics **transcends the formal organization of the state**; the state is usually conceived through its **'ends'**, which **drags us to the realm of moral philosophy**; and the concept postulates **a particular type of organization**, excluding the top organisations of some societies and introducing **'pre-state'** and **'stateless'** societies, so that political organisation appears **not** to be a universal phenomenon. ⚠️ Each limb attacks a different property — reference, method and scope — and each is answered differently later in the chapter. ❌ "Old-fashioned" attacks none of them; ❌ quantitative method is nowhere given as the ground of the objection.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present the objection as a general behaviouralist doctrine. ✅ The chapter says the distaste was **confined to some exponents of liberal political theory, especially to some American political scientists**, and that qualification is what makes the counter-evidence of Lesson 2 relevant rather than contradictory.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the grounds on which the concept of the state was criticised in modern political theory. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Traditional theory treated the state as its whole object — **Gettel** called political science **'the science of the state'** and **Garner** held that it **'begins and ends with the state'**. ⚠️ Modern criticism attacked that centrality on three distinct grounds. ✅ First, reference: the term denotes a **formal concept** while real politics **transcends the formal organization of the state**. ✅ Second, method: conceiving the state through its **'ends'** **drags us to the realm of moral philosophy, far removed from the real world of politics**. ✅ Third, scope: the concept postulates **a particular type of organization**, generating **'pre-state'** and **'stateless'** societies and implying that political organisation is **not a universal phenomenon**. ✅ **Easton** collected five incompatible usages and concluded the word **'ought to be abandoned entirely'**. ⚠️ The criticism is therefore about analytical utility, not antiquity, and its force is limited: it issued from **some** liberal, largely American scholars.

**Rubric [10]:** Gettel or Garner named with the exact formula **1** · all three lettered limbs, correctly distinguished **3** · Easton named with the 1953 work **1** · the **abandoned entirely** verdict **2** · at least two of the five Eastonian senses **1** · the confinement of the objection to some liberal, largely American scholars **1** · a closing sentence that treats the criticism as an argument about utility **1**.

---

## Lesson 2 — Easton's verdict, and the four ways the state came back

Progress: 2 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — why the concept survived

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §2 and §4.1, with the Advanced companion's §3.2 and §6, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133), which supplies the Marxist vocabulary list, the state-building usage, the Skocpol citation and the redefinition of the state as an active agent.
CA search: "bringing the state back in Skocpol state capacity debate 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the 1985 volume or on the chapter's report of it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem this lesson solves

Lesson 1 left the concept condemned. ⚠️ If the verdict had stuck, the chapter would stop. It does not stop, and the reason is not that someone refuted Easton. It is that **three separate research communities went on needing the word**, and then a fourth brought it back deliberately and changed its meaning while doing so.

🔑 The examinable point is that the concept's survival has **four distinct routes**, and that the fourth one is a *redefinition*, not a restoration.

### 2. The four routes, laid out

```text
   EASTON 1953: "ought to be abandoned entirely"
                        |
   +--------+-----------+-----------+-------------+
   |        |                       |             |
 ROUTE 1  ROUTE 2               ROUTE 3       ROUTE 4
 Marxist  'state-building'      the 1980s     the REDEFINITION
 theory   in developing          swing back    state as an ACTIVE
          societies                            AGENT of shaping
   |        |                       |          and reshaping society
 slave-   institutional        Skocpol,            |
 owning   and constitutional   'Bringing the   contrast with
 feudal   mechanism            State Back In'  the EARLIER concept
 capitalist                    in Evans,       of the state as an
 socialist/                    Rueschemeyer    INSTITUTIONAL
 communist                     and Skocpol     STRUCTURE
 'pre-state'                   (eds), 1985
 'stateless'
```

### 3. Route 1 — Marxist vocabulary

✅ The chapter's wording is precise and the list should be learnt entire:

> Marxist political theory continued to use the term 'state' to denote a specific form of political organization: the terms 'slave-owning state', 'feudal state', 'capitalist state', 'socialist/communist state', as well as 'pre-state society' and 'stateless society' are the current coins of Marxist political theory.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ Two things are happening in that sentence, and candidates usually notice only the first.

- ⚠️ **First**, the term survives because it does classificatory work: it names *a specific form of political organization* that varies with the mode of production. ➡️ The doctrine behind that variation belongs to **Topic 04**.
- ⚠️ **Second — and this is the sharper point — the behaviouralists' own damning phrases, 'pre-state society' and 'stateless society', appear here as ordinary working vocabulary.** ⚠️ What was an objection in limb (c) is, in this tradition, simply a category. ✅ The chapter does not say this out loud, but it places the two phrases inside the Marxist list on the page immediately after reporting the objection, and the juxtaposition is doing argumentative work.

🔑 Answer line: the charge that the concept of the state wrongly implies pre-state and stateless societies loses its force once a tradition exists that uses both terms without embarrassment.

### 4. Route 2 — state-building

✅ The chapter continues:

> Even the empirically-oriented political scientists of the liberal tradition used the term 'state-building', especially in the context of developing societies, which signified a renewed interest in the concept of the 'state' as an institutional and constitutional mechanism.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ The irony is complete and worth stating in an answer. The very scholars who wanted the word removed found that the societies they were studying were engaged in building the thing the word named. ✅ The sense in which the word returned here is stated exactly: the state as an **institutional and constitutional mechanism**. ⚠️ That is the *old*, formal sense — the one limb (a) attacked.

### 5. Route 3 and Route 4 — Skocpol, and the change of meaning

✅ The chapter's citation is full and should be reproduced accurately:

> Then, in the 1980s attention swung back to the state, as exemplified by T. Skocpol, 'Bringing the State Back In' (*Bringing the State Back in: Strategies of Analysis in Current Research*, edited by P. Evans, D. Rueschemeyer and T. Skocpol; 1985).
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

| Element | ✅ As the chapter gives it |
|---|---|
| Decade | the **1980s** |
| Essay | **'Bringing the State Back In'** |
| Author | **T. Skocpol** |
| Volume | *Bringing the State Back In: Strategies of Analysis in Current Research* |
| Editors | **P. Evans, D. Rueschemeyer and T. Skocpol** |
| Year | **1985** |

✅ And then the decisive sentence:

> However, in contrast to the earlier concept of the state as an institutional structure, it was redefined as an active agent of shaping and reshaping society.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ Call this **Route 4**, separately from Route 3, because it is a different kind of event. Route 3 is a change of attention; Route 4 is a change of concept.

```text
   OLD CONCEPT                     NEW CONCEPT (1980s)
   state = INSTITUTIONAL           state = ACTIVE AGENT of
           STRUCTURE                       shaping and reshaping
                                           society
   passive container of politics   independent source of
   something politics happens IN   outcomes; something that
                                   ACTS ON society
```

🔑 This single contrast is the highest-yield line on the page, because it reconciles the return of the concept with the force of the original objection. ⚠️ Limb (a) said the state is a formal shell while real politics happens elsewhere. ⚠️ The redefinition concedes that a shell would be useless and asserts something else: that the state is itself an actor with effects.

### 6. The chapter's own verdict, and the hinge sentence

✅ The paragraph closes with the chapter's summary judgment and with the question that opens the next section:

> It is thus evident that, in spite of some initial suspicions and objections, the concept of the state never became entirely redundant for the study of politics. What is, then, meant by the term 'state'?
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ Note the care of the formulation. ❌ The chapter does **not** say the objection was wrong. ✅ It says the concept **never became entirely redundant**. ⚠️ That is a claim about indispensability in practice, not about the soundness of the behavioural critique, and an answer that reports it accurately is more precise than one that claims the critique was refuted.

### 7. Comparison: what each route actually proves

| Route | What it establishes | What it does **not** establish |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Marxist vocabulary | The term does classificatory work across historical forms | ❌ That the liberal objections about formalism were mistaken |
| ✅ State-building | A real process in developing societies needs the name | ❌ That the state is more than an institutional-constitutional mechanism |
| ✅ The 1980s swing | Attention returned across the discipline | ❌ A new argument, by itself |
| ✅ The redefinition | The state can be theorised as a causal agent | ⚠️ That the agent-concept and the structure-concept are the same concept |

⚠️ The last row is the one to press. ⚠️ If the state is redefined as an **active agent**, the thing that came back in 1985 is not quite the thing Easton wanted abandoned in 1953. ✅ Saying that in an answer — *the concept returned, but altered* — is both accurate and distinguishing.

### 8. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** All four routes are sociological facts about scholarship. ⚠️ A concept is not vindicated by being used; phlogiston was used for a century. ⚠️ The chapter therefore mistakes the history of a word for the defence of a concept.

✅ **Reply.** The objection would hold if the chapter stopped at p.148. ✅ It does not: the very next section supplies a definition — Machiavelli's and then Weber's — that is designed to meet objection (b) head-on by defining the state through its **specific means** rather than its **ends**. ⚠️ The usage narrative is the chapter's *motivation* for the definitional work, not a substitute for it.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply rescues the chapter's structure but not every claim in it. ⚠️ Route 4's agent-concept is asserted and never developed; the chapter does not show what the state does *as an agent* that a government plus a constitution does not do. ⚠️ The residual is therefore that the strongest of the four routes is also the least worked out on these pages, and a candidate should say so rather than inflate it.

### 9. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Any stem asking whether the state remains a useful category is answered by these four routes plus the Weberian definition of Lesson 3.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *"The state never became entirely redundant." Discuss.* — the stem is almost the chapter's own sentence, so the answer must know the sentence.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that "Skocpol refuted Easton" fabricates a confrontation the chapter does not report. ✅ The chapter reports a swing of attention and a redefinition.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the structure/agent contrast as your concluding line. It converts a narrative into an analytical point.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Easton's verdict was that the word **'ought to be abandoned entirely'**.
2. ✅ **Marxist** theory kept the term for a **specific form of political organization**.
3. ✅ Its working vocabulary: **slave-owning state, feudal state, capitalist state, socialist/communist state, pre-state society, stateless society**.
4. ⚠️ Two of those six are the behaviouralists' own objection-words used without embarrassment.
5. ✅ **Empirically-oriented liberal** scholars used **'state-building'**, especially for **developing societies**.
6. ✅ That usage revived the state as an **institutional and constitutional mechanism**.
7. ✅ In the **1980s** attention swung back, exemplified by **T. Skocpol, 'Bringing the State Back In'**.
8. ✅ Volume: *Bringing the State Back In: Strategies of Analysis in Current Research*, eds **P. Evans, D. Rueschemeyer and T. Skocpol**, **1985**.
9. ✅ The state was **redefined as an active agent of shaping and reshaping society**, in contrast to the **earlier concept of the state as an institutional structure**.
10. ✅ The chapter's verdict: the concept **never became entirely redundant for the study of politics**.
11. ⚠️ That is a claim about indispensability, ❌ not a refutation of the behavioural objection.
12. 🔑 Memorise the contrast as **structure → agent**; it is the single most quotable line on the page.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the return of the concept of the state in the 1980s cannot simply be described as a restoration of the traditional concept.

**Model answer:** ✅ Because the chapter records a change of content, not only a change of fashion. ✅ The traditional and the state-building senses both treated the state as an **institutional and constitutional mechanism** — an arrangement of organs within which politics occurs. ✅ The 1980s return, exemplified by **Skocpol's 'Bringing the State Back In'** in the **1985** Evans-Rueschemeyer-Skocpol volume, came with an explicit contrast: **in contrast to the earlier concept of the state as an institutional structure, it was redefined as an active agent of shaping and reshaping society**. ⚠️ A structure is acted within; an agent acts. ⚠️ That is why the return partly answers the formalism objection: the renewed concept is not a formal shell but a causally significant actor. ❌ Describing it as a restoration erases the only part of the episode that answers the original criticism.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say that Skocpol or the 1980s literature refuted Easton. ⚠️ The chapter reports no such confrontation. ✅ Its own conclusion is weaker and more careful — that the concept **never became entirely redundant** — and a candidate who overstates it invites the examiner to ask for the refutation that was never cited.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss how the concept of the state survived the behavioural attempt to abandon it. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ It survived along four routes. ✅ **Marxist** theory went on using the term for a **specific form of political organization**, with **slave-owning**, **feudal**, **capitalist** and **socialist/communist** states, and with **pre-state** and **stateless** societies as ordinary categories — which blunts the objection that the concept wrongly implies such societies. ✅ **Empirically-oriented liberal** scholars adopted **'state-building'** for **developing societies**, reviving the state as an **institutional and constitutional mechanism**. ✅ In the **1980s** attention swung back, exemplified by **T. Skocpol's 'Bringing the State Back In'** in the **1985** volume edited by **Evans, Rueschemeyer and Skocpol**. ✅ Crucially, the state was then **redefined as an active agent of shaping and reshaping society**, in contrast to the **earlier concept of the state as an institutional structure**. ⚠️ Survival therefore involved alteration: what returned was a causal actor, not a formal shell. ✅ The chapter's measured verdict is that the concept **never became entirely redundant**.

**Rubric [10]:** all four routes identified as distinct **3** · at least three terms from the Marxist vocabulary list **1** · the state-building sense given as institutional and constitutional mechanism **1** · Skocpol with essay title, volume and 1985 **2** · the structure-to-agent redefinition quoted or closely reported **2** · the chapter's **never became entirely redundant** verdict used as the conclusion **1**.

## Lesson 3 — Machiavelli and Weber: the modern meaning of the state

Progress: 3 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — why the concept survived

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §6, with the Advanced companion's §3.3 and §5, read against the **MEANING OF THE STATE** section (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 = printed 133), which supplies the ancient/modern contrast, Machiavelli's formulation, the nature/end distinction and Weber's two-step sociological definition.
CA search: "Weber monopoly of legitimate use of physical force definition of the state 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on a 1920 definition reported in a 2009 textbook. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem: an ancient thing with a modern name

✅ The chapter opens the section with a distinction that is easy to misread:

> It is significant that though some sort of political organization has existed since ancient times, such as Greek city-states and the Roman empire, yet the concept of the 'state' as such is comparatively modern.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ Read it twice. The claim is **not** that states are modern. ✅ The claim is that the **concept** is modern: political organisation is old, the idea that organises our thinking about it is recent.

```text
   THE THING                         THE CONCEPT
   political organization            the 'state' as such
   Greek city-states                 comparatively MODERN
   Roman empire                      Machiavelli, early 16th c.
   ancient                                 |
                                     and then given its
                                     decisive modern form by
                                     Weber, 1920
```

🔑 This is the sentence that lets you answer the hardest version of the question: *was there a state in ancient Greece?* ⚠️ The chapter's own position permits you to say there was political organisation, that the Greek city-state is one of its historical forms — Lesson 23 — and that the analytical concept under which we now describe it was not available to the people living inside it.

### 2. Machiavelli, and the move that matters

✅ The chapter's formulation:

> The contemporary concept of the state owes its origin to Machiavelli (1469-1527) who expressed this idea in early sixteenth century as 'the power which has authority over men' (*The Prince*; 1513).
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

| Element | ✅ As the chapter gives it |
|---|---|
| Thinker | **Machiavelli** |
| Dates | **1469-1527** |
| Period of the idea | **early sixteenth century** |
| Formulation | **'the power which has authority over men'** |
| Work | ***The Prince*; 1513** |

✅ And then the sentence that explains why this matters:

> This was an important idea because it describes the nature of the state, not the end of the state which was a question of political philosophy rather than political sociology or political science.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133)

⚠️ Stop here, because this is the chapter answering Lesson 1's objection (b) without announcing that it is doing so.

```text
   OBJECTION (b):  the 'state' is conceived in terms of its ENDS
                   -> drags us into moral philosophy
                            |
   MACHIAVELLI'S MOVE:  describe the NATURE of the state
                        (power having authority over men)
                        and leave the END to philosophy
                            |
   RESULT:  a concept fit for political sociology / political science
            rather than for political philosophy
```

🔑 Answer line: Machiavelli's contribution in this chapter is **not** realism, cunning or *raison d'état*. ✅ It is the separation of **nature** from **end**, which is precisely what makes an empirical science of the state possible.

⚠️ Note also the chapter's modest add-on: **this peculiar feature of the state has been the focus of attention of many recent thinkers**. ⚠️ "Peculiar feature" means power-with-authority — and it is the thread that runs to Weber.

### 3. Weber's definition, in two steps

✅ The chapter is careful to present Weber's definition as a two-stage argument, and an answer that gives only the famous sentence loses the first half.

✅ **Step one — the negative, methodological claim:**

> Sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends... Ultimately, one can define the modern state sociologically only in terms of the specific means peculiar to it, as to every political association, namely the use of physical force. (*From Max Weber*, tr. and ed. by H.H. Gerth and C. Wright Mills)
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133), quoting Weber (1920)

✅ **Step two — the resulting definition:**

> A state is a human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.148 (printed 133), quoting Weber (1920)

⚠️ Now dissect the definition, because every clause is load-bearing and each one is separately examinable.

| Clause | What it does | What is lost if it is dropped |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **human community** | Locates the state in a population, not in a building or an office | ⚠️ The state becomes a machine rather than a community |
| ✅ **claims** | Makes the definition about an assertion of entitlement | ⚠️ Confuses the state with whoever happens to be strongest |
| ✅ **(successfully)** | Adds effectiveness — a claim that fails is not a state | ⚠️ Every rebel claimant would qualify |
| ✅ **monopoly** | Exclusivity, not merely superiority | ⚠️ The state becomes one armed body among several |
| ✅ **legitimate** | The force must be rightful, not merely effective | ⚠️ Collapses the state into organised violence |
| ✅ **physical force** | The specific *means*, which is what makes the definition sociological | ⚠️ Reintroduces ends and objection (b) |
| ✅ **within a given territory** | Bounds the claim spatially | ⚠️ A church or a transnational movement could qualify |

🔑 The parenthesis around **(successfully)** is in the chapter's text and is worth reproducing. ⚠️ It marks the clause as a qualification rather than part of the core formula, and a candidate who reproduces it shows close reading.

### 4. Why "means" and not "ends" is the whole point

⚠️ Candidates quote Weber constantly and explain him rarely. The explanation is one sentence long: **every state has been able to pursue almost every conceivable end, so ends cannot individuate the state; what no other association may do is deploy legitimate physical force, so means can.**

```text
   ENDS tried by states:   welfare · conquest · salvation · order ·
                           equality · empire · development
                           => shared with churches, parties, firms,
                              movements => cannot define the state
   MEANS reserved to it:   LEGITIMATE PHYSICAL FORCE, monopolised
                           within a territory
                           => shared with nothing else
                           => defines the state
```

⚠️ This is also why Weber's definition is compatible with the chapter's refusal, later, to treat **international recognition** as an element — Lesson 10. ✅ Recognition is an external relation; the monopoly claim is internal and territorial.

### 5. Comparison: Machiavelli and Weber, four centuries apart

| Axis | ✅ Machiavelli (1513) | ✅ Weber (1920) |
|---|---|---|
| Formulation | **the power which has authority over men** | human community claiming the **monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory** |
| What is being defined | ⚠️ The nature of the state, against its end | ⚠️ The modern state, sociologically |
| Key term | **authority** over men | **legitimate** force, **monopolised** |
| Territory | ⚠️ Not in the formulation | ✅ Explicit and essential |
| Status in the chapter | ✅ Origin of the contemporary concept | ✅ **Widely acknowledged in modern political theory** |

⚠️ The continuity is real and should be stated: both define the state by what it *is and does*, not by what it is *for*. ⚠️ The advance is equally real: Weber adds exclusivity, legitimacy, effectiveness and territory, each of which the chapter will need later.

### 6. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** Weber's definition smuggles the normative back in through the word **legitimate**. ⚠️ To say that force is legitimate is to make a judgment about rightfulness — exactly the moral territory the definition claims to have escaped. ⚠️ If so, the means-based definition is not more sociological than an ends-based one; it has only hidden its evaluation better.

✅ **Reply.** Weber's **legitimate** is sociological rather than moral: it refers to a claim that is *acknowledged* by those subject to it, not to a claim that is *justified*. ✅ The chapter's own second Weber quotation, used later at Lesson 9, supports this reading — **the right to use physical force is ascribed to other institutions or to individuals only to the extent to which the state permits it**, which describes an operative pattern of ascription, not a moral entitlement. ⚠️ On that reading, legitimacy is a fact about belief and practice, and remains empirically identifiable.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is good but incomplete. ⚠️ The chapter never says explicitly which sense of **legitimate** it is using, and later pages lean both ways — the state **commands our loyalty** at Lesson 17 has an evaluative ring that the ascription reading cannot fully absorb. ➡️ The full analysis of legitimacy as a type of belief belongs to **Topic 15**; what this chapter owns is the appearance of the word inside the definition.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The Weberian definition is the opening sentence of almost any answer on the nature or elements of the state, and it is the one line an examiner expects verbatim.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Define the state and examine the adequacy of the definition.* — the two-step structure of Weber's argument is itself the examination.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing "Weber defined the state as the body with a monopoly of force" drops **legitimate**, **claims**, **(successfully)** and **within a given territory**. ✅ Four droppable words, four lost marks.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Give the definition, then dissect one clause rather than all seven. The clause worth dissecting is **legitimate**, because it opens the objection-and-reply above.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Political organisation is ancient; the **concept** of the state is **comparatively modern**.
2. ✅ Ancient instances named: **Greek city-states** and the **Roman empire**.
3. ✅ **Machiavelli (1469-1527)**, *The Prince* (**1513**): the state is **'the power which has authority over men'**.
4. ✅ Its importance: it describes the **nature** of the state, **not the end**, which belongs to **political philosophy** rather than political sociology or political science.
5. ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)**, a **famous German sociologist**, sought a **'sociological'** definition in **1920**.
6. ✅ Weber's premise: **sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends**.
7. ✅ Weber's method: define it **only in terms of the specific means peculiar to it** — **the use of physical force**.
8. ✅ Weber's definition: **a human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory**.
9. ✅ Source given by the chapter: *From Max Weber*, translated and edited by **H.H. Gerth and C. Wright Mills**.
10. ✅ The chapter describes this definition as **widely acknowledged in modern political theory**.
11. ⚠️ Seven load-bearing clauses: human community · claims · (successfully) · monopoly · legitimate · physical force · within a given territory.
12. ⚠️ The means-not-ends strategy is the direct answer to the behavioural objection (b) of Lesson 1.
13. ⚠️ The word **legitimate** is the point at which critics reopen the normative question.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the clause list: **Community Claims Monopoly of Legitimate Force in Territory** — five beats, in the chapter's order.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does Weber insist that the modern state can be defined only by its means, and what would go wrong with a definition by ends?

**Model answer:** ✅ Because the ends a state pursues are shared with other associations and vary across history, while the means are not shared at all. ✅ Weber's premise is stated flatly — **sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends** — and his method follows: define it **only in terms of the specific means peculiar to it, as to every political association, namely the use of physical force**. ⚠️ A definition by ends fails twice. ⚠️ Empirically, it fails to individuate, because welfare, order, salvation, conquest and development have all been pursued by churches, parties, empires and firms as well as by states. ⚠️ Methodologically, it fails because, as the chapter says of Machiavelli's contrary move, the end of the state is **a question of political philosophy rather than political sociology or political science** — so an ends-definition imports evaluation into what is meant to be description. ✅ The means-definition yields the usable formula: a **human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not reduce the definition to "the state has a monopoly of force". ⚠️ Dropping **legitimate** turns the state into any dominant armed body; dropping **claims** and **(successfully)** removes the distinction between an assertion and an achievement; dropping **within a given territory** lets a transnational organisation satisfy the definition.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine Weber's sociological definition of the state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Weber begins negatively: **sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends**, since one can **define the modern state sociologically only in terms of the specific means peculiar to it, as to every political association, namely the use of physical force**. ✅ The resulting definition is that a state is **a human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory**. ⚠️ Each clause does work: *community* locates the state in a people, *(successfully)* adds effectiveness, *monopoly* exclusivity, *legitimate* distinguishes authority from naked force, and *territory* bounds the claim. ⚠️ Its strength is that it individuates the state where ends cannot, since welfare, order and conquest are shared with other associations. ⚠️ Its difficulty is **legitimate**, which reintroduces evaluation unless read sociologically as acknowledged entitlement. ✅ The chapter records it as **widely acknowledged in modern political theory**.

**Rubric [10]:** the negative premise about ends **2** · the specific-means method **1** · the full definition reproduced with at least five of its clauses **3** · a working explanation of two clauses **2** · the difficulty located in **legitimate** **1** · the chapter's own assessment of its standing **1**.

## Lesson 4 — Five more definitions, and the single emphasis each adds

Progress: 4 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §6, with the Advanced companion's §5 and its Laski correction, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.149 (printed 134), which supplies the MacIver, MacIver-Page, Laski, Watkins and Roberts definitions, the boxed definition of **Association**, and the opening of **ELEMENTS OF THE STATE**.
CA search: "Laski pluralism state one association among many sovereignty 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on a 1931 textbook definition. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why one definition is never enough

Weber gave us a formula. ⚠️ A formula tells you what to look for but not what the thing is *contrasted with*. Each of the five definitions on this page contrasts the state with something else, and the contrast is the content.

```text
   THE STATE IS DISTINGUISHED FROM ...
   MacIver (1926)        -> other ASSOCIATIONS      by scope + function
   MacIver & Page (1950) -> other associations      by FINAL POWER OF COERCION
   Laski (1931)          -> VOLUNTARY associations  by compulsory membership
   Watkins (1968)        -> any grouping            by a SINGLE SOVEREIGN
   Roberts (1971)        -> any territory           by SECURED COMPLIANCE
```

🔑 Five definitions, one shared structure: **state versus association**. That is the question the page is really answering.

### 2. MacIver, 1926 — scope and function

✅ **R.M. MacIver**, in *The Modern State* (**1926**), sought to distinguish the state from other kinds of association in that **it embraces the whole of people in a specific territory** and **it has the special function of maintaining social order**. ✅ It performs this function **through its agent, the government 'which speaks with the voice of law'**.

⚠️ Three separate markers are packed into one sentence, and candidates usually retrieve one.

| Marker | What it excludes |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Embraces the whole of people in a specific territory** | ⚠️ Every partial association — a union embraces workers, a church embraces believers |
| ✅ **Special function of maintaining social order** | ⚠️ Associations with other purposes, however large |
| ✅ **Acts through government, which speaks with the voice of law** | ⚠️ Groupings whose will is expressed as opinion rather than law |

🔑 The phrase **'which speaks with the voice of law'** is the seed of Lesson 17's whole distinction: the government is the *voice*, the state is what is spoken for.

### 3. MacIver and Page, 1950 — the final power of coercion

✅ **R.M. MacIver and C.H. Page** (*Society: An Introductory Analysis*; **1950**):

> The state is distinguished from all other associations by its exclusive investment with the final power of coercion.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.149 (printed 134)

⚠️ Two adjectives do all the work. **Exclusive** rules out sharing; **final** rules out appeal to a higher coercive authority. ⚠️ Together they are the associational translation of Weber's **monopoly**.

### 4. Laski, 1931 — compulsion, and the correction that must accompany it

✅ **Harold J. Laski**, in *An Introduction to Politics* (**1931**):

> Whereas all other associations are voluntary in character, and can bind the individual only as he chooses membership of them, once he is a resident of some given state, legally he has no choice but to obey its commands. The state, so to say, is the crowning-point of the modern social edifice, and it is in its supremacy over all other forms of social grouping that its special nature is to be found.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.149 (printed 134)

⚠️ ⚠️ **This passage is the single most dangerous quotation in the chapter, and it must be handled explicitly.** Read in isolation it makes Laski sound like a theorist of absolute sovereignty. ❌ He is not. Laski is political theory's best-known **pluralist** critic of the claim that the state holds a single, morally supreme, all-encompassing sovereignty.

| ⚠️ What the quoted passage actually claims | ❌ What it must not be read as claiming |
|---|---|
| ✅ Membership of the state is **non-voluntary**: residence alone creates legal obligation | ❌ That obedience is morally unconditional |
| ✅ Other associations bind only **as he chooses membership** | ❌ That other associations have no legitimate claims |
| ✅ The state is legally the **crowning-point** and legally **supreme over other forms of social grouping** | ❌ That the state's authority is morally superior by definition |

✅ Both canonical owners insist on this correction, and the Advanced companion states the reason: the passage is Laski's **definitional** point about compulsory membership and territorial jurisdiction, not an endorsement of omnipotent sovereignty. ⚠️ Laski's own considered position is that the state is **one association among many** whose authority must be morally earned through service, and that because society is federal, authority must be federal too. ➡️ The full pluralist case — the attack on the Austinian sovereign on historical, international and federal-organisational grounds — belongs to **Topic 11**, and the chapter itself does not carry it.

🔑 Answer line: Laski's sentence in this chapter establishes a **legal** fact about compulsion; it is not his **normative** account of authority, and writing it as though it were is a substantive error.

### 5. The boxed definition of association

✅ The page carries a definition box:

> **Association** — A human organization formed for the fulfilment of certain common objectives of its members which motivates them to work together.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.149 (printed 134)

⚠️ The box is not decoration. ✅ Every one of the five definitions is a statement of how the state differs from *this*; without the box the differentiations have nothing to differ from. ⚠️ Note two features: an association is formed **for common objectives of its members**, and those objectives **motivate** them. ⚠️ Neither is true of the state on Laski's account — you do not form it and you need not be motivated by its objectives.

### 6. Watkins, 1968 and Roberts, 1971 — sovereignty and compliance

✅ **Frederick M. Watkins** (*International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences*; **1968**) defines the state as **'a geographically delimited segment of human society united by common obedience to a single sovereign'**. ✅ The chapter adds that Watkins **lays special emphasis on the element of sovereignty — the characteristic of the supreme law-making authority whose decisions are final**, and that **the supremacy of the commands of the state is an essential element which distinguishes it from all other associations of men**.

✅ **Geoffrey K. Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; **1971**) supplies a working definition:

> A territorial area in which a population is governed by a set of political authorities, and which successfully claims the compliance of the citizenry for its laws, and is able to secure such compliance by its monopolistic control of legitimate force.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.149 (printed 134)

⚠️ Roberts is Weber operationalised. ✅ Four testable conditions appear: a **territorial area**; a **population governed by a set of political authorities**; a **successful claim** to **compliance**; and the capacity to **secure such compliance by monopolistic control of legitimate force**. ⚠️ The gain over Weber is that the claim is tied to a measurable outcome — compliance — rather than to a bare assertion.

### 7. Comparison, and the transition to the elements

| Definition | Distinctive emphasis | Nearest Weberian clause |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ MacIver 1926 | ⚠️ Comprehensiveness + order-maintenance + law | human community, within a territory |
| ✅ MacIver & Page 1950 | ⚠️ **Exclusive** and **final** coercion | monopoly |
| ✅ Laski 1931 | ⚠️ **Compulsory** membership through residence | (successfully) claims |
| ✅ Watkins 1968 | ⚠️ A **single sovereign**, finality of decision | legitimate |
| ✅ Roberts 1971 | ⚠️ **Secured compliance** by monopolistic control | the whole formula, operationalised |

✅ The chapter then draws the obvious inference: **in the light of the various definitions of the state, it is customary to identify the state by its constituent elements**, which are **population, territory, government and sovereignty**.

⚠️ The word **customary** is a small honesty worth noticing. ⚠️ The chapter does not claim the four-element analysis is deduced from the definitions; it reports that it is the conventional way of unpacking them. ❌ Do not present the four elements as logically derived.

### 8. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** Five definitions stacked on one page is a survey, not an argument. ⚠️ Worse, they conflict: Laski's **supremacy over all other forms of social grouping** sits badly with the pluralism he is famous for, and Watkins's **single sovereign** is precisely what twentieth-century federal and pluralist theory denies.

✅ **Reply.** The definitions are not offered as rivals but as complementary differentiations, each isolating one property that the others leave implicit. ✅ Read that way they are cumulative: comprehensiveness (MacIver), exclusivity and finality of coercion (MacIver and Page), compulsion (Laski), unity and finality of law-making (Watkins), effectiveness (Roberts). ✅ The chapter's own next move — the four elements — treats them exactly so.

⚠️ **Residual.** The cumulative reading cannot absorb everything. ⚠️ Watkins's **single sovereign** is a strong claim that the chapter never defends and that its own later treatment of federal and plural societies strains. ⚠️ And the Laski quotation, left unglossed on the page, actively misleads. ⚠️ The honest statement is that the page is a competent roster with one landmine in it, and that the landmine is defused by reading Laski's own pluralism alongside it.

### 9. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Any "define the state" stem can be answered with Weber plus **two** of these five; using all five wastes words.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How is the state distinguished from other associations?* — this page is the entire answer, and the box on **Association** supplies the contrast term.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting Laski's **crowning-point** sentence as his view of sovereignty. ✅ Add one clause of correction and the same quotation becomes a strength.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Pair MacIver and Page with Laski: exclusivity of coercion plus compulsion of membership covers the distinction in two lines.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **MacIver**, *The Modern State* (**1926**): the state **embraces the whole of people in a specific territory** and has the **special function of maintaining social order**, acting through **government 'which speaks with the voice of law'**.
2. ✅ **MacIver and Page**, *Society: An Introductory Analysis* (**1950**): the state is distinguished **by its exclusive investment with the final power of coercion**.
3. ✅ **Laski**, *An Introduction to Politics* (**1931**): other associations are **voluntary**; residence in a state leaves **no choice but to obey its commands**; the state is **the crowning-point of the modern social edifice**.
4. ⚠️ That passage is a point about **compulsory membership**, ❌ not an endorsement of absolute sovereignty; Laski is a **pluralist**.
5. ✅ Box: an **association** is **a human organization formed for the fulfilment of certain common objectives of its members which motivates them to work together**.
6. ✅ **Watkins**, *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences* (**1968**): **a geographically delimited segment of human society united by common obedience to a single sovereign**.
7. ✅ Watkins emphasises **sovereignty** — **the supreme law-making authority whose decisions are final**.
8. ✅ **Roberts**, *A Dictionary of Political Analysis* (**1971**): a **territorial area** whose population is **governed by a set of political authorities**, which **successfully claims the compliance of the citizenry** and secures it by **monopolistic control of legitimate force**.
9. ⚠️ Roberts is Weber made testable: the claim is tied to actual **compliance**.
10. ✅ The chapter concludes that it is **customary** to identify the state by **population, territory, government and sovereignty**.
11. ⚠️ **Customary**, not deduced — the four-element list is a convention of exposition.
12. 🔑 Mnemonic for the five emphases: **Comprehensive · Coercive · Compulsory · Commanding · Complied-with**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the chapter's own definitions, state three different ways in which the state is distinguished from an association, and explain why one of them must be qualified before it is used.

**Model answer:** ✅ First, **scope and function**: MacIver holds that the state **embraces the whole of people in a specific territory** and has the **special function of maintaining social order**, whereas an association is formed **for certain common objectives of its members**. ✅ Second, **coercion**: MacIver and Page distinguish the state **by its exclusive investment with the final power of coercion** — exclusive excludes sharing, final excludes appeal. ✅ Third, **compulsion of membership**: Laski observes that other associations **can bind the individual only as he chooses membership of them**, while residence in a state leaves him **no choice but to obey its commands**. ⚠️ The third needs qualification. ⚠️ Laski's accompanying description of the state as **the crowning-point of the modern social edifice** reads as absolutism, but it records a legal fact about jurisdiction, not a moral hierarchy; Laski's own position treats the state as one association among many whose authority must be earned. ❌ Quoting the sentence without that qualification misattributes monism to the leading pluralist.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the four elements as deduced from these definitions. ⚠️ The chapter says only that it is **customary** to identify the state by its constituent elements, which is a statement about convention; the definitions constrain the list without entailing it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss how the state is distinguished from other associations. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ An **association** is **a human organization formed for the fulfilment of certain common objectives of its members**; the state differs on four counts. ✅ **Scope**: MacIver holds that it **embraces the whole of people in a specific territory**, with the **special function of maintaining social order**, acting through government **'which speaks with the voice of law'**. ✅ **Coercion**: MacIver and Page distinguish it **by its exclusive investment with the final power of coercion**. ✅ **Compulsion**: Laski notes that other associations bind **only as he chooses membership**, while a resident has **no choice but to obey**. ✅ **Finality of law**: Watkins defines it as a **geographically delimited segment of human society united by common obedience to a single sovereign**, stressing **the supreme law-making authority whose decisions are final**. ⚠️ Laski's additional **crowning-point** language is a legal, not a moral, supremacy — he himself denies the state automatic moral primacy.

**Rubric [10]:** the boxed definition of association used as the contrast term **1** · MacIver's two markers **2** · the exclusive-and-final coercion formula **2** · Laski's compulsion point with the exact voluntary/resident contrast **2** · Watkins or Roberts for finality or secured compliance **2** · the qualification protecting Laski from a monist reading **1**.

## Lesson 5 — Population: the first element

Progress: 5 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the **Population** subsection (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.149-150 = printed 134-135), which supplies the three unity conditions, the self-sufficiency argument and the rejection of homogeneity.
CA search: "homogeneous population requirement modern state diversity 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ targeted search returned contemporary migration commentary with no bearing on the chapter's definitional claim. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The intuition: a crowd is not a people

Any number of human beings can stand in one place without constituting a state. ⚠️ The chapter's treatment of population is therefore not about counting; it is about the conditions under which a number of people becomes the kind of thing a state can be built on.

✅ The opening is flat: **the state is a human institution. The population is, therefore, an essential element of the state.** ⚠️ Then comes the qualification that carries the marks:

> However, the population can constitute a state only when it is united by the condition of interdependence, consciousness of common interest, and general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.149-150 (printed 134-135)

### 2. The three unity conditions

```text
   A NUMBER OF PEOPLE
          |
   (i) INTERDEPENDENCE
       -> material: each needs the others
          |
   (ii) CONSCIOUSNESS OF COMMON INTEREST
       -> cognitive: they know they do
          |
   (iii) GENERAL REGARD FOR A SET OF COMMON RULES
         OF BEHAVIOUR AND INSTITUTIONS
       -> normative: they accept shared standards
          |
     A POPULATION CAPABLE OF CONSTITUTING A STATE
```

| Condition | Kind of condition | What its absence produces |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Interdependence | ⚠️ Material/objective | ⚠️ Coexisting but unconnected groups |
| ✅ Consciousness of common interest | ⚠️ Subjective/cognitive | ⚠️ Objective linkage experienced as domination |
| ✅ General regard for common rules and institutions | ⚠️ Normative | ⚠️ Shared awareness without shared authority |

⚠️ The order is an ascent, and it matters. Interdependence can exist without being noticed; noticing it does not yet produce agreement on rules. ✅ Only the third condition reaches the institutional level — and it is the one that makes the state possible rather than merely desirable.

🔑 Note the word **general** in **general regard**. ✅ The chapter does not require unanimity. ⚠️ That modesty is what allows the later rejection of homogeneity to be consistent.

### 3. Size, and the self-sufficiency argument

✅ **The size of population for constituting a state cannot be fixed**, yet the chapter adds a preference and then an argument:

- ✅ It is **always better that such population is self-sufficient to meet all the needs of life**.
- ✅ If goods or services must be procured from other states, the population **should usually be able to pay for them**.
- ✅ **The possibility of foreign aid, as a temporary measure, cannot be ruled out.**
- ✅ **In any case, economic self-sufficiency is essential for the stability of a state.**

⚠️ Read the four steps as a graded argument, not a slogan.

```text
   self-sufficiency           -> best case
      |  if not
   capacity to PAY            -> acceptable
      |  if not
   FOREIGN AID (temporary)    -> tolerable, as a stopgap
      |  in all cases
   ECONOMIC SELF-SUFFICIENCY  -> essential for STABILITY
                                 (not for EXISTENCE)
```

🔑 The last line is the examinable distinction. ⚠️ The chapter never says economic self-sufficiency is an element of statehood; it says it is **essential for the stability of a state**. ❌ Treating it as a fifth element is an error; ✅ treating it as a condition of durability is the chapter's claim.

⚠️ There is a live tension here with Lesson 28's account of **neo-colonialism**, where the chapter argues that aid and unequal exchange *impoverish* developing nations. ⚠️ If aid is tolerable here and exploitative there, the difference must lie in the word **temporary**, and an answer that spots the connection is making an argument rather than reciting two pages.

### 4. The rejection of homogeneity

✅ The strongest and most quotable passage in the subsection:

> The population of a state need not belong to a single race, religion, language or culture. A homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state. The modern state claims to reconcile the interests of various groups of its citizens.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.150 (printed 135)

⚠️ Three distinct claims are packed here, and they do different work.

| Claim | Type | Where it returns |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Need not belong to a single **race, religion, language or culture** | ⚠️ A permission | Lesson 20, nation on a wider base |
| ✅ Homogeneity **no longer considered an essential feature** | ⚠️ A historical report about theory | Lesson 29, against one-nation-one-state |
| ✅ The modern state **claims to reconcile the interests of various groups** | ⚠️ A functional claim — and note **claims** | 🧩 Lessons 41-45, secularism and multiculturalism |

🔑 The verb **claims** is doing careful work. ⚠️ The chapter does not assert that modern states succeed in reconciling group interests; it reports the claim. ✅ That restraint is exactly the opening the supplement exploits when it asks whether equal citizenship is really delivered.

### 5. Comparison: population here, nationality later

| Axis | ✅ Population (L5) | ✅ Nationality (L19) |
|---|---|---|
| What unites | Interdependence, common interest, common rules | ⚠️ Common race, language, religion, culture, compactness, aspirations, historical development |
| Homogeneity | ❌ Not required | ⚠️ Typically presupposed |
| Basis | ⚠️ Functional and institutional | ⚠️ Largely **based on birth** |
| Effect | ⚠️ Inclusive of several groups | ⚠️ **Separates one set of people from other such sets** |

⚠️ Set side by side, the two passages are the chapter's deepest internal argument: the element of the state is built on conditions that *can* be shared across difference, while nationality is built on conditions that *cannot*. ✅ That is why the chapter can later reject the formula that each nationality should have its own state.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The three unity conditions are so demanding that few actual states satisfy them, and so vague that any state can be said to. ⚠️ Deeply divided societies lack **consciousness of common interest**, yet the chapter would not deny them statehood.

✅ **Reply.** The conditions qualify the population as capable of **constituting** a state, not as a continuing test of statehood; once constituted, the state persists so long as it retains **sovereignty**, as Lesson 9 states. ✅ The chapter's own word **general** — *general* regard for common rules — concedes that compliance is partial. ⚠️ And divided societies are exactly where the chapter says the modern state **claims to reconcile** interests, which presupposes that the interests are not already reconciled.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply leaves a real gap. ⚠️ The chapter offers no threshold: how little **consciousness of common interest** is too little? ⚠️ Lesson 32's examples of states that could not **assimilate themselves into one nation** show that the question is live, and the chapter answers it descriptively rather than conceptually.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The homogeneity sentence is the bridge from the elements of the state to every diversity question on the paper.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is a homogeneous population necessary for a modern state?* — the answer is three sentences from this page, and the marks are in the reconciliation clause.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that the chapter requires economic self-sufficiency for statehood. ✅ It requires it for **stability**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Quote the three unity conditions as a triad; they are short, exact and almost never reproduced correctly.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The state is a human institution**; population is therefore **an essential element**.
2. ✅ Population constitutes a state only when united by **interdependence**, **consciousness of common interest**, and **general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions**.
3. ⚠️ The three conditions ascend from material to cognitive to normative.
4. ✅ **The size of population for constituting a state cannot be fixed.**
5. ✅ Self-sufficiency to **meet all the needs of life** is preferred; otherwise the population **should usually be able to pay**.
6. ✅ **Foreign aid, as a temporary measure, cannot be ruled out.**
7. ✅ **Economic self-sufficiency is essential for the stability of a state** — ❌ not for its existence.
8. ✅ The population **need not belong to a single race, religion, language or culture**.
9. ✅ **A homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state.**
10. ✅ The modern state **claims to reconcile the interests of various groups of its citizens**.
11. ⚠️ The verb **claims** is deliberate; the chapter does not assert success.
12. ⚠️ Population unites across difference; nationality, at Lesson 19, divides on the basis of birth.
13. 🔑 Mnemonic for the triad: **Interdependence · Interest · Institutions**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says population is an essential element of the state but that a homogeneous population is not. Show that the two statements are consistent, and identify the clause that does the reconciling.

**Model answer:** ✅ They are consistent because the chapter's unity conditions are functional rather than ascriptive. ✅ Population qualifies as the element of a state when it is united by **interdependence**, **consciousness of common interest**, and **general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions** — none of which mentions descent, faith, speech or custom. ✅ The chapter therefore adds that the population **need not belong to a single race, religion, language or culture** and that **a homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state**. ⚠️ The reconciling clause is the third unity condition: shared **rules of behaviour and institutions** can be accepted by people who share nothing else, which is precisely what a plural state asks of its citizens. ✅ The chapter completes the thought by saying that the modern state **claims to reconcile the interests of various groups of its citizens**. ⚠️ Note **claims** — the chapter reports an undertaking, not an achievement.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat economic self-sufficiency as a fifth element or as a condition of statehood. ⚠️ The chapter grades it: self-sufficiency is best, the capacity to pay is acceptable, temporary foreign aid **cannot be ruled out**, and self-sufficiency is **essential for the stability of a state** — stability, not existence.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine population as an element of the state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The state being **a human institution**, population is **an essential element**, but not any aggregate will do. ✅ A population constitutes a state only when united by **interdependence**, **consciousness of common interest**, and **general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions**. ✅ **The size of population for constituting a state cannot be fixed**; self-sufficiency in **all the needs of life** is preferable, the capacity to pay for imports is acceptable, and **foreign aid, as a temporary measure, cannot be ruled out**, since **economic self-sufficiency is essential for the stability of a state**. ⚠️ Stability, not existence. ✅ Decisively, the population **need not belong to a single race, religion, language or culture**, and **a homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state**, which instead **claims to reconcile the interests of various groups**. ⚠️ The word **claims** keeps the assessment open.

**Rubric [10]:** all three unity conditions, correctly worded **3** · the unfixable-size point **1** · the graded self-sufficiency argument including the temporary-aid clause **2** · stability distinguished from existence **1** · the rejection of homogeneity with the four named markers **2** · the reconciliation clause with attention to **claims** **1**.

## Lesson 6 — Territory I: Engels, Seeley, and the community that is not a state
