━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The intuition: why would a state need to stand still?

A trade union does not own land. A church may operate in forty countries. ⚠️ So why is territory an *element* of the state rather than a convenience?

✅ The chapter's first answer is comparative:

> Other associations either exist within the state or they extend their sphere to several states; they do not need separate territory. But the state must possess a territory where its authority is accepted without dispute or challenge.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.150 (printed 135)

```text
   OTHER ASSOCIATIONS            THE STATE
   exist WITHIN a state   OR     must POSSESS a territory
   extend ACROSS states          where its authority is ACCEPTED
                                 WITHOUT DISPUTE OR CHALLENGE
   -> territory optional         -> territory constitutive
```

🔑 The decisive phrase is **without dispute or challenge**. ⚠️ It is the spatial form of Weber's **monopoly**: a monopoly must have a boundary, because otherwise there is nothing it is a monopoly *of*.

### 2. Engels, and the shift from kinship to locality

✅ The chapter brings in **Friedrich Engels**, *Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State* (**1884**), who **notes that the formation of the state is accompanied by a division of population according to territory**.

✅ The argument is then set out in three moves:

| Stage | ✅ What organises people | ✅ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-state | ⚠️ **Ties of kinship**, among **nomadic tribes, moving from one place to another in search of food** | ⚠️ Membership follows descent |
| Formation of the state | ⚠️ **Division of population according to territory** | ⚠️ Membership follows location |
| State | ✅ **Citizens are allowed to exercise their rights and duties wherever they settle, irrespective of gens and tribes** | ✅ **The organization of citizens according to locality is the common feature of all states** |

⚠️ This is one of the chapter's most under-used passages, and it is doing more than historical description. ✅ It explains *why* territory is an element and not an accident: because territory is the principle by which the state identifies its members. ⚠️ Kinship answers "who are you descended from"; territory answers "where do you live" — and Laski's definition at Lesson 4 is precisely the legal expression of that second answer.

🔑 Mark the technical word **gens**, kept from Engels: it denotes a descent group. ✅ The phrase **irrespective of gens and tribes** is the chapter's compressed statement of what the territorial principle abolishes.

### 3. Seeley's objection, and why the chapter rejects it

✅ **Some writers, like John Seeley (1834-95), hold that a fixed territory is not an essential aspect of a state.** ✅ The ground is that **the nomadic tribes, who do not possess fixed territory, do constitute a state**. ✅ The chapter's response is blunt: **this view is, however, no longer held valid**.

⚠️ But the interesting part is the *manner* of the rejection, which is a concession rather than a denial:

> The nomadic tribes do have the institution of authority, or even government with custom-based law, but not a state. Political sociologists concede the existence of a 'political system' in such communities, but their organization still does not qualify to be a state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.150 (printed 135)

```text
   NOMADIC TRIBE
     ✅ institution of AUTHORITY        -> yes
     ✅ GOVERNMENT with custom-based law -> yes
     ✅ a POLITICAL SYSTEM               -> yes (political sociologists concede)
     ❌ a STATE                          -> no (fixed territory absent)
```

🔑 This box is the chapter's answer to behavioural objection **(c)** from Lesson 1 — and the answer is a concession, not a refutation. ✅ Yes, political organisation is universal; ❌ no, statehood is not. ⚠️ The chapter keeps both halves, which is why it can continue to use the narrow concept without claiming that politics begins with the state.

⚠️ Note also the precision of the middle line. ✅ The chapter grants nomadic communities **government** — the third element — while denying them the state. ⚠️ That proves the elements are not independently sufficient: you can have one without the whole.

### 4. The international-law reinforcement

✅ The chapter adds a second ground, external rather than internal:

> Moreover, the modern state is not a matter of internal organization; it needs international recognition as well, so as to enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations. International law regards possession of a fixed territory as the essential attribute of the state. Demarcation of physical boundaries is, therefore, essential for establishing the real identity of a state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.150 (printed 135)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Read this carefully, because it appears to contradict Lesson 10.** ⚠️ Here the chapter says the modern state **needs international recognition**; four pages later it says **international recognition is only incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element of the state itself**.

✅ The two statements can be reconciled, and the reconciliation is examinable:

| ✅ p.150 claim | ✅ p.152 claim | ⚠️ Reconciliation |
|---|---|---|
| Recognition is needed **so as to enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations** | Recognition is **incidental to the sovereignty of the state** | ⚠️ p.150 is about **participation** in the international order; p.152 is about **existence** |
| International law requires **a fixed territory** | ⚠️ Recognition may be withheld for decades without the state ceasing to exist | ⚠️ The legal requirement attaches to **territory**, not to **recognition** |

🔑 Answer line: the chapter requires territory for statehood and recognition for international participation. ❌ It never requires recognition for statehood. ⚠️ A candidate who notices the apparent clash and resolves it this way has done something most answers never attempt.

### 5. Comparison: what territory does that population cannot

| Function | ✅ Supplied by population | ✅ Supplied by territory |
|---|---|---|
| Identifying who is bound | ⚠️ Partly — through shared rules | ✅ Decisively — **organization of citizens according to locality** |
| Bounding the monopoly of force | ❌ No | ✅ Yes — **without dispute or challenge** |
| Satisfying international law | ❌ No | ✅ Yes — **the essential attribute of the state** |
| Distinguishing state from other associations | ⚠️ Weakly | ✅ Strongly — others need no **separate territory** |

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter rejects Seeley by appeal to current opinion — **no longer held valid** — and to international law. ⚠️ Neither is an argument from the concept of the state; both are arguments from authority. ⚠️ If nomadic communities have authority, government and custom-based law, the burden is on the chapter to say what *political* work the missing territory was doing.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter does supply that work, through Engels. ✅ Territory is the principle of membership: with it, **citizens are allowed to exercise their rights and duties wherever they settle, irrespective of gens and tribes**. ⚠️ A kinship-organised community cannot extend obligation to a stranger who settles among it without adopting him; a territorially organised one does so automatically. ✅ That is a conceptual difference, not a matter of fashion, and it is what makes Laski's **once he is a resident** sentence possible.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply secures the distinction but not the chapter's confidence. ⚠️ The phrase **no longer held valid** remains an appeal to consensus, and the chapter nowhere engages Seeley's actual argument. ⚠️ It is honest to say that the chapter's reasons are better than its rhetoric here.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The nomadic case is the sharpest available test of whether "state" and "political organisation" are the same thing.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is a fixed territory essential to the state?* — name Seeley, concede the political system, deny the state.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that nomadic tribes have no government. ✅ The chapter grants them **government with custom-based law**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the Engels line about **gens and tribes** as your explanatory step; it converts an assertion into a reason.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Other associations **exist within the state** or **extend their sphere to several states**; they **do not need separate territory**.
2. ✅ The state **must possess a territory where its authority is accepted without dispute or challenge**.
3. ✅ **A state comes into existence only when its population is settled in a fixed territory.**
4. ✅ **Engels**, *Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State* (**1884**): state formation is **accompanied by a division of population according to territory**.
5. ✅ Pre-state nomads are **held together by the ties of kinship**; with the state, citizens exercise rights and duties **wherever they settle, irrespective of gens and tribes**.
6. ✅ **The organization of citizens according to locality is the common feature of all states.**
7. ✅ **John Seeley (1834-95)** held that fixed territory is **not** essential, since nomadic tribes constitute a state.
8. ✅ The chapter replies that this view is **no longer held valid**.
9. ✅ Nomadic tribes **do have the institution of authority, or even government with custom-based law, but not a state**.
10. ✅ Political sociologists concede a **'political system'** in such communities.
11. ✅ The modern state **needs international recognition** to act **as a member of the comity of nations**.
12. ✅ **International law regards possession of a fixed territory as the essential attribute of the state**, so **demarcation of physical boundaries** is essential to its **real identity**.
13. ⚠️ Recognition is needed for participation; it is **not** required for existence — see Lesson 10.
14. 🔑 Memorise the ladder: **authority → government → political system → ✗ state**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate argues that because nomadic tribes have chiefs, rules and obedience, they must have states. Using the chapter, explain exactly where the argument fails and what the chapter concedes to it.

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter concedes almost everything the candidate claims and still denies the conclusion. ✅ Nomadic tribes **do have the institution of authority, or even government with custom-based law**, and **political sociologists concede the existence of a 'political system' in such communities** — so chiefs, rules and obedience are all granted. ✅ What is denied is statehood, because **a state comes into existence only when its population is settled in a fixed territory**. ⚠️ The reason is Engels's: state formation is **accompanied by a division of population according to territory**, which replaces kinship with locality so that **citizens are allowed to exercise their rights and duties wherever they settle, irrespective of gens and tribes**. ⚠️ A kinship order can bind descendants; only a territorial order can bind a stranger who settles. ✅ The chapter therefore rejects **Seeley's** contrary view as **no longer held valid**, and notes that **international law regards possession of a fixed territory as the essential attribute of the state**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read the sentence that the modern state **needs international recognition** as making recognition an element. ⚠️ It concerns the state's capacity to **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**; the chapter later states expressly that recognition is **only incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine whether a fixed territory is essential to the existence of a state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter holds that it is, and argues on three levels. ✅ Comparatively, other associations **exist within the state** or **extend their sphere to several states** and **do not need separate territory**, whereas the state **must possess a territory where its authority is accepted without dispute or challenge**. ✅ Conceptually, **Engels** showed that state formation involves **a division of population according to territory**, replacing kinship so that citizens act **irrespective of gens and tribes**; hence **the organization of citizens according to locality is the common feature of all states**. ✅ Legally, **international law regards possession of a fixed territory as the essential attribute of the state**. ⚠️ **Seeley's** contrary claim that nomadic tribes constitute states is rejected as **no longer held valid**, though the chapter concedes them **authority**, **government with custom-based law** and a **'political system'**. 🔑 The concession matters: political organisation is universal, statehood is not.

**Rubric [10]:** the comparative contrast with other associations **2** · Engels named with the 1884 work and the kinship-to-locality argument **3** · the locality sentence quoted or closely reported **1** · Seeley named and dated with the chapter's verdict **2** · the concession of authority, government and political system **1** · the international-law ground **1**.

## Lesson 7 — Territory II: law, extent, and the four functions of territory

Progress: 7 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the close of the **Territory** subsection (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.150-151 = printed 135-136), which supplies the physical extent of territory, the territorial-waters rule, the four functions of territory and the geographical/political basis of demarcation.
CA search: "territorial waters three miles limit maritime boundary doctrine 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned contemporary maritime-zone material governed by instruments the chapter does not cite; ❌ nothing bears on its 2009 formulation, and no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What counts as territory

✅ The chapter's physical definition is compact and exact:

> The territory of a state includes the land, water and air-space within its boundary. It also extends usually to a distance of three miles into the sea from its coast, and is known as territorial waters, which may be sought to be extended further in times of war.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.150 (printed 135)

```text
                      AIR-SPACE
   ---------------------------------------------
        LAND            |        WATER
   ---------------------------------------------
                        | <-- usually 3 miles -->
                        |   TERRITORIAL WATERS
                        |   (may be sought to be
                        |    extended in times of war)
```

⚠️ Three cautions, each of which protects an answer.

- ⚠️ The chapter writes **usually** before **three miles**. ✅ It is reporting a customary measure, not laying down a rule.
- ⚠️ The extension clause is conditional and passive — **may be sought to be extended** — which is weaker than a claim that states *do* extend it.
- ❌ Do not update this figure with a modern maritime-zone regime inside an answer that cites this chapter. ⚠️ The chapter's edition is **2009** and the sentence is a general illustration; ➡️ exact maritime limits belong to the International Relations and law owners, not here.

### 2. The four functions of territory

✅ The chapter then gives territory four distinct jobs, and the list is the heart of the subsection:

| # | ✅ Function, in the chapter's terms | ⚠️ What kind of claim |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Territory symbolizes the sphere of sovereignty of the state** | Legal-conceptual |
| 2 | **Territory provides for natural resources for the sustenance of the population** | Material |
| 3 | **Territory provides for a sense of security and immense opportunities for a fuller life for its residents** | Social-psychological |
| 4 | **It is an object of sentimental attachment** — people **love and worship their motherland** and are **prepared to make supreme sacrifices** for its **territorial integrity** | Affective |

✅ And then the sentence that links territory to the whole later half of the chapter:

> The feeling of patriotism—the sense of belonging to a state—binds the people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures, by the thread of national unity and mutual cooperation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

🔑 This is the hinge between the elements and the nation. ⚠️ Patriotism is defined here as **the sense of belonging to a state** — a *territorial-political* attachment — and it is said to bind people of **different races, religions, languages and cultures**. ⚠️ Lesson 19 will say that **nationality** does the opposite, separating one set of people from others on exactly those bases. ✅ Patriotism unites across the markers that nationality divides on, and territory is what patriotism attaches to.

```text
   TERRITORY  ->  PATRIOTISM (belonging to a STATE)
                      -> binds DIFFERENT races, religions,
                         languages, cultures
                      -> "thread of national unity and
                          mutual cooperation"
   vs
   BIRTH      ->  NATIONALITY (L19)
                      -> SEPARATES one set of people from
                         other such sets
```

### 3. Size, and the two bases of demarcation

✅ **Like population, the size of the territory of a state cannot be fixed.** ⚠️ The parallel with Lesson 5 is deliberate: neither element has a threshold.

✅ The chapter then distinguishes two ways boundaries come to be drawn:

| Basis | ✅ How the chapter describes it | ⚠️ What follows |
|---|---|---|
| **Geographical** | Territory is **usually a geographical phenomenon, dividing different states by sea, mountains or other big natural barriers** | ⚠️ Boundaries look given, and disputes look like matters of fact |
| **Political** | **Sometimes territories are demarcated mainly on a political basis rather than on a geographical basis**; then **the peoples' sense of identification with a particular state becomes the basis of territorial demarcation** | ⚠️ Boundaries are produced by identification, so disputes are about allegiance |

✅ And the consequence: **reallocation of territories can bring about a merger or alteration of the existing states or emergence of new states**.

🔑 The sentence about identification is the chapter's quiet admission that territory is not prior to politics. ⚠️ If **the peoples' sense of identification** can fix a boundary, then the element is partly constituted by the thing it was supposed to ground — and Lesson 32's examples of states that split are this sentence working itself out.

### 4. Comparison: territory in the three places the chapter uses it

| Where | ✅ What territory does there |
|---|---|
| Weber's definition (L3) | Bounds the **monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force** |
| Engels and Seeley (L6) | Supplies the **principle of membership** — locality replaces kinship |
| This lesson | Supplies **sovereignty's sphere**, **resources**, **security**, **attachment** |

⚠️ The three uses are not restatements. ✅ The first is logical, the second is sociological, the third is a mix of material and affective. ✅ An answer that distinguishes them demonstrates control of a topic most candidates treat as one undifferentiated list.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** Functions 2, 3 and 4 are not reasons for territory being an *element* of the state. ⚠️ A corporation's estate also supplies resources; a religious community's holy land also attracts devotion. ⚠️ Only function 1 — territory as the sphere of sovereignty — is constitutive; the rest are consequences that could be absent without the state ceasing to exist.

✅ **Reply.** The objection is right about the logic and wrong about the purpose. ✅ The chapter's constitutive argument was made at Lesson 6 through Engels and international law; this passage is explaining what territory *does* once it is in place, which is a different question. ⚠️ Function 4 in particular is doing forward work: without **sentimental attachment** to territory, the chapter could not later explain why national movements are organised around land, nor why **territorial integrity** is treated as worth **supreme sacrifices**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply does not rescue the chapter from a looseness of vocabulary. ⚠️ The passage slides from **sense of belonging to a state** to **national unity** within one sentence, treating patriotism and nationalism as continuous when Lessons 19-20 will insist on separating nation from nationality and Lesson 29 will define nationalism as a distinct ideology. ⚠️ The honest reading is that the chapter uses "national" loosely here and precisely later, and a candidate should take the later usage as controlling.

### 6. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The four functions convert a one-word element into a scorable paragraph.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Discuss territory as an element of the state.* — physical extent, then four functions, then the two bases of demarcation.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reciting a modern maritime limit as though it were the chapter's. ✅ The chapter says **usually** **three miles**, with a wartime extension clause.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** End with the identification sentence; it is the most sophisticated line available on territory and leads naturally into the nation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Territory **includes the land, water and air-space within its boundary**.
2. ✅ It extends **usually** **three miles into the sea from its coast** as **territorial waters**, which **may be sought to be extended further in times of war**.
3. ✅ Function 1: territory **symbolizes the sphere of sovereignty of the state**.
4. ✅ Function 2: it **provides for natural resources for the sustenance of the population**.
5. ✅ Function 3: it provides **a sense of security and immense opportunities for a fuller life**.
6. ✅ Function 4: it is **an object of sentimental attachment** — people **love and worship their motherland** and make **supreme sacrifices** for **territorial integrity**.
7. ✅ **Patriotism** is defined as **the sense of belonging to a state**.
8. ✅ Patriotism **binds the people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures, by the thread of national unity and mutual cooperation**.
9. ✅ **The size of the territory of a state cannot be fixed.**
10. ✅ Territory is **usually a geographical phenomenon**, dividing states by **sea, mountains or other big natural barriers**.
11. ✅ Sometimes demarcation is **mainly on a political basis**, and then **the peoples' sense of identification with a particular state becomes the basis of territorial demarcation**.
12. ✅ **Reallocation of territories can bring about a merger or alteration of the existing states or emergence of new states.**
13. ⚠️ Patriotism unites across the markers that nationality divides on — the hinge to Lessons 19-20.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the four functions: **Sovereignty · Sustenance · Security · Sentiment**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says territory is **usually a geographical phenomenon** but that it is **sometimes** demarcated on a political basis. Explain why the second case is theoretically more significant than the first.

**Model answer:** ✅ Because it reverses the direction of explanation. ✅ On the geographical basis, boundaries follow **sea, mountains or other big natural barriers**, so the territory looks prior to the political community that occupies it. ✅ On the political basis, **the peoples' sense of identification with a particular state becomes the basis of territorial demarcation** — the community's allegiance fixes the line. ⚠️ That makes territory partly a product of politics rather than its external container, which is why the chapter can immediately add that **reallocation of territories can bring about a merger or alteration of the existing states or emergence of new states**. ⚠️ It also explains why boundary disputes are rarely settled by geography alone: if identification is the criterion, the dispute is about allegiance. 🔑 The admission is modest in tone and large in consequence, and it prepares the nation/state mismatch taught later.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the four functions of territory as four arguments for territory being an element. ⚠️ Only the first — territory as **the sphere of sovereignty** — is constitutive; resources, security and sentiment describe what territory does once a state exists, and the constitutive case was made separately through Engels and international law.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss the functions that territory performs for the modern state. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Territory **includes the land, water and air-space within its boundary**, extending **usually** **three miles into the sea** as **territorial waters**. ✅ It performs four functions. ✅ It **symbolizes the sphere of sovereignty of the state**, giving the monopoly of legitimate force a boundary. ✅ It **provides for natural resources for the sustenance of the population**. ✅ It supplies **a sense of security and immense opportunities for a fuller life for its residents**. ✅ And it is **an object of sentimental attachment**, for which people are **prepared to make supreme sacrifices**. ⚠️ The fourth generates the politically decisive result: **patriotism—the sense of belonging to a state—binds the people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures**. ⚠️ Yet territory is not simply given: where demarcation is **mainly on a political basis**, **the peoples' sense of identification** fixes the boundary, so that reallocation can merge, alter or create states.

**Rubric [10]:** the physical extent including air-space and the qualified three-mile rule **2** · all four functions in the chapter's terms **3** · patriotism defined as the sense of belonging to a state **1** · the binding-across-difference clause **1** · the geographical/political contrast in demarcation **2** · the merger-alteration-emergence consequence **1**.

## Lesson 8 — Government: the third element

Progress: 8 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the **Government** subsection (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.151-152 = printed 136-137), which supplies Garner's definition, the abstract/concrete contrast, the list of common services, the taxation entitlement, the disjointed-particles image and the colony passage.
CA search: "definition of government agency machinery common policies 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on a 1928 definition reproduced in a 2009 textbook. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Garner's definition, and the four verbs inside it

✅ The chapter returns to **J.W. Garner** (*Political Science and Government*; **1928**) — the same author whose **begins and ends with the state** formula opened the chapter:

> government is the agency or machinery through which common policies are determined and by which common affairs are regulated and common interests promoted.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

| Component | ✅ Wording | ⚠️ What it governs |
|---|---|---|
| What it is | **agency or machinery** | ⚠️ An instrument, not a principal |
| Function 1 | common policies are **determined** | ⚠️ Decision |
| Function 2 | common affairs are **regulated** | ⚠️ Administration |
| Function 3 | common interests are **promoted** | ⚠️ Positive provision |

🔑 Note that the definition contains no mention of persons, parties or offices. ✅ Government is defined functionally, which is why the chapter can say in the next breath that governments change while the state persists.

### 2. The abstract and the concrete

✅ The chapter's compression is memorable:

> If the state represents an abstract concept, government is its concrete form.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

✅ Five consequences follow immediately, and they are worth learning as a block because together they explain why confusion between the two is so natural:

```text
   AUTHORITY of the state      -> EXERCISED by government
   FUNCTIONS of the state      -> PERFORMED by government
   LAWS of the state           -> MADE, DECLARED and ENFORCED
                                  by government
   JUSTICE                     -> DISPENSED by the JUDICIAL ORGAN
                                  of government
   A CITIZEN                   -> has to DEAL WITH government
   TRANSACTIONS between states -> take place through the MEDIUM
     (INCLUDING WAR)              of their governments
```

⚠️ Read the box as the chapter's own explanation of the error it is about to forbid. ✅ If every visible act of the state is performed by the government, then the identification of the two is a natural mistake rather than a careless one — and that is why the chapter spends a whole section, Lesson 17, undoing it.

### 3. The services and the taxing power

✅ The chapter grounds the abstraction in a concrete list:

> Government is responsible for the maintenance of law and order and for the provision of common services—defence, issue of currency, foreign relations, roads, bridges, and even transport, communications, water, electricity, health and education, etc. and it is entitled to levy taxes for the provision of all such services.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

| Group | ✅ Items the chapter names |
|---|---|
| Classical / order functions | **maintenance of law and order**, **defence**, **issue of currency**, **foreign relations** |
| Infrastructure | **roads**, **bridges** |
| ⚠️ Extended, marked by **even** | **transport**, **communications**, **water**, **electricity**, **health**, **education** |
| Enabling power | ✅ **entitled to levy taxes for the provision of all such services** |

🔑 The word **even** before **transport** is the chapter signalling that the second group is an expansion beyond the classical minimum. ⚠️ That signal matters later: Lesson 31's **'service state'** and the Third World's development burden are the same expansion seen from the other end. ➡️ The full welfare-state debate belongs to **Topic 13**.

### 4. The disjointed particles

✅ The chapter's justification of government is a single vivid sentence:

> Without government, the people are a chaotic mass of disjointed particles, without common aims, common interests or a common organization.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

⚠️ Note what the sentence asserts and what it does not. ✅ It asserts that without government there are no **common aims, common interests or a common organization**. ❌ It does not assert that there is no society — which would contradict Lesson 12's claim that **men can live without a state, but not without society**. ⚠️ The image is about *political* integration, and keeping that boundary is the difference between a precise answer and a contradiction.

### 5. Governments change; states persist — and the exception

✅ The chapter now states the rule and its one exception, and the exception is where the marks are:

> However, government and state should not be treated as co-terminous. Governments may rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state, so long as they are formed and dissolved according to the established custom, procedure or constitution of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.151 (printed 136)

✅ The exception:

> But a state will lose its identity if it is suppressed by an alien power so much so that the established procedure of forming a government is also suspended. When the people of a state lose their right to have a government according to the established procedure, i.e. a legitimate government enjoying customary respect and obedience of the people, the state is reduced to a colony of the imperial power which suppressed it.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.151-152 (printed 136-137)

```text
   GOVERNMENT CHANGES
     within established custom/procedure/constitution
            -> STATE UNAFFECTED
   GOVERNMENT-FORMING PROCEDURE ITSELF SUSPENDED
     by an ALIEN POWER
            -> people lose the right to a LEGITIMATE
               government enjoying CUSTOMARY RESPECT
               AND OBEDIENCE
            -> STATE REDUCED TO A COLONY
```

⚠️ Three precisions make this passage examinable rather than ornamental.

- ⚠️ The test is **procedural**, not substantive: what destroys the state's identity is the suspension of the *procedure for forming a government*, not a bad government.
- ⚠️ The agent is **an alien power**. ⚠️ The chapter does not here say that an internal usurpation has the same effect, and an answer should not add that claim.
- ✅ The conclusion is a definition of colonial status in terms of the state's own identity — which is why Lesson 26's colonialism box and Lesson 27's decolonisation narrative follow from this element rather than from a separate historical interest.

🔑 Answer line: for this chapter, a colony is not merely a territory under foreign rule; it is **a state whose procedure for constituting its own government has been suspended**.

### 6. Comparison with the other elements

| Element | ⚠️ Can it be lost without losing statehood? | ✅ Chapter's evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Population | ❌ No — the state is **a human institution** | p.149 |
| Territory | ❌ No — a state exists **only when its population is settled in a fixed territory** | p.150 |
| Government | ⚠️ Individual governments, yes; the **procedure** for forming one, no | pp.151-152 |
| Sovereignty | ❌ No — its loss means **anarchy and disappearance of the state** | p.152 |

⚠️ Government is the only element with this two-level structure, and that is precisely why it is the element most easily confused with the state itself.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter makes government an **element** of the state and then insists the two are not co-terminous. ⚠️ But an element is a constituent part; if government constitutes the state, then when the government is overthrown something constitutive has gone. ⚠️ The chapter cannot have government both inside the definition and outside the identity.

✅ **Reply.** The ambiguity is resolved by distinguishing the *institution* from its *incumbents*. ✅ The element is the standing arrangement — the **established custom, procedure or constitution** by which policies are **determined**, affairs **regulated** and interests **promoted**. ✅ What rises and falls is the set of incumbents occupying that arrangement. ⚠️ The chapter marks the distinction exactly where it matters: the state survives changes of government but not suspension of the procedure of government-formation.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works for the alien-power case and is silent on the domestic one. ⚠️ If an internal dictatorship suspends the constitution indefinitely, the chapter's own test appears to be met, yet the chapter speaks only of **an alien power** and of reduction to **a colony**. ⚠️ That gap is unfilled on these pages, and ❌ nothing is supplied on the chapter's behalf here.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Government is the element that connects this topic to every institutional question on the paper, and the only one with a built-in theory of colonial subjugation.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Distinguish between state and government.* — begin with Garner's definition and the abstract/concrete contrast; the fuller treatment is Lesson 17.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that a change of government changes the state. ✅ The chapter's condition is the suspension of the **established procedure** by **an alien power**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** The services list is a cheap source of concrete detail; quote three or four items rather than all twelve.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Garner** (1928): government is **the agency or machinery through which common policies are determined and by which common affairs are regulated and common interests promoted**.
2. ✅ **If the state represents an abstract concept, government is its concrete form.**
3. ✅ The state's **authority is exercised** and its **functions are performed** by government.
4. ✅ Laws are **made, declared and enforced** by government; **justice is dispensed by the judicial organ**.
5. ✅ Government maintains **law and order** and provides **defence, issue of currency, foreign relations, roads, bridges**, and **even transport, communications, water, electricity, health and education**.
6. ✅ It is **entitled to levy taxes for the provision of all such services**.
7. ✅ **Without government, the people are a chaotic mass of disjointed particles, without common aims, common interests or a common organization.**
8. ✅ A citizen **has to deal with government**; transactions between states, **including war**, take place **through the medium of their governments**.
9. ✅ **Government and state should not be treated as co-terminous.**
10. ✅ Governments **may rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state**, so long as they are formed and dissolved **according to the established custom, procedure or constitution**.
11. ✅ A state **loses its identity** if **suppressed by an alien power** so that the **established procedure of forming a government is also suspended**.
12. ✅ The people then lose their right to **a legitimate government enjoying customary respect and obedience**, and the state is **reduced to a colony of the imperial power**.
13. ⚠️ The test is procedural and the agent is alien; ❌ the domestic case is not addressed here.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for Garner's three verbs: **Determine · Regulate · Promote**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Under what precise condition does the chapter say a state loses its identity, and why is that condition about procedure rather than about the quality of government?

**Model answer:** ✅ The condition is that the state is **suppressed by an alien power so much so that the established procedure of forming a government is also suspended**, so that **the people of a state lose their right to have a government according to the established procedure, i.e. a legitimate government enjoying customary respect and obedience of the people**; the state is then **reduced to a colony of the imperial power which suppressed it**. ⚠️ The test is procedural because the chapter has already separated the element from its incumbents: **governments may rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state, so long as they are formed and dissolved according to the established custom, procedure or constitution**. ⚠️ A bad, weak or unpopular government leaves the procedure intact, and the people retain the capacity to replace it. ✅ Only when the procedure itself is suspended does the political community lose the ability to constitute its own authority — and that, not misgovernment, is what colonial subjugation means on this account.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conclude from **without government, the people are a chaotic mass of disjointed particles** that society collapses without government. ⚠️ The sentence denies **common aims, common interests or a common organization** — political integration — while the chapter elsewhere insists that **men can live without a state, but not without society**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine government as an element of the state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Garner** defines government as **the agency or machinery through which common policies are determined and by which common affairs are regulated and common interests promoted**. ✅ Everything the state visibly does passes through it: authority is **exercised**, functions **performed**, laws **made, declared and enforced**, and justice **dispensed by the judicial organ**. ✅ It supplies **law and order**, **defence**, **issue of currency**, **foreign relations**, infrastructure and **even transport, communications, water, electricity, health and education**, and is **entitled to levy taxes** for them. ⚠️ Yet the two are **not co-terminous**: governments **rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state**, provided they are formed **according to the established custom, procedure or constitution**. ✅ Only when **an alien power** suspends that procedure does the state lose its identity and stand **reduced to a colony**. 🔑 The element is the standing arrangement, not its occupants.

**Rubric [10]:** Garner's definition with all three verbs **2** · the abstract/concrete contrast **1** · at least four named services plus the taxing entitlement **2** · the non-co-terminous rule with the established-procedure proviso **2** · the alien-power exception and the reduction to a colony **2** · a closing line separating the arrangement from its incumbents **1**.

---

## Lesson 9 — Sovereignty: the fourth element

Progress: 9 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the **Sovereignty** subsection (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 = printed 137), which supplies the internal/external account, the derivation of governmental authority from the state, Weber's second quotation and the anarchy consequence.
CA search: "sovereignty supreme power of the state legitimate force doctrine 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's definitional treatment. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What the chapter means by sovereignty, in four limbs

✅ The definition is built up clause by clause:

> Sovereignty denotes the supreme or ultimate power of the state to make laws or take political decisions—establishing public goals, fixing priorities and resolving conflicts—as also enforcing such laws and decisions by the use of legitimate force. In fact, sovereignty denotes the final authority of the state over its population and its territory.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

```text
   SOVEREIGNTY
     = supreme / ultimate power of the state
         |
     (i) TO MAKE LAWS or TAKE POLITICAL DECISIONS
          - establishing PUBLIC GOALS
          - fixing PRIORITIES
          - resolving CONFLICTS
         |
     (ii) TO ENFORCE them BY THE USE OF LEGITIMATE FORCE
         |
     => FINAL AUTHORITY of the state over its
        POPULATION and its TERRITORY
```

⚠️ Two features repay attention. ✅ The decision-limb is expanded into three named activities — **establishing public goals, fixing priorities and resolving conflicts** — which most candidates never reproduce. ⚠️ And the closing formula ties sovereignty to the two earlier elements: authority over **population** and **territory**, which is why sovereignty is placed fourth rather than first.

### 2. The derivation clause

✅ The chapter's most important single sentence in this subsection is easy to read past:

> This authority may be exercised by the government of the day, but it essentially belongs to the state from which it is derived by the government.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

⚠️ This is the chapter's answer to the question Lesson 8 raised: if government does everything visible, where does its right to do so come from?

```text
   STATE  --- owns sovereignty ---> GOVERNMENT
                                      exercises it
                                      derives it
   => a government's authority is DERIVATIVE
   => the state's authority is ORIGINAL
```

🔑 Answer line: sovereignty belongs to the state **essentially**; the government holds it **derivatively**. ✅ That single word *derived* is what makes it intelligible to obey a law while condemning the ministry that passed it — the theme of Lesson 17.

### 3. What sovereignty permits the state to do

✅ Four powers follow, and they are usefully split into internal and external:

| Sphere | ✅ What the state does by virtue of sovereignty |
|---|---|
| Internal | **declares—through the agency of the government—its laws and decisions** |
| Internal | **issues commands which are binding on all citizens**, **claims obedience thereto**, and **punishes the offenders** |
| External | **deals independently with other states** |
| Comparative | Its commands are **treated as superior to those of any other association or institution** |

✅ The comparative limb is stated in a form worth quoting, because it is startling:

> Commands of the state are treated as superior to those of any other association or institution, even to the dictates of social customs or conscience of individual, because sovereignty is the sole preserve of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

⚠️ Handle this sentence with care. ✅ It says commands **are treated as** superior — a report of legal practice. ❌ It does not say they *ought* to prevail over conscience. ⚠️ If it did, the chapter would be contradicting its own later insistence that **any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**. ➡️ The question of when conscience may rightly refuse obedience belongs to **Topic 14**.

### 4. Weber's second appearance

✅ The chapter returns to Weber (**1920**) to close the point:

> The right to use physical force is ascribed to other institutions or to individuals only to the extent to which the state permits it. The state is considered the sole source of the 'right' to use violence. (*From Max Weber*, tr. and ed. by H.H. Gerth and C. Wright Mills)
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

⚠️ Note the architecture. ✅ Weber appeared at Lesson 3 to define the state; he reappears here to explain the monopoly clause of that definition. ✅ The monopoly does not mean that only officials ever use force; it means that **any other permitted use is a permission granted by the state**. ⚠️ A private security guard, a parent, a sportsman and a citizen acting in self-defence all use force lawfully — and each does so **only to the extent to which the state permits it**.

🔑 This is the cleanest available gloss on "monopoly of legitimate force", and it defuses the standard undergraduate objection that states plainly do not monopolise violence.

✅ The chapter completes the contrast: **other associations are either voluntary or based on custom or necessity**, and **the right to use legitimate coercion in its own right is the exclusive prerogative of the state**. ⚠️ The phrase **in its own right** is the operative one — others may coerce by permission, the state alone coerces by right.

### 5. Loss of sovereignty

✅ The consequence clause is severe and precise:

> A state continues to exist so long as it is armed with sovereignty. If a state loses its sovereignty because of internal revolt or external aggression, the result is anarchy and disappearance of the state as such.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

| ✅ Cause | ✅ Result |
|---|---|
| **Internal revolt** | **Anarchy and disappearance of the state as such** |
| **External aggression** | The same |

⚠️ Compare this with Lesson 8's colony passage and a real difference appears, which is examinable:

```text
   LOSS OF THE GOVERNMENT-FORMING PROCEDURE
     cause: AN ALIEN POWER
     result: the state is REDUCED TO A COLONY
             (it persists, in subordinated form)

   LOSS OF SOVEREIGNTY
     cause: INTERNAL REVOLT or EXTERNAL AGGRESSION
     result: ANARCHY and DISAPPEARANCE of the state AS SUCH
```

🔑 Two different failures with two different outcomes. ⚠️ Subjugation degrades the state; loss of sovereignty ends it. ✅ An answer that distinguishes them is using the chapter rather than summarising it.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** If sovereignty means **final authority** and a monopoly of the right to coerce, then federal states, states bound by treaty and states inside international organisations are not fully sovereign — and Lesson 31 will say expressly that each nation-state **should accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty**. ⚠️ The element therefore appears to be absent from most contemporary states.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's sovereignty is a claim about the **source** of binding authority within a territory, not about the absence of self-imposed limits. ✅ A treaty obligation is assumed by the state and operates because the state's own legal order gives it effect; it is the derivation clause applied externally. ⚠️ Lesson 31's **reasonable restraints** are described as something a state **accepts**, which presupposes rather than denies the capacity to bind itself.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is sound as far as it goes and does not reach Lesson 34, where the chapter says the nation-state must **concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty**. ⚠️ *Adjustment* is vaguer than *acceptance*, and the chapter never specifies how much adjustment is compatible with **final authority**. ➡️ The sustained treatment of that problem is **Topic 12**; here it is an acknowledged open edge.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Sovereignty is the element that links this topic to the whole of Topics 11 and 12, and the derivation clause is the bridge to the state/government distinction.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Why is sovereignty regarded as the most important element of the state?* — the answer is the existence clause plus the exclusive-prerogative clause.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that sovereignty means the state alone uses force. ✅ It means the state alone has the right **in its own right**, and all other uses are permitted.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Pair the derivation clause with Weber's permission sentence; together they explain both where the government's authority comes from and what the monopoly actually asserts.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Sovereignty is the **supreme or ultimate power of the state to make laws or take political decisions**.
2. ✅ The decision-limb covers **establishing public goals, fixing priorities and resolving conflicts**.
3. ✅ It includes **enforcing such laws and decisions by the use of legitimate force**.
4. ✅ Sovereignty denotes the **final authority of the state over its population and its territory**.
5. ✅ The authority **may be exercised by the government of the day**, but **essentially belongs to the state from which it is derived by the government**.
6. ✅ By sovereignty the state **declares** laws, **issues commands binding on all citizens**, **claims obedience** and **punishes the offenders**.
7. ✅ By sovereignty it also **deals independently with other states**.
8. ✅ Its commands are **treated as superior to those of any other association or institution, even to the dictates of social customs or conscience of individual**.
9. ✅ **Weber (1920)**: the right to use physical force is ascribed to others **only to the extent to which the state permits it**; the state is **the sole source of the 'right' to use violence**.
10. ✅ Other associations are **voluntary or based on custom or necessity**; legitimate coercion **in its own right** is the state's **exclusive prerogative**.
11. ✅ **A state continues to exist so long as it is armed with sovereignty.**
12. ✅ Loss of sovereignty through **internal revolt or external aggression** produces **anarchy and disappearance of the state as such**.
13. ⚠️ Colonial subjugation degrades the state; loss of sovereignty ends it.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the four powers: **Declare · Command · Punish · Deal**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how Weber's statement about permitted force clarifies what the "monopoly" in his definition of the state actually claims.

**Model answer:** ✅ It converts an implausible empirical claim into a precise juridical one. ⚠️ Taken literally, "monopoly of force" is false: parents, guards, sportsmen and citizens acting in self-defence all use force lawfully. ✅ Weber's clarification is that **the right to use physical force is ascribed to other institutions or to individuals only to the extent to which the state permits it**, and that **the state is considered the sole source of the 'right' to use violence**. ⚠️ So the monopoly is not over the *exercise* of force but over its *authorisation*. ✅ The chapter's own gloss completes it: other associations are **voluntary or based on custom or necessity**, whereas **the right to use legitimate coercion in its own right is the exclusive prerogative of the state**. 🔑 The phrase **in its own right** marks the difference between original and delegated entitlement, which is the same structure as the derivation of governmental authority from the state.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read **commands of the state are treated as superior ... even to the dictates of ... conscience of individual** as a moral endorsement. ⚠️ The chapter is reporting how legal systems treat such commands; it separately insists that a theory without **a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**, which would be incoherent if conscience were simply overridden.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss sovereignty as an element of the state. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Sovereignty is the **supreme or ultimate power of the state to make laws or take political decisions... as also enforcing such laws... by the use of legitimate force**, denoting the **final authority of the state over its population and its territory**. ✅ That authority **may be exercised by the government of the day, but it essentially belongs to the state**. ✅ It lets the state issue **commands... binding on all citizens**, **punish the offenders** and **deal independently with other states**. ✅ **Weber**: force is permitted to others **only to the extent to which the state permits it**, the state being **the sole source of the 'right' to use violence**. ✅ Hence a state **continues to exist so long as it is armed with sovereignty**; its loss through **internal revolt or external aggression** brings **anarchy and disappearance of the state as such**.

**Rubric [10]:** the full definition including the three named decision-activities **2** · the final-authority formula over population and territory **1** · the derivation clause **2** · at least three of the four sovereign powers **2** · Weber's permitted-force sentence **2** · the existence-and-loss clause with both causes **1**.

## Lesson 10 — International recognition: outcome, not condition

Progress: 10 / 45 | Stage: Foundation | Part II — definitions and elements

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 and §9, with the Advanced companion's §4 and §12, read against the close of the **Sovereignty** subsection (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 = printed 137), which supplies the recognition argument, the Bangladesh illustration, the USSR and People's Republic of China illustration and the incidental-not-fundamental verdict.
CA search: "international recognition statehood constitutive declaratory theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current recognition disputes governed by instruments and facts the chapter does not address; ❌ none bears on its 2009 formulation, and no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The question, posed sharply

If a political community has population, territory, government and sovereignty, but no other state will acknowledge it, is it a state?

⚠️ The question is not academic. It is the standard distractor in every objective test on this topic, and it is the standard omission in written answers that list four elements and move on.

✅ The chapter answers it in one paragraph, and the answer is a *conditional* one:

> Some writers regard 'international recognition' as an essential element of the state. This denotes formal recognition of the sovereignty of the state over a given territory and population by other states. International recognition, however, is the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

```text
   SOME WRITERS:   recognition = a FIFTH ELEMENT
                            |
   THE CHAPTER:    recognition = formal recognition OF
                   THE SOVEREIGNTY of the state over a
                   given territory and population,
                   BY OTHER STATES
                            |
                   => it is the OUTCOME of sovereignty
                   => NOT a condition of EXISTENCE
```

🔑 The decisive word is **outcome**. ⚠️ Recognition presupposes sovereignty, because what is recognised *is* the sovereignty. ✅ A condition cannot presuppose what it conditions, so recognition cannot be a condition of the state's existence.

### 2. The two illustrations, and why there are two

✅ **Illustration one — delay at the beginning:**

> When a new state, like Bangladesh, comes into existence, it may be recognized by some states immediately while other states may withhold their recognition for quite a long time. Much depends on the foreign policy of a state whether to recognize the new state immediately or to delay it.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

✅ **Illustration two — prolonged withholding by a major power:**

> USA had withheld recognition of the new states of USSR and People's Republic of China for decades after they came into existence, but they did exist as states.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

| Illustration | ⚠️ What it establishes |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Bangladesh** | Recognition is **staggered**, and its timing turns on **the foreign policy of a state** — so it tracks the recogniser's interests, not the recognised state's attributes |
| ✅ **USSR** and the **People's Republic of China**, unrecognised by the **USA** for **decades** | Non-recognition can persist for decades while the entity **did exist as states** — so recognition is not necessary for existence |

⚠️ The two illustrations are doing different jobs and an answer should use both. ✅ The first shows that recognition is *political*; the second shows that it is *dispensable*. ⚠️ Only the second refutes the fifth-element thesis; the first explains why anyone was tempted by it.

### 3. The verdict

✅ The chapter concludes:

> Hence, international recognition is only incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element of the state itself.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.152 (printed 137)

⚠️ Note the two negations and keep them separate. ❌ Recognition is not a **fundamental element**. ⚠️ It *is* **incidental to the sovereignty** — which is not nothing: an incident of something is a normal accompaniment of it.

### 4. The apparent contradiction with Lesson 6, resolved

⚠️ Two pages earlier the chapter wrote that **the modern state is not a matter of internal organization; it needs international recognition as well, so as to enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**.

✅ Both sentences stand, because they answer different questions:

| Question | ✅ Chapter's answer | ✅ Page |
|---|---|---|
| Does a state need recognition to **exist**? | ❌ No — recognition is **incidental**, and the USSR and PRC **did exist as states** | p.152 |
| Does a state need recognition to **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**? | ✅ Yes | p.150 |

🔑 Formula worth memorising: **recognition is necessary for international participation, not for statehood**. ⚠️ A candidate who quotes only one of the two sentences will appear to have misread whichever one the examiner has in mind; a candidate who reconciles them has shown command of the page.

### 5. Comparison: what the four elements share that recognition does not

| Property | ✅ Population, territory, government, sovereignty | ❌ International recognition |
|---|---|---|
| Located inside the state | ✅ Yes | ❌ No — it is **by other states** |
| Its absence destroys or degrades the state | ✅ Yes | ❌ No — the USSR and PRC persisted |
| Independent of others' choices | ✅ Yes | ❌ No — **much depends on the foreign policy of a state** |
| Presupposed by recognition | ✅ Sovereignty is | ⚠️ — |

⚠️ The third row is the strongest argument available and the chapter leaves it implicit: an element whose presence depends on *other* states' policy preferences would make statehood a gift in the hands of foreigners. ✅ The chapter's own sentence about **foreign policy** supplies the premise; the inference is a safe one and should be marked as inference.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter settles the question by assertion. ⚠️ Recognition may be an outcome of sovereignty in logic and still be a condition of sovereignty in practice: a community that no one recognises cannot make treaties, join organisations, defend its boundaries in law or exercise many of the powers Lesson 9 lists. ⚠️ If sovereignty is **final authority** over population and territory, an unrecognised entity has that authority only precariously.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter concedes the practical half of this and keeps the conceptual half, which is why the p.150 sentence exists. ✅ It accepts that recognition is needed **so as to enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations** — the practical disability is granted. ⚠️ What it denies is that the disability destroys statehood, and its evidence is historical rather than definitional: two of the largest states of the twentieth century went unrecognised by the most powerful state for **decades** and **did exist as states**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The historical evidence is strong where the unrecognised entity is large and secure and weak where it is neither. ⚠️ The chapter does not consider an entity that is unrecognised *and* unable to maintain its monopoly of force, which is where the practical and conceptual questions converge. ❌ Nothing is supplied here on the chapter's behalf.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the single most reliable discriminator between a four-element answer and a five-element error.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is international recognition an element of the state?* — the full answer is four sentences long and needs both illustrations.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting only the p.150 sentence and concluding that recognition is required. ✅ Reconcile the two.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use **outcome, not condition** as your thesis sentence; it is the chapter's own formulation and is immediately gradable.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Some writers regard 'international recognition' as an essential element of the state.**
2. ✅ Recognition means **formal recognition of the sovereignty of the state over a given territory and population by other states**.
3. ✅ It is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence**.
4. ✅ **Bangladesh**: a new state may be **recognized by some states immediately** while others **withhold their recognition for quite a long time**.
5. ✅ **Much depends on the foreign policy of a state** whether to recognise immediately or delay.
6. ✅ The **USA** withheld recognition of the **USSR** and the **People's Republic of China** for **decades**, **but they did exist as states**.
7. ✅ Verdict: recognition is **only incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element of the state itself**.
8. ⚠️ Earlier, at p.150, the chapter says the modern state **needs international recognition** to **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**.
9. ⚠️ The two statements are reconciled by distinguishing **participation** from **existence**.
10. ⚠️ Recognition presupposes sovereignty; a condition cannot presuppose what it conditions.
11. ⚠️ Recognition depends on others' foreign policy, which no genuine element of the state does.
12. 🔑 Thesis line: **outcome, not condition** — and it is the chapter's own phrase.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconcile the chapter's claim that the modern state **needs international recognition** with its later verdict that recognition is **only incidental** to statehood.

**Model answer:** ✅ The two sentences answer different questions. ✅ The earlier sentence concerns capacity in the international order: the modern state **needs international recognition as well, so as to enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations** — that is, to make treaties, hold membership and assert claims. ✅ The later verdict concerns existence: recognition is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence**, and is therefore **only incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element of the state itself**. ✅ The evidence is historical: the **USA** withheld recognition of the **USSR** and the **People's Republic of China** for **decades**, **but they did exist as states**. ⚠️ So recognition is necessary for international **participation** and unnecessary for **statehood**. 🔑 The conceptual reason stands behind the historical one: what is recognised is the sovereignty, so recognition presupposes what the fifth-element thesis says it creates.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not infer from the Bangladesh illustration that recognition is unimportant. ⚠️ The chapter's point there is narrower — that its timing follows **the foreign policy of a state** rather than the attributes of the state being recognised — which is what makes it unsuitable as an element, not what makes it trivial.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine whether international recognition should be treated as an element of the state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ It should not. ✅ **Some writers regard 'international recognition' as an essential element**, but it is **formal recognition of the sovereignty of the state over a given territory and population by other states** — it presupposes the sovereignty it is supposed to create, and is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence**. ✅ Evidence: when a new state such as **Bangladesh** appears, some recognise it **immediately** while others **withhold their recognition for quite a long time**, since **much depends on the foreign policy of a state**; the **USA** withheld recognition of the **USSR** and **People's Republic of China** for **decades**, **but they did exist as states**. ⚠️ Recognition remains necessary to **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**. ✅ Hence it is **incidental to the sovereignty of the state, not a fundamental element**.

**Rubric [10]:** the fifth-element thesis stated as the position being tested **1** · recognition correctly defined as recognition of sovereignty by other states **2** · the outcome-not-condition argument **2** · the Bangladesh illustration with the foreign-policy clause **2** · the USSR and PRC illustration with **decades** and **did exist as states** **2** · the participation/existence reconciliation **1**.

## Lesson 11 — State and society I: the two reasons the distinction matters

Progress: 11 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part III — the state's neighbours

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §5 and §9, with the Advanced companion's §4 and §7, read against the opening of **STATE AND SOCIETY** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.153 = printed 138), which supplies the confusion diagnosis, the twofold justification, the society/state functional contrast and the two modes of relationship.
CA search: "distinction between state and society totalitarianism political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's conceptual argument. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A distinction with a political purpose

✅ The chapter opens by naming the error and then — unusually — by naming why the error is dangerous:

> The term 'state' is sometimes used synonymously with 'society'. However, such usage arises from confusion—intentional or unintentional. A distinction between state and society is desirable, not only for scientific precision but also for saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.153 (printed 138)

```text
   WHY DISTINGUISH STATE FROM SOCIETY?
        |
   (1) SCIENTIFIC PRECISION
        -> two different objects, two different logics
        |
   (2) SAVING THE INDIVIDUAL FROM
       ABSOLUTIST, AUTHORITARIAN AND
       TOTALITARIAN RULE
        -> if state = society, every social
           obligation becomes a political command
```

⚠️ Note the phrase **intentional or unintentional**. ✅ The chapter is saying that the conflation is sometimes a mistake and sometimes a tactic — which is why reason (2) exists. 🔑 This is the only place in the chapter where a conceptual confusion is described as potentially deliberate, and it is worth quoting for that reason alone.

### 2. The functional contrast

✅ The chapter defines each side by what it does:

| | ✅ Society | ✅ State |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | **An association of human beings which fulfils all their needs of life—from cradle to grave** | **Society politically organized** |
| Need served | ⚠️ All of them | **Their particular need of political organization** |
| How | ⚠️ Through the whole range of human relationship | **Subjects them to binding laws and decisions to provide for order and security, and common services** |
| Threshold | ⚠️ — | ✅ A society qualifies as a state only **when it is governed by a common set of rules, regulations and a supreme decision-making authority** |

🔑 The phrase **from cradle to grave** is the chapter's own and is the shortest way to state the scope difference. ⚠️ It appears again, transposed, in the welfare literature; ➡️ that usage belongs to **Topic 13**.

### 3. The two modes of relationship

✅ This is the subsection's analytical core and the part most often reduced to a slogan:

> Society binds men into multifarious relationships—all such relationships do not fall in the domain of a state. Social relationships are usually determined by necessity, custom, courtesy, morality, mutual understanding, agreement or even contract; political relations are mainly determined by command and obedience.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.153 (printed 138)

```text
   SOCIAL RELATIONS                 POLITICAL RELATIONS
   determined by:                   determined by:
     necessity                        COMMAND
     custom                           and
     courtesy                         OBEDIENCE
     morality
     mutual understanding
     agreement
     even contract
   -> SEVEN modes                   -> ONE mode
```

⚠️ Count them: the chapter names **seven** determinants of social relationship and **one** pair for political relationship. ✅ That asymmetry is the argument. ⚠️ Social life is plural in its modes of coordination; political life is monotone. ⚠️ Which is exactly why a society that is wholly politicised loses six of its seven ways of holding people together — the content of reason (2) above.

✅ The chapter adds the corresponding breadth claim: **social relations cover a large variety of subjects, to meet all the needs of human life—physical, emotional, intellectual, spiritual, and so on.**

### 4. Comparison: what each kind of relation can and cannot do

| Task | ⚠️ Can command-and-obedience do it? | ⚠️ Can the social modes do it? |
|---|---|---|
| Secure order and security | ✅ Yes, directly | ⚠️ Partly, and unreliably |
| Provide common services | ✅ Yes | ⚠️ Partly |
| Generate friendship, courtesy, mutual understanding | ❌ No | ✅ Yes |
| Sustain moral obligation | ❌ Not by command | ✅ Yes |
| Bind strangers inside a territory | ✅ Yes | ⚠️ Only weakly |

⚠️ Read the table as the chapter's implicit answer to anyone who thinks the state could simply absorb society: the two bottom rows are things command cannot produce, and a political order that tries to produce them by command destroys what it was trying to secure.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The sharp contrast is overdrawn. ⚠️ Much of what the state does is not command at all — it persuades, incentivises, subsidises, informs and negotiates — while much of social life is coercive without being political, as family and caste authority show. ⚠️ If so, **command and obedience** is not a reliable marker.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's claim is comparative, not exclusive: political relations are **mainly** determined by command and obedience. ✅ And the asymmetry survives the objection, because the state's persuasion operates against a background of enforceable law — it is the state that **issues commands which are binding on all citizens**, **claims obedience thereto**, and **punishes the offenders**, as Lesson 9 established. ⚠️ Social coercion, by contrast, works through the modes listed — custom, morality, necessity — and cannot call on legitimate force **in its own right**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The second half of the objection is not fully answered. ⚠️ The chapter has no account of coercive social power, and in particular no account of domination exercised through custom. ➡️ That gap is filled, from a different direction, by Gramsci at Lesson 15 and by the feminist critique in the supplement at Lesson 45 — both of which describe unfreedom produced inside "society" rather than by the state.

### 6. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The twofold justification — precision *and* protection — is the sentence that turns a definitional distinction into a political argument.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Distinguish between state and society.* — scope, function, modes of relationship, then the liberty reason.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that society is "wider" and stopping. ✅ Wider in **scope**; different in **mode**; and prior in **origin**, as Lesson 12 shows.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Quote the seven social determinants against the single political pair; it is the most economical demonstration of the difference.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Using **state** synonymously with **society** arises from confusion, **intentional or unintentional**.
2. ✅ The distinction is desirable **not only for scientific precision but also for saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule**.
3. ✅ The state is **society politically organized**.
4. ✅ Society is **an association of human beings which fulfils all their needs of life—from cradle to grave**.
5. ✅ The state fulfils **their particular need of political organization**.
6. ✅ It does so by subjecting them to **binding laws and decisions to provide for order and security, and common services**.
7. ✅ A society qualifies as a state only when governed by **a common set of rules, regulations and a supreme decision-making authority**.
8. ✅ Society binds men into **multifarious relationships**, and **all such relationships do not fall in the domain of a state**.
9. ✅ Social relations are determined by **necessity, custom, courtesy, morality, mutual understanding, agreement or even contract** — seven modes.
10. ✅ Political relations are **mainly determined by command and obedience** — one mode.
11. ✅ Social relations meet needs that are **physical, emotional, intellectual, spiritual, and so on**.
12. ⚠️ The seven-to-one asymmetry is the argument, not an ornament.
13. 🔑 Mnemonic for the political mode: **one pair — command and obedience**; for the social, **seven, beginning with necessity**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says the state/society distinction is needed for two reasons. Explain why the second reason is political rather than merely scientific, and show how the rest of the subsection supports it.

**Model answer:** ✅ The second reason is **saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule** — a claim about liberty, not about taxonomy. ✅ It is supported by the mode-contrast. ✅ Society coordinates through **necessity, custom, courtesy, morality, mutual understanding, agreement or even contract**, and meets needs that are **physical, emotional, intellectual, spiritual**, whereas political relations are **mainly determined by command and obedience**. ⚠️ If state and society are identified, every one of those plural social modes is replaced by the single political one, and every obligation a person owes to anyone becomes enforceable by authority. ✅ That is precisely the totalitarian outcome the chapter later names, when identification leads to **complete subordination of man to the authority of government, unrestrained by any control-mechanism**. 🔑 Hence the distinction is a safeguard: it keeps most of social life outside the reach of command.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the contrast as exclusive. ⚠️ The chapter writes that political relations are **mainly** determined by command and obedience, and that **all such relationships do not fall in the domain of a state** — both are comparative claims about predominance, not denials that states persuade or that societies coerce.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine why political theory insists on distinguishing the state from society. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Two reasons are given, and only the second is political. ✅ The first is **scientific precision**: society is **an association of human beings which fulfils all their needs of life—from cradle to grave**, while the state is **society politically organized**, serving **their particular need of political organization** through **binding laws and decisions**. ✅ The second is **saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule**. ✅ It rests on a contrast of modes: social relations are determined by **necessity, custom, courtesy, morality, mutual understanding, agreement or even contract**, whereas political relations are **mainly determined by command and obedience**. ⚠️ Identifying the two therefore replaces seven modes of coordination with one, converting every social obligation into an enforceable command. ✅ The chapter adds that **all such relationships do not fall in the domain of a state**, and that the conflation may be **intentional or unintentional**. 🔑 Precision protects liberty.

**Rubric [10]:** both reasons named, with the second quoted **3** · the cradle-to-grave definition of society **1** · the state as society politically organised, with its particular need **2** · at least four of the seven social determinants **2** · the command-and-obedience contrast **1** · a closing line linking precision to liberty **1**.
