## Lesson 17 — State and government: the organ and the organization

Progress: 17 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part III — the state's neighbours

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §5 and §9, with the Advanced companion's §4 and §7, read against **STATE AND GOVERNMENT** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.156 = printed 141), which supplies MacIver's organ/organization passage from *The Web of Government* (1965), the obedience/loyalty contrast, the credibility argument and the control-mechanism test.
CA search: "distinction between state and government loyalty obedience constitutional control 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's conceptual argument. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem restated

✅ The chapter concedes the whole force of the confusion before correcting it: **government is regarded as an essential element of the state. In actual practice, the state is represented by the government. Governments exercise all authority and functions on behalf of the state.**

⚠️ Then the correction: **However, the terms 'state' and 'government' should not be used synonymously. 'State' represents a wider and more stable entity than 'government'.**

```text
   WIDER      -> the state includes more than the government
   MORE STABLE -> the state outlasts particular governments
```

🔑 Two adjectives, two different arguments. ✅ The *width* argument is MacIver's; the *stability* argument is the rise-and-fall argument of Lesson 8.

### 2. MacIver's organ and organization

✅ The chapter quotes **R.M. MacIver** (*The Web of Government*; **1965**) at length:

> When we speak of the state we mean the organization, of which government is the administrative organ. Every social organization must have a focus of administration, an agency by which its policies are given specific character and translated into action. But the organization is greater than the organ. In this sense, the state is greater and more inclusive than government. A state has a constitution, a code of laws, a way of setting up its government, a body of citizens. When we think of this whole structure we think of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.156 (printed 141)

```text
   THE STATE = THE ORGANIZATION
        |-- a CONSTITUTION
        |-- a CODE OF LAWS
        |-- a WAY OF SETTING UP ITS GOVERNMENT
        |-- a BODY OF CITIZENS
        |
        +-- GOVERNMENT = THE ADMINISTRATIVE ORGAN
                 "a focus of administration, an agency by
                  which its policies are given specific
                  character and translated into action"
   PRINCIPLE: "THE ORGANIZATION IS GREATER THAN THE ORGAN"
```

✅ The four-item inventory — **constitution**, **code of laws**, **way of setting up its government**, **body of citizens** — is the most concrete statement in the chapter of what the state contains beyond its government. 🔑 Reproducing all four is the difference between asserting that the state is wider and showing it.

⚠️ Note that the third item is the **way of setting up its government**. ✅ That is exactly the thing whose suspension, at Lesson 8, reduces a state to a colony. ⚠️ The two passages are a single argument distributed across five pages.

### 3. Obedience and loyalty

✅ The chapter's most quotable pair of sentences:

> The state serves as a symbol of unity of the people. The image of the state inspires unity among the people and provides them with an identity as a nation. It arouses national pride and a spirit of sacrifice among the people. Government only represents a working arrangement to carry out functions of the state. Government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty. Government may be good or bad, efficient or inefficient, but the state will continue to be a symbol of our national greatness. We may criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state!
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.156 (printed 141)

| | ✅ Government | ✅ State |
|---|---|---|
| What it commands | **Obedience** | **Loyalty** |
| What it is | **A working arrangement to carry out functions of the state** | **A symbol of unity of the people** |
| Quality | **May be good or bad, efficient or inefficient** | **Will continue to be a symbol of our national greatness** |
| Our stance | ⚠️ We may **criticize or condemn** it | ⚠️ We may still **acclaim** its greatness |

🔑 **Government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty** is the single highest-yield sentence in Part III. ⚠️ It is also the sentence that makes democratic criticism coherent: without the distinction, criticising ministers would be disloyalty to the political community.

⚠️ A caution, however. ⚠️ The passage runs **identity as a nation**, **national pride** and **national greatness** together with the state, which anticipates the nation-state identification that Lessons 19-20 and 32 will complicate. ⚠️ The chapter's later position is that nation and state are **generally regarded as concomitant** but that **many examples will prove otherwise**. ✅ Take the later statement as controlling.

### 4. The credibility argument and the control-mechanism test

✅ The chapter refuses to let the distinction become an excuse for tolerating bad government:

> It is, however, essential that our duties and obligations toward the state should be determined by the character of the government it creates. If the government loses its credibility, it should either be replaced according to the established procedure, or the credibility of the state itself will be eroded.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.156 (printed 141)

```text
   BAD GOVERNMENT, NOT REPLACED
        |
   CREDIBILITY OF THE STATE ITSELF IS ERODED
        |
   => loyalty to the state REQUIRES replacing
      discredited governments
```

✅ And the test that follows is the chapter's standing criterion for evaluating any theory of the state:

> The government should be subjected to constant watch so that it conforms to the image of the state as the protector and promoter of our common interests. Any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.156 (printed 141)

🔑 Memorise **a concrete control mechanism over the government**. ✅ It is applied to idealism at Lesson 18, invoked at Lesson 12's disastrous-consequences clause, and is the chapter's measure of an acceptable political theory.

### 5. Comparison: three propositions that must be held together

| Proposition | ✅ Source in the chapter | ⚠️ What it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| The state is **wider and more stable** than government | p.156 | ⚠️ Treating a change of ministry as a change of regime |
| Our obligations **should be determined by the character of the government it creates** | p.156 | ⚠️ Using the distinction to insulate rulers from judgment |
| A theory must supply **a concrete control mechanism over the government** | p.156 | ⚠️ Idealist sacralisation of the state |

⚠️ Candidates reliably retrieve the first and drop the second and third, which inverts the chapter's balance. ✅ The distinction exists to make criticism *possible*, not to make it *illegitimate*.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The obedience/loyalty distinction is psychologically appealing and politically convenient for incumbents. ⚠️ Governments routinely describe criticism of policy as disloyalty to the state, and the chapter's own language — **national pride**, **a spirit of sacrifice**, **our national greatness** — supplies the vocabulary for doing so.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter anticipates this and blocks it twice. ✅ First, loyalty to the state is made conditional on governmental accountability: **if the government loses its credibility, it should either be replaced according to the established procedure, or the credibility of the state itself will be eroded** — so protecting the state *requires* replacing bad governments. ✅ Second, the control-mechanism test rules out any theory that cannot be used against rulers. ⚠️ On the chapter's own terms, the incumbent's appeal to loyalty is self-defeating.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works inside constitutional conditions and is silent outside them. ⚠️ The phrase **according to the established procedure** presupposes that a procedure exists and functions; where it does not, the chapter offers no further guidance. ➡️ The right of resistance belongs to **Topic 14**, and ❌ nothing is supplied here on the chapter's behalf.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the most frequently examined distinction in the topic, and the one most often answered in two lines when it supports twelve.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Distinguish between state and government.* — MacIver's four-item inventory, the obedience/loyalty sentence, then the control-mechanism test.
- ❌ **Trap.** Concluding that the state deserves unconditional loyalty. ✅ The chapter makes the state's credibility depend on governmental accountability.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Close with the control-mechanism test; it converts a classificatory answer into an evaluative one.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **In actual practice, the state is represented by the government**, which **exercises all authority and functions on behalf of the state**.
2. ✅ Yet the terms **should not be used synonymously**: the state is **a wider and more stable entity**.
3. ✅ **MacIver**, *The Web of Government* (**1965**): the state is **the organization, of which government is the administrative organ**.
4. ✅ Government is **a focus of administration, an agency by which its policies are given specific character and translated into action**.
5. ✅ **But the organization is greater than the organ.**
6. ✅ A state has **a constitution, a code of laws, a way of setting up its government, a body of citizens**.
7. ✅ The state is **a symbol of unity of the people** and provides **an identity as a nation**.
8. ✅ **Government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty.**
9. ✅ **Government may be good or bad, efficient or inefficient**, but the state remains **a symbol of our national greatness**.
10. ✅ **We may criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state.**
11. ✅ Our duties toward the state **should be determined by the character of the government it creates**.
12. ✅ A discredited government must be **replaced according to the established procedure**, or **the credibility of the state itself will be eroded**.
13. ✅ **Any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences.**
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for MacIver's inventory: **Constitution · Code · Creation-procedure · Citizens**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the chapter prevents the state/government distinction from becoming a shield for bad government.

**Model answer:** ✅ It attaches two conditions to the distinction. ✅ The first is a credibility link: **our duties and obligations toward the state should be determined by the character of the government it creates**, so that **if the government loses its credibility, it should either be replaced according to the established procedure, or the credibility of the state itself will be eroded**. ⚠️ Loyalty to the state therefore demands, rather than forbids, the removal of discredited governments. ✅ The second is a test on theories: **the government should be subjected to constant watch so that it conforms to the image of the state as the protector and promoter of our common interests**, and **any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**. ✅ The chapter applies that test at once, rejecting the idealist theory precisely because it **does not make a distinction between state and government**. 🔑 The distinction is thus an instrument of accountability, not an immunity.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat **government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty** as making loyalty unconditional. ⚠️ The chapter immediately makes the state's own credibility depend on whether discredited governments are replaced, so unconditional loyalty would erode the very thing it claims to honour.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Distinguish between the state and the government, and explain the political importance of the distinction. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **MacIver**: the state is **the organization, of which government is the administrative organ**, since **the organization is greater than the organ**. ✅ A state has **a constitution, a code of laws, a way of setting up its government, a body of citizens**, whereas government is **a focus of administration... by which its policies are given specific character and translated into action**. ✅ The state is therefore **a wider and more stable entity**: **governments may rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state**. ✅ Hence **government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty**, and **we may criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state**. ⚠️ The importance is accountability: **any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**.

**Rubric [10]:** MacIver's organ/organization formula with the greater-than principle **2** · all four items of the state inventory **2** · the wider-and-more-stable claim **1** · the obedience/loyalty sentence **2** · the criticise-and-still-acclaim consequence **1** · the credibility link and the control-mechanism test **2**.

## Lesson 18 — Idealist, liberal-democratic and Marxist readings of the distinction

Progress: 18 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part IV — three theories of the state-government relation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 and §9, with the Advanced companion's §6 and §7, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.156-157 (printed 141-142), which supplies the idealist theory and Hegel's march-of-God phrase, the liberal-democratic account of the state as the will of society, and the Marxist treatment of government as the agency of a class state.
CA search: "idealist theory of the state Hegel march of God on earth criticism 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's exposition of these three theories. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. One test, three verdicts

⚠️ Lesson 17 ended with a test: does the theory provide **a concrete control mechanism over the government**? ✅ The chapter now applies it to three traditions in turn, and the results are not symmetrical.

```text
                   DOES IT DISTINGUISH STATE FROM GOVERNMENT?
                                  |
   IDEALIST  ------------------>  NO
      Hegel: state, esp. the nation-state, as
      'the march of God on earth'
      demands COMPLETE SUBORDINATION of man
      WITHOUT ensuring that the actual
      government conforms to the ideal
                                  |
   LIBERAL-DEMOCRATIC  --------->  YES, and insists on it
      state = product of the 'will of society'
            = instrument of 'conflict-resolution'
            = securing the COMMON INTEREST
      society constitutes government BY FREE CHOICE
      government RESPONSIBLE to the people
      works with the CONTINUOUS CONSENT of the people
      recognises the FALLIBILITY OF GOVERNMENT
                                  |
   MARXIST  -------------------->  NO, but for the opposite reason
      government = AGENCY of the state
      imperfection of government ATTRIBUTED TO
      THE STATE ITSELF
      class-divided society => any government
      serves the DOMINANT CLASS
      => transformation and ULTIMATE WITHERING AWAY
         of the state
```

🔑 Idealism and Marxism both refuse the distinction; they differ in the direction of the refusal. ⚠️ Idealism elevates the government by identifying it with an ideal state; Marxism condemns the state by identifying it with a flawed government. ✅ Saying this in one sentence is the sophisticated form of the answer.

### 2. The idealist theory

✅ The chapter's indictment is compact:

> The idealist theory does not make a distinction between state and government. It creates an image of the perfect state. Hegel eulogized the state, especially the nation-state, as the 'march of God on earth'! This theory demands complete subordination of man to the authority and command of the state, without ensuring whether the actual government which makes such demands, conforms to the image of the ideal state or not!
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.156-157 (printed 141-142)

| Step | ✅ What the theory does | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Fails to distinguish state from government | ⚠️ The actual and the ideal merge |
| 2 | **Creates an image of the perfect state** | ⚠️ Sets the standard inside the thing being judged |
| 3 | **Hegel eulogized the state, especially the nation-state, as the 'march of God on earth'** | ⚠️ Sacralises it |
| 4 | **Demands complete subordination of man to the authority and command of the state** | ⚠️ Removes the ground of criticism |
| 5 | **Without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the image of the ideal state** | ⚠️ The fatal gap |

⚠️ Two notes of precision. ✅ The chapter says Hegel eulogised the state **especially the nation-state** — a detail that connects the idealist theory to Part V. ⚠️ And the exclamation marks are the chapter's own, in both sentences; they signal that this is the one theory the chapter treats with open disapproval.

⚠️ Observe also a tension the chapter does not resolve. ⚠️ The same Hegel supplied, at Lesson 14, the civil-society/state distinction that Marcuse called **particularly significant** for analysing modern unfreedom. ✅ So Hegel gives the chapter one of its most useful distinctions and one of its most criticised doctrines. ⚠️ Noticing this is accurate reading, not a contradiction to be resolved on the chapter's behalf.

### 3. The liberal-democratic theory

✅ The chapter's approval is equally explicit — **the liberal-democratic theory is more rational in this respect** — and the account has five components:

| # | ✅ Component |
|---|---|
| 1 | The state is **a product of the 'will of society'** |
| 2 | It is **an instrument of 'conflict-resolution'** |
| 3 | It secures **the common interest** |
| 4 | Society is authorised **to constitute a government by free choice** |
| 5 | Government must be **responsible to the people** and **work with the continuous consent of the people** |

✅ And the clinching generalisation:

> Any political theory which creates a truly constitutional government cannot be ignorant of the fallibility of government. It must recognize the distinction between the state and government.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

🔑 **The fallibility of government** is the key phrase. ⚠️ Constitutionalism, on this account, is not a set of institutions but an acknowledgment: governments can be wrong, so the political order must be built to correct them. ✅ That is the positive form of the control-mechanism test.

⚠️ Component 1 — **the will of society** — is the chapter cashing in Lesson 12's claim that **it is society which chooses the pattern of its political grouping**. ✅ The two passages are a single argument.

### 4. The Marxist theory

✅ The chapter's exposition:

> Marxist theory treats government as agency of the state. It attributes any imperfection of government to the state itself. Accordingly, so long as society is divided into dominant and dependent classes, any government is bound to serve as an instrument of the dominant class. Thus, Marxist theory regards the state itself as an instrument of class exploitation, and advocates transformation, and ultimate withering away, of the state in order to restore 'authority' to a classless society.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

```text
   government = AGENCY of the state
        |
   imperfection of government -> attributed to THE STATE ITSELF
        |
   society divided into DOMINANT and DEPENDENT classes
        |
   ANY government is bound to serve THE DOMINANT CLASS
        |
   the state is an INSTRUMENT OF CLASS EXPLOITATION
        |
   TRANSFORMATION + ULTIMATE WITHERING AWAY of the state
        |
   'AUTHORITY' RESTORED to a CLASSLESS SOCIETY
```

⚠️ Mark the phrase **in order to restore 'authority' to a classless society**, with the chapter's own inverted commas around authority. ✅ The Marxist goal here is not the abolition of all coordination but the return of authority to a society without classes. ❌ Reporting it as "the abolition of authority" misstates the chapter. ➡️ The full doctrine of the withering away belongs to **Topic 04**.

⚠️ The structural point: Marxism refuses the distinction *because* it denies that the state is a neutral organisation of which government is merely the organ. ✅ If the state is itself class-structured, then a bad government is not a deviation from a good state but an expression of it.

### 5. Comparison on three axes

| Axis | ✅ Idealist | ✅ Liberal-democratic | ✅ Marxist |
|---|---|---|---|
| State/government distinction | ❌ Rejected | ✅ Required | ❌ Rejected |
| Source of the state | ⚠️ An ideal realised | **The 'will of society'** | ⚠️ Class division |
| What follows for the citizen | **Complete subordination** | **Continuous consent** and free choice of government | ⚠️ Transformation and **withering away** |
| Passes the control-mechanism test? | ❌ No | ✅ Yes | ⚠️ Not within the class state; the remedy is to end it |

⚠️ The fourth row is where the three theories are genuinely compared rather than listed. ✅ The chapter's test is institutional; Marxism's answer is structural, so it neither passes nor fails in the chapter's terms. ⚠️ Saying that precisely is better than forcing a verdict the chapter does not give.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter judges three theories by one criterion of its own choosing. ⚠️ A Marxist would reply that the control mechanism is itself part of the superstructure and will be operated by the dominant class, so passing the test proves nothing. ⚠️ An idealist would reply that the chapter has caricatured a doctrine about the ethical significance of political community as a licence for obedience.

✅ **Reply.** The test is not arbitrary, because it follows from the chapter's own account of government as **fallible**: a theory that cannot correct a fallible agent is defective on its own terms. ✅ Against the Marxist reply, the chapter does not claim that control mechanisms are class-neutral; it claims that a theory which provides none is **bound to have disastrous consequences**, which is compatible with conceding that existing mechanisms are imperfect. ⚠️ Against the idealist reply, the chapter's criticism is narrow and textual: idealism **demands complete subordination... without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the image of the ideal state**, which is a point about the gap between ideal and actual, not about the value of community.

⚠️ **Residual.** The chapter nevertheless treats idealism more summarily than the other two, and its two exclamation marks do argumentative work that an argument should do. ⚠️ It also never asks whether the liberal-democratic account can explain governments that retain **continuous consent** while serving sectional interests — the question Marxism poses most sharply. ⚠️ Both gaps are real and should be stated rather than papered over.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Any question on the state/government distinction that goes beyond definition is answered by these three theories.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Examine the idealist theory of the state.* — the five-step indictment plus the control-mechanism test.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Marxism makes no distinction between state and government "like idealism". ✅ Both refuse it, for opposite reasons, with opposite consequences.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the opposite-directions sentence as your conclusion; it shows comparison rather than enumeration.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The idealist theory does not make a distinction between state and government.**
2. ✅ **It creates an image of the perfect state.**
3. ✅ **Hegel eulogized the state, especially the nation-state, as the 'march of God on earth'.**
4. ✅ Idealism **demands complete subordination of man to the authority and command of the state**.
5. ✅ It does so **without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the image of the ideal state or not**.
6. ✅ **The liberal-democratic theory is more rational in this respect.**
7. ✅ It treats the state as **a product of the 'will of society'**, an **instrument of 'conflict-resolution'** and of **securing the common interest**.
8. ✅ It **authorizes society to constitute a government by free choice**.
9. ✅ It demands that government be **responsible to the people** and **work with the continuous consent of the people**.
10. ✅ **Any political theory which creates a truly constitutional government cannot be ignorant of the fallibility of government.**
11. ✅ **Marxist theory treats government as agency of the state** and **attributes any imperfection of government to the state itself**.
12. ✅ While society is **divided into dominant and dependent classes, any government is bound to serve as an instrument of the dominant class**.
13. ✅ It regards the state as **an instrument of class exploitation** and advocates **transformation, and ultimate withering away, of the state in order to restore 'authority' to a classless society**.
14. 🔑 One-line comparison: **idealism elevates government into the state; Marxism condemns the state into government; liberal democracy keeps them apart.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Idealism and Marxism both refuse the state/government distinction. Explain why the refusals are not the same, and what each loses as a result.

**Model answer:** ✅ Idealism refuses it upwards. ✅ It **creates an image of the perfect state**, eulogised by Hegel as **the 'march of God on earth'**, and then **demands complete subordination of man to the authority and command of the state, without ensuring whether the actual government which makes such demands, conforms to the image of the ideal state**. ⚠️ What is lost is any ground for criticising rulers, because the actual government inherits the ideal state's authority. ✅ Marxism refuses it downwards. ✅ It **treats government as agency of the state** and **attributes any imperfection of government to the state itself**, holding that while society is **divided into dominant and dependent classes, any government is bound to serve as an instrument of the dominant class**. ⚠️ What is lost is the possibility of reform within the existing order, since the remedy becomes **transformation, and ultimate withering away, of the state**. 🔑 Same refusal, opposite directions, opposite costs.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not describe the Marxist goal as the abolition of authority. ⚠️ The chapter's wording is that withering away restores **'authority' to a classless society**, with the inverted commas its own — authority is relocated, not eliminated.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the idealist theory of the state and explain why it is criticised. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The idealist theory **does not make a distinction between state and government** and **creates an image of the perfect state**; **Hegel eulogized the state, especially the nation-state, as the 'march of God on earth'**. ✅ From this it **demands complete subordination of man to the authority and command of the state**. ⚠️ The criticism follows from the first failure: the demand is made **without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the image of the ideal state**. ⚠️ Since every demand in practice issues from a government, sacralising the state transfers the ideal's authority to a fallible agent and removes the ground of criticism. ✅ The chapter's standing test is decisive: **any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**. ✅ The liberal-democratic alternative is preferred because it **cannot be ignorant of the fallibility of government**.

**Rubric [10]:** the failure to distinguish, with the perfect-state image **2** · Hegel's phrase quoted and attributed with the nation-state qualifier **2** · the complete-subordination demand **2** · the non-conformity gap stated as the decisive criticism **2** · the control-mechanism test applied **1** · the liberal-democratic contrast via fallibility **1**.

## Lesson 19 — Nationality: the narrow base

Progress: 19 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part V — nation, nationality, self-determination

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the opening of **STATE AND NATION** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 = printed 142), which supplies the nation-state vocabulary, the definition of nationality, the birth-basis claim, the separation effect and the rejection of one-nationality-one-state.
CA search: "nationality common race language religion culture political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned contemporary identity politics commentary with no bearing on the chapter's definitional claim. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The vocabulary that hides the problem

✅ The chapter begins with an observation about language:

> The modern state usually takes the form of a nation-state. The frontiers of the state are called national frontiers; the interest of the state is described as national interest; the character of the people of a state is called its national character. Relations between different states are known as international relations.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

```text
   frontiers OF THE STATE        -> called NATIONAL frontiers
   interest OF THE STATE         -> called NATIONAL interest
   character of the PEOPLE       -> called NATIONAL character
   relations between STATES      -> called INTERnational relations
   => everyday vocabulary ASSUMES that state = nation
```

🔑 This is a linguistic argument with a conceptual payoff. ⚠️ Four common expressions substitute "national" for "state", so the identification of nation with state is built into ordinary speech — which is why distinguishing them requires deliberate effort, and why the chapter spends three lessons on it.

### 2. Nationality defined

✅ The chapter's definition is a list and must be learnt as one:

> Nationality usually denotes a set of people inspired by a feeling of unity based on common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations and historical development.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

| # | ✅ Basis of nationality | ⚠️ Type |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Common race** | Ascriptive |
| 2 | **Language** | Ascriptive in practice |
| 3 | **Religion** | Ascriptive in practice |
| 4 | **Culture** | Ascriptive in practice |
| 5 | **Geographical compactness** | ⚠️ Spatial |
| 6 | **Common political aspirations** | ⚠️ Volitional |
| 7 | **Historical development** | ⚠️ Temporal |

⚠️ Note that items 6 and 7 reappear in the definition of **nation** at Lesson 20. ✅ So nationality and nation are not disjoint; the nation keeps the volitional and historical elements and drops the ascriptive ones. 🔑 That overlap is what makes the pair confusing and what a precise answer must handle.

### 3. The birth-basis claim and its consequences

✅ The chapter's critical move:

> Most of these factors are based on birth and provide little scope for expanding the horizons of social relationships. Feelings of nationality separate one set of people from other such sets. Sometimes this is accompanied by a sense of one's own superiority, or a sense of disdain for others which may lead to tensions, wars and other disastrous consequences. In any case, the feeling of nationality grows from a relatively narrow base.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

```text
   MOST FACTORS ARE BASED ON BIRTH
            |
   LITTLE SCOPE FOR EXPANDING THE HORIZONS
   OF SOCIAL RELATIONSHIPS
            |
   FEELINGS OF NATIONALITY SEPARATE ONE SET
   OF PEOPLE FROM OTHER SUCH SETS
            |
   sometimes + A SENSE OF ONE'S OWN SUPERIORITY
             + A SENSE OF DISDAIN FOR OTHERS
            |
   -> TENSIONS, WARS AND OTHER DISASTROUS CONSEQUENCES
            |
   IN ANY CASE: a RELATIVELY NARROW BASE
```

⚠️ Four precisions protect an answer here.

- ⚠️ **Most**, not all, of the factors are based on birth — which is why 6 and 7 survive into the concept of nation.
- ⚠️ The separation claim is unconditional: feelings of nationality **separate**. ⚠️ The superiority and disdain are conditional: **sometimes**.
- ⚠️ The chain to **tensions, wars** runs through the conditional, not through the separation alone. ❌ Do not report the chapter as saying that nationality causes war.
- ✅ The closing clause is a concession-plus-verdict: **in any case** — whatever else is true — the base is **relatively narrow**.

⚠️ Place this beside Lesson 7: **patriotism—the sense of belonging to a state—binds the people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures**. ✅ Nationality separates on the very markers patriotism binds across. 🔑 That opposition is the chapter's central contrast in Part V and the best single sentence to carry into an answer.

### 4. The rejection of one-nationality-one-state

✅ The chapter rejects a position before stating its own:

> Some writers define nation on the same basis as nationality and then advocate a separate state for each nationality. This view is no longer held valid.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.157 (printed 142)

| ⚠️ The rejected view | ✅ The chapter's verdict |
|---|---|
| Nation is defined like nationality | ❌ Rejected |
| Each nationality should have a separate state | ❌ **No longer held valid** |

⚠️ Two reasons are available from the surrounding text, and an answer should supply them because the chapter does not argue the rejection at length. ⚠️ First, the base is too narrow: a political order built on birth-markers cannot expand **the horizons of social relationships**. ⚠️ Second, as Lesson 5 established, **a homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state**, so the premise that a state needs one nationality has already been denied.

➡️ The ideological elaboration of the one-nation-one-state formula is nationalism, taught at Lesson 29; the practical mismatch is at Lesson 32.

### 5. Comparison: the three terms, kept apart

| Term | ✅ What it rests on | ⚠️ What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Nationality** | **Common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations, historical development**, mostly **based on birth** | ⚠️ **Separates one set of people from other such sets** |
| **Nation** (L20) | **Sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny** in **a defined territory** | ✅ Includes **different nationalities** |
| **State** | Population, territory, government, sovereignty | ✅ Legal-political, **need not** be culturally uniform |

⚠️ The decisive structural fact is that nationality is a **sub-state** category and nation is a **state-wide** one. ✅ A state may contain many nationalities and one nation; what it cannot do, on the chapter's account, is require one nationality.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter's account of nationality is pejorative. ⚠️ It associates nationality with birth, narrowness, superiority, disdain and war, while reserving the positive vocabulary — unity, destiny, allegiance — for nation. ⚠️ Yet the two definitions share **common political aspirations** and **historical development**, so the moral asymmetry is not supported by the conceptual difference.

✅ **Reply.** The asymmetry is grounded in the base, not in attitude. ✅ Because **most of these factors are based on birth**, nationality offers **little scope for expanding the horizons of social relationships** — a structural limit, not a moral judgment. ⚠️ The pejorative consequences are explicitly conditional: superiority and disdain occur **sometimes**, not necessarily. ✅ And the chapter does not deny nationality's value; it denies only that nationality should be the principle of state formation.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply does not fully dissolve the problem. ⚠️ If a nation also rests partly on **common history** and a **sense of unity**, it is not obvious why those are expansive while nationality's **historical development** is narrow. ⚠️ The chapter's answer can only be that the nation admits **groups of people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures** — a difference of inclusiveness rather than of kind. ⚠️ That is a real difference and a weaker one than the chapter's rhetoric suggests.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The nation/nationality distinction is the chapter's own and is the hinge of every question on nationalism.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Distinguish between nation and nationality.* — list the seven bases, state the birth claim, then the separation effect.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that nationality causes war. ✅ The chapter routes the consequence through a conditional **sometimes**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Set the separation clause against Lesson 7's patriotism clause; the contrast is the chapter's own and reads as analysis.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The modern state usually takes the form of a nation-state.**
2. ✅ Hence **national frontiers**, **national interest**, **national character** and **international relations** — four expressions that assume the identity.
3. ✅ **Nationality usually denotes a set of people inspired by a feeling of unity.**
4. ✅ Its seven bases: **common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations and historical development**.
5. ✅ **Most of these factors are based on birth.**
6. ✅ They **provide little scope for expanding the horizons of social relationships**.
7. ✅ **Feelings of nationality separate one set of people from other such sets.**
8. ✅ Sometimes accompanied by **a sense of one's own superiority, or a sense of disdain for others**.
9. ✅ This **may lead to tensions, wars and other disastrous consequences**.
10. ✅ **In any case, the feeling of nationality grows from a relatively narrow base.**
11. ✅ Some writers define nation like nationality and advocate **a separate state for each nationality**; this view is **no longer held valid**.
12. ⚠️ Items 6 and 7 of the list survive into the definition of **nation**; the ascriptive items do not.
13. ⚠️ Nationality separates on exactly the markers that patriotism, at Lesson 7, binds across.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the seven bases: **Race · Language · Religion · Culture · Compactness · Aspirations · History**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter say that the feeling of nationality grows from a "relatively narrow base", and what follows politically?

**Model answer:** ✅ Because **most of these factors are based on birth**. ✅ Nationality rests on **common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations and historical development**, and the first four — together with compactness — are not chosen. ⚠️ Unchosen markers cannot be extended to outsiders, so they **provide little scope for expanding the horizons of social relationships**. ✅ Politically, two things follow. ✅ First, **feelings of nationality separate one set of people from other such sets**, **sometimes** with **a sense of one's own superiority, or a sense of disdain for others**, which **may lead to tensions, wars and other disastrous consequences**. ✅ Second, the proposal to give **a separate state for each nationality** is rejected as **no longer held valid**, since a state built on a narrow base could not accommodate the plural population the chapter has already said the modern state need not avoid. 🔑 Narrow base, divisive effect, rejected political formula.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not state that nationality necessarily produces conflict. ⚠️ The chapter's separation claim is unconditional, but superiority and disdain are introduced by **sometimes**, and it is through them that the route to **tensions, wars** runs. ⚠️ Dropping the qualifier converts a careful claim into an overstatement an examiner can puncture.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss the concept of nationality and its political implications. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Nationality usually denotes a set of people inspired by a feeling of unity based on common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations and historical development**. ⚠️ Its narrowness follows from the fact that **most of these factors are based on birth**. ✅ Politically, **feelings of nationality separate one set of people from other such sets**, **sometimes** accompanied by **a sense of one's own superiority, or a sense of disdain for others**, which **may lead to tensions, wars and other disastrous consequences**; **in any case, the feeling of nationality grows from a relatively narrow base**. ✅ Hence the chapter rejects the view that defines nation like nationality and advocates **a separate state for each nationality** as **no longer held valid**. ⚠️ The contrast is instructive: patriotism, **the sense of belonging to a state**, binds people of **different races, with different religions, languages and cultures**.

**Rubric [10]:** the definition with at least five of the seven bases **3** · the birth-basis claim and the limited-horizons consequence **2** · the separation effect **2** · the conditional route to superiority, disdain and conflict **1** · the narrow-base verdict **1** · the rejection of one-nationality-one-state **1**.

## Lesson 20 — Nation: the wider base

Progress: 20 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part V — nation, nationality, self-determination

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §5, with the Advanced companion's §4, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.157-158 (printed 142-143), which supplies the definition of nation, the different-nationalities clause, the undivided-allegiance claim, the transcendence of birth and the equality-of-nations inference.
CA search: "nation defined common destiny undivided allegiance different nationalities 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's definitional treatment. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The definition, clause by clause

✅ The chapter's definition of nation:

> A nation grows on a much wider base. It refers to people living in a defined territory, inspired by a sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny though they may belong to different nationalities. In other words, groups of people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures, etc. may live together and feel united as citizens of the same state, owing their undivided allegiance to that state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.157-158 (printed 142-143)

| # | ✅ Element of the definition | ⚠️ Comment |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **People living in a defined territory** | ⚠️ Territorial, like the state |
| 2 | **A sense of unity** | ⚠️ Subjective |
| 3 | **Common political aspirations** | ⚠️ Shared with nationality's list |
| 4 | **Common interests** | ⚠️ New — functional |
| 5 | **Common history** | ⚠️ Shared with nationality's list |
| 6 | **Common destiny** | ⚠️ New — prospective |
| 7 | **Though they may belong to different nationalities** | ✅ The decisive clause |

🔑 Element 6, **common destiny**, is the chapter's most distinctive addition and the easiest to drop. ⚠️ Nationality looks backwards to origins; nation looks forwards to a shared future. ✅ That is why the nation can include people whose origins differ.

```text
   NATIONALITY                     NATION
   mostly BASED ON BIRTH           grows on a MUCH WIDER BASE
   looks BACKWARD (descent)        looks FORWARD (common destiny)
   SEPARATES sets of people        unites DIFFERENT NATIONALITIES
   sub-state                       co-extensive with the state
   narrow base                     TRANSCENDS THE CONDITIONS OF BIRTH
```

### 2. Undivided allegiance, and the extension to residents

✅ Two further claims complete the definition:

- ✅ Groups of different races, religions, languages and cultures **may live together and feel united as citizens of the same state, owing their undivided allegiance to that state**.
- ✅ **Thus, nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state.**

⚠️ The second sentence is doing serious work and is routinely missed. ✅ Nationhood extends to **permanent residents** — a status acquired by settlement, not by descent. ⚠️ That makes the nation, on this account, closer in logic to the territorial principle of Lesson 6 than to the ascriptive bases of Lesson 19. ✅ Engels's locality principle and Gauba's nationhood are the same move applied at different levels.

⚠️ The phrase **undivided allegiance** should also be noted, because it reappears with a qualification at Lesson 32, where the chapter describes naturalised citizens **so deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin that they are prepared to make sacrifices for the welfare of those countries**. ⚠️ The two passages sit in tension: allegiance is defined as undivided and then described as divided. ⚠️ The chapter does not reconcile them, and an answer should note the tension rather than smooth it.

### 3. The honest concession, and the inference

✅ The chapter does not idealise the nation:

> Members of a nation of course distinguish themselves from other nations. They may sometimes be prejudiced against other peoples. Yet a logical outcome of the idea of a nation postulates equality among nations, their co-existence and cooperation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.158 (printed 143)

```text
   CONCEDED:   nations DISTINGUISH themselves from others
               they MAY SOMETIMES BE PREJUDICED
                        |
   ARGUED:     YET a LOGICAL OUTCOME of the idea of a nation
               postulates
                 - EQUALITY among nations
                 - CO-EXISTENCE
                 - COOPERATION
```

⚠️ The structure is a concession followed by a conceptual claim, and the claim is explicitly about **logical outcome**, not about behaviour. ✅ If a nation is constituted by a sense of unity among people of different origins, then the same reasoning applied across nations yields equality rather than hierarchy. 🔑 This is the chapter's internal bridge from nation to internationalism, and it is why Lesson 31 is not an afterthought.

### 4. Comparison: nation, nationality and state, finally aligned

| Question | ✅ Nationality | ✅ Nation | ✅ State |
|---|---|---|---|
| Territory required? | ⚠️ **Geographical compactness** only | ✅ **A defined territory** | ✅ Yes, essential |
| Membership acquired how? | ⚠️ Mostly by **birth** | ✅ By unity and residence — **extends to the permanent residents** | ✅ By legal status within territory |
| Internal diversity? | ❌ Presupposes similarity | ✅ Explicitly admits **different nationalities** | ✅ **A homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature** |
| Effect on outsiders | ⚠️ **Separates** | ⚠️ Distinguishes, ✅ but postulates **equality among nations** | ⚠️ Deals with them as states |

⚠️ Read the third row across. ✅ Nation and state agree against nationality: both admit internal diversity. ⚠️ That agreement is what allows the chapter to accept the **nation-state** as a workable form while rejecting the formula that each nationality needs a state.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The definition of the nation is so inclusive that it collapses into the definition of the state. ⚠️ People in a defined territory, united, owing undivided allegiance, including permanent residents — this describes the citizenry. ⚠️ If so, the nation adds nothing, and the chapter's own later insistence that nation and state are *not* concomitant becomes unintelligible.

✅ **Reply.** The nation adds a subjective element the state does not require. ✅ The state is constituted by population, territory, government and sovereignty; the nation requires a **sense of unity** and a **common destiny** — attitudes, not institutions. ⚠️ That is exactly why the two can come apart in both directions: the **Kurds** are a nation without a state, while **Lebanon**, **Cyprus** and others are states whose peoples **have not been able to assimilate themselves into one nation** — both cases from Lesson 32. ✅ The definitions are distinct precisely where the mismatches occur.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply exposes a further difficulty rather than removing it. ⚠️ If the nation requires a **sense of unity**, the chapter supplies no account of how that sense is produced — except at Lesson 29, where it may be **created** or **invented** through symbols. ⚠️ A nation that can be invented sits awkwardly with a nation defined by **common history**, and the chapter never faces the question.

### 6. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This definition is what makes plural statehood theoretically defensible and is the natural opening of any multiculturalism answer that wants a political-theory grounding.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Can a state contain more than one nationality?* — the different-nationalities clause answers it directly.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating nation as a synonym for nationality or for state. ✅ The chapter separates all three.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Quote **nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state**; it is the chapter's most portable sentence on inclusion.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **A nation grows on a much wider base.**
2. ✅ It refers to **people living in a defined territory**, inspired by **a sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny**.
3. ✅ **Though they may belong to different nationalities.**
4. ✅ Groups of **different races, with different religions, languages and cultures** may **live together and feel united as citizens of the same state**.
5. ✅ They owe **their undivided allegiance to that state**.
6. ✅ **Nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state.**
7. ✅ **Members of a nation of course distinguish themselves from other nations** and **may sometimes be prejudiced against other peoples**.
8. ✅ **Yet a logical outcome of the idea of a nation postulates equality among nations, their co-existence and cooperation.**
9. ⚠️ **Common destiny** is prospective; nationality's bases are retrospective.
10. ⚠️ Extension to **permanent residents** parallels Engels's locality principle at Lesson 6.
11. ⚠️ **Undivided allegiance** is qualified in practice at Lesson 32 by the attachment of naturalised citizens to countries of origin.
12. ⚠️ Nation and state agree against nationality in admitting internal diversity.
13. ⚠️ The nation adds a **subjective** element — unity and destiny — that the state's four elements do not require.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the definition: **Territory · Unity · Aspirations · Interests · History · Destiny — despite different nationalities**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the chapter's definition of the nation makes it possible for one state to contain several nationalities, and identify the clause that carries the weight.

**Model answer:** ✅ The weight is carried by **though they may belong to different nationalities**. ✅ The nation is defined as **people living in a defined territory, inspired by a sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny**, and none of those requires shared descent, speech, faith or custom. ✅ Hence **groups of people of different races, with different religions, languages and cultures, etc. may live together and feel united as citizens of the same state, owing their undivided allegiance to that state**, and **nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state**. ⚠️ The structural reason is that the nation's bases are partly **prospective** — a **common destiny** can be shared by people whose pasts differ — whereas nationality's bases are **mostly based on birth** and therefore unshareable. 🔑 Inclusion is possible because the criterion is forward-looking.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat **undivided allegiance** as a description of fact. ⚠️ It states what membership of a nation involves; the chapter itself later describes naturalised citizens **so deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin that they are prepared to make sacrifices for the welfare of those countries**, and the tension between the two passages is left unresolved.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Distinguish between nation and nationality. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Nationality** denotes **a set of people inspired by a feeling of unity based on common race, language, religion, culture... and historical development**; since **most of these factors are based on birth**, it offers **little scope for expanding the horizons of social relationships** and **separates one set of people from other such sets**, growing from **a relatively narrow base**. ✅ A **nation grows on a much wider base**: **people living in a defined territory, inspired by a sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny though they may belong to different nationalities**. ✅ Hence people of **different races, with different religions, languages and cultures** may **feel united as citizens of the same state**, since **nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state**. ⚠️ The chapter therefore rejects **a separate state for each nationality**.

**Rubric [10]:** nationality defined with at least five bases and the birth claim **3** · the separation effect and narrow-base verdict **1** · the nation defined with at least four of its six elements **3** · the different-nationalities clause **1** · the transcendence-of-birth sentence **1** · the rejection of one-nationality-one-state as the consequence **1**.

## Lesson 21 — National self-determination, and the task of nation-building

Progress: 21 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part V — nation, nationality, self-determination

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §5 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §4 and §9, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.158 (printed 143), which supplies the 1920 acceptance claim, the boxed definition of national self-determination with Wilson and Lenin, the four UN instruments and the nation-building paragraph.
CA search: "national self-determination United Nations declaration friendly relations 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current self-determination disputes that the chapter does not address and that would require independent verification; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. From idea to principle

✅ The chapter dates the transition precisely:

> Since 1920, the principle of national self-determination has been almost universally accepted which has led to the establishment of nation-states, and rapid development of international law to regulate relations between nation-states.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.158 (printed 143)

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **Since 1920** | ⚠️ A date, and an examinable one |
| **Almost universally accepted** | ⚠️ *Almost* — the chapter does not claim unanimity |
| Consequence 1 | ✅ **Establishment of nation-states** |
| Consequence 2 | ✅ **Rapid development of international law to regulate relations between nation-states** |

🔑 Note the pairing of the two consequences. ⚠️ The same principle that multiplies states also multiplies the law needed to manage them — which is the chapter's first hint that nationalism and internationalism grow together rather than simply opposing each other.

### 2. The boxed definition, and the two authors of the principle

✅ **National Self-Determination — The principle that each nation has the right to be independent and to choose a suitable form of government for itself.**

⚠️ Two limbs: **independence** and **choice of a suitable form of government**. ❌ A candidate who gives only the first has given half the definition.

✅ The box then names two very different originators, at the same moment, with different purposes:

| | ✅ **Woodrow Wilson (1856-1924)** | ✅ **V.I. Lenin (1870-1924)** |
|---|---|---|
| When | **At the end of the First World War (1914-18)** | **Around this time** |
| Conceived it primarily as | **A criterion for the break-up of the empires defeated in the war** | **The ground for granting independence to dependent nations from colonial and imperial domination** |
| Named targets | **Austro-Hungarian, German and Ottoman empires** | ⚠️ Colonial and imperial domination generally |
| Scope | ⚠️ Restricted to the defeated | ⚠️ Universal in principle |

```text
   SAME PRINCIPLE, TWO PURPOSES
        WILSON                    LENIN
   break up the DEFEATED     end COLONIAL and IMPERIAL
   empires:                  domination everywhere
     Austro-Hungarian
     German
     Ottoman
        |                           |
   applies to the losers     applies to the colonies
   of one war                of the winners too
```

🔑 This asymmetry is the most examinable fact on the page. ⚠️ Wilson's version, applied to the defeated empires, left the victorious colonial empires untouched; Lenin's version did not. ✅ The chapter does not say this in so many words, but the two descriptions are placed side by side and the contrast is the chapter's own construction.

### 3. The four instruments that made it law

✅ **After the Second World War (1939-45), the United Nations upheld this principle through various international documents.** ✅ Four are named:

| # | ✅ Instrument | ✅ Year |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **UN General Assembly resolution on the independence of colonial peoples** | **1960** |
| 2 | **Two UN covenants on human rights** | **1966** |
| 3 | **UN General Assembly Declaration on Friendly Relations among States** | **1970** |
| 4 | **Final Act of the Conference for Security and Cooperation in Europe** | **1975** |

✅ Their cumulative effect: these **have gradually transformed it into a general legal principle of the international community**.

⚠️ Three cautions. ⚠️ The chapter gives short titles, not citations; ❌ do not expand them into formal instrument names the page does not supply. ⚠️ The verb is **gradually transformed** — a process over fifteen years, not a single act. ✅ And the destination is **a general legal principle**, which is weaker than a rule with a determinate remedy. ➡️ The institutional detail belongs to the **International Relations** owners.

### 4. Nation-building: the task that follows independence

✅ The chapter turns from principle to difficulty:

> The developing countries, i.e. the countries of Asia, Africa and Latin America who won their independence from colonial and imperial domination after the Second World War (1939-45), are faced today with the gigantic task of nation-building. Most of them evolved a national sentiment during their struggle for independence, but disruptive tendencies started to emerge after they achieved their independence.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.158 (printed 143)

```text
   STRUGGLE FOR INDEPENDENCE
      -> national sentiment EVOLVES
             |
   INDEPENDENCE ACHIEVED
      -> DISRUPTIVE TENDENCIES START TO EMERGE
             |
   TASK: NATION-BUILDING
      = inculcating A FEELING OF UNITY
      + the process of INTEGRATION INTO COMPACT GROUPS
             |
   BOUND TO REMAIN ELUSIVE UNLESS
      (a) they CONTAIN THEIR WIDENING ECONOMIC DISPARITIES
      (b) they FREE THEMSELVES COMPLETELY FROM THE FOUL
          PLAY OF NEO-COLONIAL POWERS who continue to
          exploit them DESPITE THEIR POLITICAL INDEPENDENCE
```

⚠️ The structure of this passage is an argument, not a lament, and should be used as one.

- ⚠️ **The paradox**: the sentiment that produced independence does not survive it. ✅ A movement against a common opponent generates unity; the opponent's departure removes the unifying condition.
- ✅ **The definition**: nation-building is **inculcating a feeling of unity and the process of their integration into compact groups** — a subjective task and a structural one.
- ✅ **The two conditions**: the attempt is **bound to remain elusive** unless disparities are contained and neo-colonial exploitation ended. ⚠️ Both conditions are economic, which is the chapter's distinctive claim here — nation-building fails for material reasons, not merely cultural ones.

🔑 Answer line: on this account, national integration is an economic problem wearing a cultural face.

🔶 **Owner extension.** Both canonical owners add a cautious Indian illustration not present on the chapter's pages: post-independence linguistic reorganisation of states as a widely known pattern of nation-building that accommodates internal diversity rather than requiring one-nation-one-state congruence. 🔶 It is marked as owner-supplied, and ➡️ exact years, commission names and state lists are routed to the **Polity** and **History** owners rather than asserted here.

### 5. Comparison: the principle and its two faces

| Aspect | ✅ Self-determination as a right | ⚠️ Self-determination as a problem |
|---|---|---|
| Content | **Each nation has the right to be independent and to choose a suitable form of government for itself** | ⚠️ Which group counts as a nation? |
| Effect | ✅ **Establishment of nation-states**; break-up of empires | ⚠️ **Disruptive tendencies** inside the new states |
| Logic | ⚠️ A principle of liberation | ⚠️ The same principle, applied internally, becomes secession |

⚠️ The chapter does not state the second column, but it supplies both halves and places them two paragraphs apart. ✅ Lesson 32's list of states **divided into many parts due to such disruptive tendencies** is the second column realised. 🔑 A strong answer links the right and the problem as one principle applied at two levels.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The principle is indeterminate. ⚠️ It grants rights to "nations", but the chapter's own definition of a nation requires a **sense of unity** and a **common destiny** — subjective conditions that any sufficiently mobilised group can claim. ⚠️ A principle that cannot identify its own beneficiaries cannot settle disputes about who may secede.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's own definition narrows the field more than the objection allows. ✅ A nation is tied to **a defined territory** and to **permanent residents**, and the chapter rejects the equation of nation with nationality, so a group sharing only descent markers does not qualify. ⚠️ Moreover, the principle entered international practice as a **general legal principle**, applied historically to the break-up of empires and to decolonisation — contexts in which the claimant was identifiable by the fact of alien rule.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply handles the colonial case and not the post-colonial one. ⚠️ Where there is no alien ruler, the identification problem is untouched, and the chapter's only response is the practical one: contain disparities and end external exploitation. ⚠️ It never addresses what should happen when a territorially concentrated minority inside an independent state claims the same right. ➡️ That question is taken up, from the rights side, by Kymlicka's self-government rights at Lesson 44.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The Wilson/Lenin pairing and the four instruments are the chapter's only sustained passage of international-legal history and are highly quotable.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Examine the principle of national self-determination.* — definition, two originators, four instruments, then the nation-building difficulty.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating self-determination as purely a decolonisation principle. ✅ Wilson's version was about **the empires defeated in the war**; Lenin's was the colonial one.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Close with the two economic conditions on nation-building; they are the chapter's distinctive contribution and most answers omit them.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Since 1920**, national self-determination has been **almost universally accepted**.
2. ✅ It led to **the establishment of nation-states** and to the **rapid development of international law**.
3. ✅ Box: the principle is that **each nation has the right to be independent and to choose a suitable form of government for itself**.
4. ✅ **Woodrow Wilson (1856-1924)**, at the end of the **First World War (1914-18)**, conceived it **primarily as a criterion for the break-up of the empires defeated in the war** — **Austro-Hungarian, German and Ottoman**.
5. ✅ **V.I. Lenin (1870-1924)**, around the same time, conceived it **essentially as the ground for granting independence to dependent nations from colonial and imperial domination**.
6. ✅ After the **Second World War (1939-45)** the **United Nations** upheld the principle through various documents.
7. ✅ **UN General Assembly resolution on the independence of colonial peoples (1960)**.
8. ✅ **Two UN covenants on human rights (1966)**.
9. ✅ **UN General Assembly Declaration on Friendly Relations among States (1970)**.
10. ✅ **Final Act of the Conference for Security and Cooperation in Europe (1975)**.
11. ✅ These **gradually transformed it into a general legal principle of the international community**.
12. ✅ Developing countries face **the gigantic task of nation-building**; national sentiment evolved **during their struggle for independence**, but **disruptive tendencies started to emerge after they achieved their independence**.
13. ✅ Nation-building is **inculcating a feeling of unity and the process of their integration into compact groups**.
14. ✅ It is **bound to remain elusive** unless they **contain their widening economic disparities** and **free themselves completely from the foul play of neo-colonial powers**.
15. 🔑 Mnemonic for the four instruments: **60 · 66 · 70 · 75**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Wilson and Lenin are both credited with the principle of self-determination. Show that they were advancing different versions of it, and why the difference matters.

**Model answer:** ✅ Wilson, **at the end of the First World War (1914-18)**, conceived it **primarily as a criterion for the break-up of the empires defeated in the war, i.e. Austro-Hungarian, German and Ottoman empires**. ✅ Lenin, **around this time**, conceived it **essentially as the ground for granting independence to dependent nations from colonial and imperial domination**. ⚠️ The difference is one of scope. ⚠️ Wilson's criterion applies to the losers of a particular war, so it reorganises Central and Eastern Europe while leaving the overseas empires of the victors intact; Lenin's ground applies wherever colonial or imperial domination exists, including those empires. ⚠️ The difference matters because the later history runs on Lenin's version rather than Wilson's: the **UN General Assembly resolution on the independence of colonial peoples (1960)** and the instruments that followed extended the principle to decolonisation, **gradually** transforming it into **a general legal principle of the international community**. 🔑 Same principle, two scopes, and the wider scope prevailed.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not state the principle as independence alone. ⚠️ The chapter's box gives two limbs — **the right to be independent** *and* **to choose a suitable form of government for itself** — and the second limb is what connects self-determination to democratic choice rather than merely to sovereignty.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the principle of national self-determination and the difficulties that follow its exercise. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The principle: **each nation has the right to be independent and to choose a suitable form of government for itself**. ✅ **Wilson** conceived it **primarily as a criterion for the break-up of the empires defeated in the war**; **Lenin**, **essentially as the ground for granting independence to dependent nations from colonial and imperial domination**. ✅ **Since 1920** it has been **almost universally accepted**, and UN instruments of **1960**, **1966**, **1970** and **1975** **gradually transformed it into a general legal principle of the international community**. ⚠️ The difficulty appears afterwards. ✅ New states **evolved a national sentiment during their struggle for independence, but disruptive tendencies started to emerge after they achieved their independence**, leaving **the gigantic task of nation-building** — **inculcating a feeling of unity**. ✅ That task is **bound to remain elusive** unless they **contain their widening economic disparities** and end **the foul play of neo-colonial powers**.

**Rubric [10]:** both limbs of the definition **2** · Wilson and Lenin distinguished by scope **2** · the 1920 date and at least two UN instruments with years **2** · the sentiment-then-disruption paradox **1** · nation-building defined in the chapter's terms **1** · both economic conditions for success **2**.

## Lesson 22 — From savagery to the state, and the tribal state

Progress: 22 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part VI — the historical forms of the state

Book context: Canonical Core owner §4 and §6, with the Advanced companion's §3, read against **RISE AND GROWTH OF THE MODERN NATION-STATE** and **THE TRIBAL STATE** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.158-159 = printed 143-144), which supply the six factors, the family sequence, the six-form typology and the pre-state controversy.
