## Lesson 27 — The twentieth century: revolutions, independence and the Third World

Progress: 27 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part VII — the modern nation-state

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §4, §7 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §3 and §12, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.163 (printed 148), which supplies the two twentieth-century changes, the list of newly independent countries, the Third World designation, the socialist nation-state argument and the expansion of international organisation.
CA search: "third world developing nations decolonisation nation state stabilisation 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned contemporary development commentary with no bearing on the chapter's 2009 historical account. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Two changes, one cause

✅ The chapter's structure is explicit:

> Political consciousness and national movements started emerging in the subject peoples in the twentieth century, especially after the First World War (1914-18). Besides, people in some countries were otherwise suffering under despotic rule. The political aspirations of the oppressed people of different countries brought about two important changes in the twentieth century.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.163 (printed 148)

```text
   CAUSE: THE POLITICAL ASPIRATIONS OF THE OPPRESSED PEOPLE
          (a) subject peoples under colonial rule
          (b) people suffering under DESPOTIC RULE
                        |
          +-------------+--------------+
          |                            |
   (a) SOCIALIST REVOLUTIONS     (b) ATTAINMENT OF INDEPENDENCE
       inspired by Marxist            by the peoples of ASIA and
       ideology                       AFRICA from their colonial
       first in RUSSIA (1917)         rulers, especially after the
       later CHINA (1949)             SECOND WORLD WAR (1939-45)
       and other countries
```

⚠️ Note the chapter's own lettering, **(a)** and **(b)**, and the dates it attaches: **Russia (1917)**, **China (1949)**. ✅ Both are given inside the sentence and both are examinable.

🔑 The analytical point is the shared cause. ⚠️ The chapter derives two very different twentieth-century transformations — communist revolution and decolonisation — from the same source, **the political aspirations of the oppressed people**. ✅ That is a substantive claim, and it explains why the chapter can later treat socialist states and new nations as converging on the same political form.

### 2. The named countries, and the designation

✅ **Attainment of independence by the peoples of Asia and Africa from their colonial rulers, especially after the Second World War (1939-45), such as India, Pakistan, Burma, Egypt, Nigeria, Algeria, Ghana, Fiji, Vietnam, Indonesia, Libya, Syria, Zimbabwe and Namibia.**

✅ **These new nations along with Latin American countries are collectively described as the 'Third World' or the developing nations.**

| ✅ Component | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| Fourteen countries named | ⚠️ A sample, not a list of all |
| **Along with Latin American countries** | ⚠️ Latin America is added, though its independence is much older — see Lesson 37 |
| **'Third World'** | ⚠️ The chapter's inverted commas; a book-period category |
| **Or the developing nations** | ⚠️ Offered as a synonym here; distinguished at Lesson 35 |

⚠️ A standing caution for this whole part of the chapter. ⚠️ **Third World**, **first world**, **second world**, **North-South** and **developing nations** are historically situated vocabulary of a **2009** text. ✅ Use them as the chapter's categories; ❌ do not present them as current geopolitical description, and ❌ do not update the chapter's country examples with present-day claims.

### 3. The condition of the new states

✅ The chapter does not stop at independence:

> Their social, economic and political life was shattered during the colonial rule; and they are now faced with the gigantic task of development, challenge of poverty and disruptive forces within, largely because of the exploitation of the masses by their ruling classes, sometimes in collusion with foreign powers. A large number of these nations have been subjected to military dictatorship and oppression.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.163 (printed 148)

| ✅ Burden | ⚠️ Source identified by the chapter |
|---|---|
| **The gigantic task of development** | ⚠️ Life **shattered during the colonial rule** |
| **Challenge of poverty** | ⚠️ Same |
| **Disruptive forces within** | ✅ **Largely because of the exploitation of the masses by their ruling classes, sometimes in collusion with foreign powers** |
| **Military dictatorship and oppression** | ⚠️ Reported as a fact about **a large number of these nations** |

🔑 The third row is the chapter's sharpest claim and the one most often omitted. ⚠️ It locates internal disruption primarily in **domestic** class exploitation, with foreign collusion as a secondary and intermittent factor. ✅ That is a more demanding diagnosis than blaming colonialism alone, and it matches the nation-building conditions of Lesson 21, where **widening economic disparities** came first and neo-colonial **foul play** second.

⚠️ Note the word **collusion**, which the chapter used earlier of rulers and priests in the Oriental empire. ✅ The same word, the same structure: domestic power sustained by an external or supernatural partner.

### 4. Why socialism did not abolish the nation-state

✅ A short argument with a large consequence:

> Marxian socialism, which brought about socialist systems in the world, advocated a world-wide organization of workers. Initially, it did not subscribe to the idea of confining workers' movements to national boundaries. However, harsh realities of human nature and practical necessity forced the people of the socialist states to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states. A large number of socialist states have now relinquished socialism and are now heading towards liberalization as independent nation-states. Similarly, the new nations of the Third World have also stabilized as nation-states.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.163 (printed 148)

```text
   MARXIAN SOCIALISM
     advocates a WORLD-WIDE ORGANIZATION OF WORKERS
     does NOT confine movements to national boundaries
                |
   "HARSH REALITIES OF HUMAN NATURE AND PRACTICAL NECESSITY"
                |
   socialist states ACCEPT and PERPETUATE their position
   AS NATION-STATES
                |
   many later RELINQUISH SOCIALISM, head towards
   LIBERALIZATION as INDEPENDENT NATION-STATES
                |
   the new nations of the Third World ALSO STABILIZE
   AS NATION-STATES
                |
   => the nation-state absorbs BOTH twentieth-century changes
```

⚠️ Two cautions. ⚠️ The explanatory phrase **harsh realities of human nature** is an appeal to an unargued general claim about human beings; ✅ report it as the chapter's wording and ❌ do not rest an argument on it. ⚠️ The companion phrase **practical necessity** is the defensible half and can carry the explanation on its own.

🔑 The conclusion is the important part: both the revolutionary and the anti-colonial routes end in the same form. ✅ That is why Lesson 32's question — whether the nation-state can still perform its role — is posed to the whole world and not to one bloc.

### 5. The growth of international organisation

✅ The last movement of the page:

> The stability of the nation-state system has led to the vast development of international law and international organizations (like the United Nations and its specialized agencies) to regulate the behaviour of nation-states, international transactions, to ensure collaboration in the development of science and technology, art, literature and culture as also to tackle global problems like prevention of atmospheric pollution, sharing of rare but essential resources, saving humanity from injustice, and so on.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.163 (printed 148)

| ✅ Purpose of international organisation | ⚠️ Type |
|---|---|
| **Regulate the behaviour of nation-states** | Order |
| **International transactions** | Economic |
| **Collaboration in the development of science and technology, art, literature and culture** | Cooperative |
| **Prevention of atmospheric pollution** | ⚠️ Transboundary — reappears at Lesson 33 |
| **Sharing of rare but essential resources** | ⚠️ Distributive |
| **Saving humanity from injustice** | ⚠️ Normative |

⚠️ The structural irony is the chapter's own, though unstated: **the stability of the nation-state system** is what produced the international institutions that Lesson 31 will say require states to accept **reasonable restraints on sovereignty**. ✅ Internationalism here is a consequence of the nation-state's success, not of its failure.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The claim that socialist states accepted nation-statehood because of **harsh realities of human nature** explains nothing. ⚠️ It substitutes an assertion about human beings in general for an account of the specific pressures — security competition, resource control, bureaucratic interest — that produced the outcome.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter supplies a second and better reason in the same clause: **practical necessity**. ⚠️ And its surrounding material specifies what that necessity consisted in, since the same section says the nation-state system generated international law, transactions and organisations that only states could enter. ✅ A movement that refused the state form would have had no standing in the order it had to operate within.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is a reconstruction, not the chapter's argument, and the human-nature clause remains unsupported on the page. ⚠️ It is also in tension with the chapter's own historical method elsewhere, which explains political forms by material and institutional conditions rather than by fixed human attributes. ⚠️ The inconsistency is recorded rather than resolved.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This page supplies the chapter's transition from history to contemporary politics and contains its only account of why the nation-state became universal.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Why did the nation-state become the universal political form in the twentieth century?* — two changes, one cause, and the convergence argument.
- ❌ **Trap.** Using **Third World** as a current description. ✅ Mark it as the chapter's book-period category.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the convergence line — socialist states and new nations both stabilising as nation-states — as your conclusion; it is analytical rather than narrative.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Political consciousness and national movements started emerging in the subject peoples in the twentieth century, especially after the First World War (1914-18).**
2. ✅ The common cause was **the political aspirations of the oppressed people of different countries**.
3. ✅ Change (a): **socialist revolutions inspired by Marxist ideology, first in Russia (1917), and later in China (1949) and other countries**.
4. ✅ Change (b): **attainment of independence by the peoples of Asia and Africa from their colonial rulers, especially after the Second World War (1939-45)**.
5. ✅ Named: **India, Pakistan, Burma, Egypt, Nigeria, Algeria, Ghana, Fiji, Vietnam, Indonesia, Libya, Syria, Zimbabwe and Namibia**.
6. ✅ These, **along with Latin American countries**, are **collectively described as the 'Third World' or the developing nations**.
7. ✅ Their life was **shattered during the colonial rule**; they face **the gigantic task of development, challenge of poverty and disruptive forces within**.
8. ✅ The internal disruption arises **largely because of the exploitation of the masses by their ruling classes, sometimes in collusion with foreign powers**.
9. ✅ **A large number of these nations have been subjected to military dictatorship and oppression.**
10. ✅ **Marxian socialism... advocated a world-wide organization of workers** and did not confine movements to national boundaries.
11. ✅ **Harsh realities of human nature and practical necessity forced the people of the socialist states to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states.**
12. ✅ Many socialist states **relinquished socialism** and headed **towards liberalization as independent nation-states**; the new Third World nations **also stabilized as nation-states**.
13. ✅ The stability of the system produced **vast development of international law and international organizations (like the United Nations and its specialized agencies)**.
14. ⚠️ The **human nature** clause is unargued; **practical necessity** carries the explanation.
15. 🔑 Memorise the convergence: **revolution and decolonisation both end in the nation-state**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter derives two opposite twentieth-century transformations from a single cause and says both ended in the same political form. Reconstruct that argument.

**Model answer:** ✅ The single cause is **the political aspirations of the oppressed people of different countries**, arising both among **subject peoples** under colonial rule and among those **suffering under despotic rule**. ✅ From it came **(a) socialist revolutions inspired by Marxist ideology, first in Russia (1917), and later in China (1949)**, and **(b) attainment of independence by the peoples of Asia and Africa... especially after the Second World War (1939-45)**. ⚠️ The two are ideologically opposite — one abolishing capitalism, the other ending alien rule. ✅ Yet both converge: although **Marxian socialism... advocated a world-wide organization of workers** and refused national confinement, **harsh realities of human nature and practical necessity forced the people of the socialist states to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states**; and **the new nations of the Third World have also stabilized as nation-states**. 🔑 The nation-state therefore absorbed both transformations, which is why **the stability of the nation-state system** could generate **vast development of international law and international organizations**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not attribute the internal disruption of new states solely to colonialism. ⚠️ The chapter's own wording places it **largely because of the exploitation of the masses by their ruling classes, sometimes in collusion with foreign powers** — a domestic diagnosis first, with external involvement as secondary and occasional.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss the twentieth-century changes that reshaped the nation-state system. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **The political aspirations of the oppressed people of different countries** produced **two important changes**: **socialist revolutions inspired by Marxist ideology, first in Russia (1917), and later in China (1949)**, and **attainment of independence by the peoples of Asia and Africa from their colonial rulers, especially after the Second World War (1939-45)** — **India, Pakistan, Burma, Egypt, Nigeria** and others, collectively **described as the 'Third World' or the developing nations**. ⚠️ Their inherited condition was severe: life **shattered during the colonial rule**, and **disruptive forces within, largely because of the exploitation of the masses by their ruling classes**. ✅ Although **Marxian socialism... advocated a world-wide organization of workers**, **practical necessity** forced socialist states **to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states**, and the new nations **also stabilized as nation-states**. ✅ That stability produced **vast development of international law and international organizations (like the United Nations...)**.

**Rubric [10]:** the single cause identified **1** · both lettered changes with Russia 1917 and China 1949 **2** · at least four named newly independent countries plus the Third World designation **2** · the internal-disruption diagnosis including the ruling-class clause **2** · the convergence of socialist states and new nations on the nation-state form **2** · the resulting growth of international law and organisations **1**.

## Lesson 28 — Neo-colonialism, and the forms exploitation now takes

Progress: 28 / 45 | Stage: Core | Part VII — the modern nation-state

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §12, read against the boxed definition of neo-colonialism and the continuation paragraph (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.163-164 = printed 148-149), which supply the four mechanisms of continuing exploitation and the terms-of-trade argument.
CA search: "neo-colonialism unequal exchange foreign aid brain drain developing nations 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned contemporary development-finance material that would require independent verification and that the chapter does not address; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The definition, set against colonialism

✅ **Neo-Colonialism — The practice under which an advanced nation does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory, but taking advantage of its superior position in organization of trade and industry, uses the resources of a developing nation as a source of cheap labour and raw materials as well as a big market of its own products. It is a subtle method of economic exploitation of developing nations by the developed nations.**

| Axis | ✅ Colonialism (L26 box) | ✅ Neo-colonialism (this box) |
|---|---|---|
| Political domination | ✅ Maintained; **establishes its own administration** | ❌ **Does not maintain its political domination** |
| Source of leverage | ⚠️ Conquest and administration | ✅ **Superior position in organization of trade and industry** |
| What is used | **Natural and human resources** | ✅ **Cheap labour and raw materials**, plus **a big market of its own products** |
| Character | ⚠️ Overt | ✅ **A subtle method of economic exploitation** |

🔑 The two boxes differ on exactly one axis — political administration — and agree on the economic content. ⚠️ That is the chapter's whole thesis in this lesson: independence changed the form of control, not its substance.

⚠️ Note also that the neo-colonial list reproduces Lesson 26's three requirements almost word for word: **cheap labour**, **raw materials**, **a big market**. ✅ The industrial-merchant class's needs of the nineteenth century are the developed nations' needs of the twentieth.

### 2. The four mechanisms

✅ The continuation paragraph names four distinct channels:

> However, exploitation in the international sphere continues in many new, subtle forms, such as neo-colonialism. The new nations require machinery and know-how from advanced nations to build up their own industrial-technological base. They get these things at the expense of their rare and valuable resources, and thus they are increasingly impoverished. The pattern of import-export of developing nations reveals how cheap they sell their own material and labour involved in their products, and how expensively they acquire the material and labour involved in the products of foreign nations! Even foreign aid to developing nations has become a source of their exploitation. Besides, large numbers of talented persons from the developing nations, highly educated and trained in their own countries at huge public expense, migrate every year to serve the advanced nations!
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.164 (printed 149)

```text
   MECHANISM 1 — TECHNOLOGY DEPENDENCE
     new nations REQUIRE machinery and know-how
     they get these AT THE EXPENSE OF THEIR RARE AND
     VALUABLE RESOURCES
     => "thus they are increasingly impoverished"
            |
   MECHANISM 2 — UNEQUAL TERMS OF TRADE
     they SELL CHEAP the material and labour in their products
     they ACQUIRE EXPENSIVELY the material and labour in
     foreign products
            |
   MECHANISM 3 — AID
     "Even foreign aid to developing nations has become
      a source of their exploitation."
            |
   MECHANISM 4 — MIGRATION OF THE TRAINED
     large numbers of talented persons, HIGHLY EDUCATED AND
     TRAINED IN THEIR OWN COUNTRIES AT HUGE PUBLIC EXPENSE,
     MIGRATE EVERY YEAR to serve the advanced nations
```

⚠️ Mechanism 2 is the chapter's most precise claim and deserves unpacking. ✅ The comparison is not between goods but between **the material and labour involved** in goods: the same quantum of labour fetches less when embodied in a developing nation's exports than when embodied in a developed nation's. ✅ That is a terms-of-trade argument stated in labour terms. ⚠️ The chapter does not name or cite any theory for it, and ❌ no theory should be attributed to the chapter here.

⚠️ Mechanism 4 is worth stating carefully. ✅ The chapter's grievance is specifically fiscal — training was paid for **at huge public expense** and the return accrues elsewhere. ❌ It is not an argument against migration as such, and should not be reported as one.

⚠️ The exclamation marks after mechanisms 2 and 4 are the chapter's own and mark these as the passages where its voice is most committed.

### 3. The tension with Lesson 5, and how to handle it

⚠️ At Lesson 5 the chapter wrote that **the possibility of foreign aid, as a temporary measure, cannot be ruled out**. ⚠️ Here it writes that **even foreign aid to developing nations has become a source of their exploitation**.

| ✅ p.150 | ✅ p.164 | ⚠️ Reconciliation |
|---|---|---|
| Aid as a **temporary measure** **cannot be ruled out** | Aid **has become a source of their exploitation** | ⚠️ The chapter's own qualifier is **temporary**; aid that persists ceases to be a stopgap and becomes a relation of dependence |

🔑 Pointing this out is a genuine use of the text rather than a summary of it. ⚠️ The chapter does not perform the reconciliation itself, so present it as a reading. ✅ What the chapter does supply is the standard by which the two statements can be judged: at Lesson 5, **economic self-sufficiency is essential for the stability of a state**.

### 4. The closing judgment

✅ **These are highly complex problems which need to be sorted out and tackled in order to save humanity from mounting exploitation and injustice.**

⚠️ Note the scale of the appeal: not "to help developing nations" but **to save humanity**. ✅ That universal framing is the chapter deliberately preparing the internationalist argument of Lesson 31, where **in the case of a clash between national interest and universal human interest, human interest should be given precedence**. ✅ The neo-colonialism paragraph is therefore not a digression but the empirical premise of the internationalist case.

### 5. Comparison: three stages of one relationship

| Stage | ✅ Political form | ✅ Economic content | ✅ Chapter's term |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nineteenth century | ⚠️ Direct administration by the colonial power | **Raw material, cheap labour, new markets** | **Colonialism** |
| After independence | ✅ Formal sovereignty of the new state | **Cheap labour and raw materials**, **a big market** | **Neo-colonialism** |
| Consequence | ⚠️ Statehood without self-sufficiency | ⚠️ **Increasingly impoverished**; nation-building **bound to remain elusive** | ⚠️ The chapter's standing diagnosis |

⚠️ Read the third row against Lesson 21: nation-building fails unless the new states **free themselves completely from the foul play of neo-colonial powers**. ✅ The two passages are a single argument, and quoting both shows that the chapter's treatment of nationhood and its treatment of economics are connected rather than parallel.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The four mechanisms are asserted rather than demonstrated. ⚠️ Technology transfer, trade and aid are voluntary transactions that developing states enter because they expect to gain; describing every such transaction as exploitation makes the charge unfalsifiable. ⚠️ And the migration of trained professionals may return value through remittances, networks and later investment.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's claim is not that any exchange is exploitative but that these particular exchanges run systematically in one direction, and it offers an observable test: **the pattern of import-export of developing nations**, which shows an asymmetry in the price of embodied **material and labour**. ✅ Nor is voluntariness decisive, since the chapter's point is about the **superior position in organization of trade and industry** that sets the terms within which the choice is made.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply states the test without supplying the evidence, and the chapter gives no figures, sources or counter-examples. ⚠️ The remittance point is not addressed at all. ⚠️ Treat the four mechanisms as the chapter's analytical categories, ❌ not as demonstrated findings, and say so in an answer.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The two boxes together are the clearest statement in the topic of the difference between political and economic domination.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Does political independence end domination?* — the neo-colonialism box plus the four mechanisms.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating neo-colonialism as a synonym for globalisation. ✅ The chapter's definition is specific: no political domination, advantage from **superior position in organization of trade and industry**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Set the two boxes side by side and identify the single axis of difference; it is the most efficient comparison available.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Neo-colonialism**: an advanced nation **does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory**.
2. ✅ It takes advantage of its **superior position in organization of trade and industry**.
3. ✅ It uses a developing nation as a source of **cheap labour and raw materials** and as **a big market of its own products**.
4. ✅ It is **a subtle method of economic exploitation of developing nations by the developed nations**.
5. ✅ Mechanism 1: new nations **require machinery and know-how**, obtained **at the expense of their rare and valuable resources**, and so are **increasingly impoverished**.
6. ✅ Mechanism 2: **the pattern of import-export** shows how **cheap they sell their own material and labour** and **how expensively they acquire** foreign material and labour.
7. ✅ Mechanism 3: **even foreign aid to developing nations has become a source of their exploitation**.
8. ✅ Mechanism 4: **large numbers of talented persons... highly educated and trained in their own countries at huge public expense, migrate every year to serve the advanced nations**.
9. ✅ These are **highly complex problems which need to be sorted out and tackled in order to save humanity from mounting exploitation and injustice**.
10. ⚠️ Colonialism and neo-colonialism differ on **one** axis — political administration — and agree on economic content.
11. ⚠️ Aid is permitted at p.150 as a **temporary measure** and condemned at p.164 once it persists.
12. ⚠️ Lesson 21's condition — freeing themselves from **the foul play of neo-colonial powers** — is this lesson's content.
13. ⚠️ The chapter supplies categories, ❌ not evidence; report them as such.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the four mechanisms: **Technology · Terms of trade · Aid · Talent**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Compare the chapter's definitions of colonialism and neo-colonialism, and state precisely what changes and what does not.

**Model answer:** ✅ **Colonialism** is the practice under which a nation-state, **after consolidating its national power, extends its domination over territories and the people of other countries for exploiting their natural and human resources**, and in which **the colonial power establishes its own administration in the subject country**. ✅ **Neo-colonialism** is the practice under which **an advanced nation does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory, but taking advantage of its superior position in organization of trade and industry, uses the resources of a developing nation as a source of cheap labour and raw materials as well as a big market of its own products**. ⚠️ What changes is the political form: direct administration disappears and formal sovereignty is real. ⚠️ What does not change is the economic content — **cheap labour**, **raw materials** and **a big market** reproduce the nineteenth-century triad of **raw material, cheap labour and new markets**. ✅ The chapter marks the difference in manner as well: neo-colonialism is **a subtle method of economic exploitation**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the chapter's two statements on foreign aid as a contradiction to be ignored. ⚠️ At p.150 aid is admissible **as a temporary measure**; at p.164 it **has become a source of their exploitation**. ⚠️ The qualifier **temporary** is the chapter's own and supplies the reading: aid that becomes structural ceases to be a stopgap.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine neo-colonialism as a form of continuing exploitation. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Under neo-colonialism **an advanced nation does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory**, but exploits its **superior position in organization of trade and industry** to use a developing nation as a source of **cheap labour and raw materials** and **a big market of its own products** — **a subtle method of economic exploitation**. ✅ Four mechanisms: new nations **require machinery and know-how**, obtained **at the expense of their rare and valuable resources**, becoming **increasingly impoverished**. ✅ **The pattern of import-export** shows how **cheap they sell their own material and labour** and how **expensively they acquire** foreign products. ✅ **Even foreign aid... has become a source of their exploitation.** ✅ And **talented persons... trained in their own countries at huge public expense, migrate every year to serve the advanced nations**. ⚠️ Political independence changes the form of domination, not its economic substance.

**Rubric [10]:** the definition with the no-political-domination clause and the trade-and-industry advantage **3** · all four mechanisms **4** · the increasingly-impoverished or save-humanity conclusion **1** · an explicit comparison with colonialism on the administration axis **2**.

## Lesson 29 — Nationalism: sentiment, ideology and invented symbols

Progress: 29 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part VIII — nationalism and internationalism

### 1. ⚠️ A disclosure before the teaching

⚠️ ⚠️ **This lesson stands at one of the chapter's two unnumbered overflow leaves, and you need to know where it falls before you read the doctrine.**

✅ The nationalism passage begins on PDF p.164 (printed 149) and continues onto PDF p.165. ⚠️ PDF p.165 carries **no folio**, and the whole of its text is a single narrow column — about thirty points wide — set in an otherwise blank page:

> the struggle of independence. It is called 'national movement'. In short, nationalism pays highest importance to one's loyalty and commitment to one's nation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.165, ✅ the leaf's entire text

✅ The leaf carries nothing else. ✅ Continuity is verified on both sides: PDF p.164 ends **it can organize itself for**, this leaf completes that sentence, and PDF p.166 (printed 150) opens a new one — so ✅ no chapter text is missing here. ❌ **Nothing is reconstructed, paraphrased from memory, inferred from the surrounding pages or supplied from any other source.** ⚠️ The pagination consequence is that the offset changes from 15 to 16 after this leaf, which is recorded in the Source Ledger.

### 2. The frame: two conflicting ideologies

✅ **The concept of nation-state is the focus of two conflicting ideologies, viz. nationalism and internationalism.**

```text
            THE NATION-STATE
                  |
      +-----------+-----------+
      |                       |
  NATIONALISM            INTERNATIONALISM
  (this lesson)          (Lessons 30-31)
```

⚠️ Note the word **focus**. ✅ The nation-state is not the property of either ideology; it is the object both are arguing about. ⚠️ That framing is why the chapter can later say the nation-state is strained without saying it is finished.

### 3. Nationalism as sentiment and as ideology

✅ The chapter's basic division:

| ✅ As a **sentiment** | ✅ As an **ideology** |
|---|---|
| **It involves an individual's attachment to his nation** | **It postulates that the structure of a state or its political organization should be founded on nationhood** |
| **A nationalist in this sense accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests** | ⚠️ Two postulates follow — see below |

✅ The two postulates, in the chapter's own words:

> In other words, each nation should constitute an independent state; and each state should represent a particular nation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.164 (printed 149)

```text
   POSTULATE 1: each NATION should constitute an
                INDEPENDENT STATE
   POSTULATE 2: each STATE should represent a
                PARTICULAR NATION
   => the two together = STRICT CONGRUENCE of nation and state
```

🔑 These are two different claims and a complete answer separates them. ⚠️ Postulate 1 is a claim about nations: none should be stateless. ⚠️ Postulate 2 is a claim about states: none should be multi-national. ✅ Lesson 32's examples break the first — the **Kurds** — and the second — **Lebanon**, **Cyprus** and others — and an answer that maps example to postulate is doing precise work.

⚠️ Notice also the tension with Lesson 20. ✅ There the chapter defined the nation as including people who **may belong to different nationalities**. ⚠️ Here nationalism demands that each state **represent a particular nation**. ✅ The two are compatible only on the chapter's own wide definition of nation — which is exactly why it rejected the narrow, nationality-based definition at Lesson 19.

### 4. The three entitlement claims

✅ Nationalism as ideology also makes three claims about how nations should be treated:

| # | ✅ Claim |
|---|---|
| 1 | People who identify themselves as a natural community and claim to be members of a nation **must live under a political system of their own choice** |
| 2 | **They should enjoy an equal status with other nations in the world community** |
| 3 | **No nation should be kept under the domination or supremacy of any other nation** |

⚠️ These are the content of self-determination from Lesson 21 restated as an ideological demand. ✅ Claim 1 matches the box's second limb — **to choose a suitable form of government for itself**; claims 2 and 3 match the **equality among nations** that Lesson 20 derived as a **logical outcome** of the idea of the nation.

🔑 The structural point: nationalism, on this account, is not inherently aggressive. ⚠️ Claims 2 and 3 are egalitarian, and it is the *combination* of strict congruence with the facts of mixed populations that generates conflict — not the entitlement claims themselves.

### 5. The invention of nationhood

✅ The chapter's most striking paragraph:

> If the members of a community regard themselves as a nation, they can create or invent some elements of nationhood in order to strengthen their national sentiment. For example, they can evolve their 'national language', adopt a 'national flag', 'national anthem' and other symbols of national identity like national tree, national flower, national fruit, national bird, national animal, national river, etc., declare national festivals, build memorials of 'national heroes' before whom the entire nation bows its head; and they can inspire the members of the nation through popular songs, paintings and statues depicting heroic episodes of their national history.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.164 (printed 149)

```text
   IF a community REGARDS ITSELF as a nation
        |
   it can CREATE OR INVENT elements of nationhood
        |
   +-- language:   'NATIONAL LANGUAGE'
   +-- insignia:   'NATIONAL FLAG' · 'NATIONAL ANTHEM'
   +-- nature:     national TREE · FLOWER · FRUIT ·
   |               BIRD · ANIMAL · RIVER
   +-- calendar:   NATIONAL FESTIVALS
   +-- persons:    memorials of 'NATIONAL HEROES' before
   |               whom the entire nation bows its head
   +-- arts:       popular SONGS, PAINTINGS and STATUES
                   depicting HEROIC EPISODES of national history
```

⚠️ Three analytical observations, and all three are examinable.

- ⚠️ **The order of causation is reversed.** The community **regards itself as a nation** *first*, and *then* creates the elements of nationhood. ✅ Symbols do not produce the nation; they **strengthen their national sentiment**.
- ⚠️ **The verbs are deliberate.** **Create or invent** is the chapter's own phrasing, and **invent** is a strong word to apply to **national heroes** and **heroic episodes of their national history**. ✅ The chapter uses it without embarrassment and without criticism.
- ⚠️ **It sits in tension with the definition of the nation.** ✅ Lesson 20 defined the nation partly by **common history**; here part of that history may be invented. ⚠️ The chapter does not address the tension, and an honest answer names it rather than smoothing it.

🔑 Answer line: the chapter holds that nationhood is felt first and furnished afterwards — which makes national symbols evidence of a nation's self-assertion rather than proof of its antiquity.

⚠️ ❌ No contemporary instance of national symbolism is named in this lesson. ✅ The chapter's own list is generic, and this file keeps it so.

### 6. The national movement, and where the text fails

✅ The last complete sentence on PDF p.164:

> If a community has evolved national consciousness but its territory is occupied by a colonial or imperial power, it can organize itself for
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.164 (printed 149)

⚠️ The sentence breaks at the page turn. ✅ The text of PDF p.165 completes it: **the struggle of independence. It is called 'national movement'.** ✅ And it supplies the section's closing formula: **In short, nationalism pays highest importance to one's loyalty and commitment to one's nation.**

```text
   PAGE TURN             OVERFLOW LEAF             NEXT NUMBERED PAGE
   -------------------   -----------------------   ---------------------
   p.164 (printed 149)   p.165 (no folio)          p.166 (printed 150)

   "...it can organize   "the struggle of          "On the other hand,
    itself for"     -->    independence. It is -->   internationalism
                           called 'national          does not regard..."
                           movement'.
                           In short, nationalism    NEW SENTENCE,
                           pays highest importance  NOTHING MISSING
                           to one's loyalty and
                           commitment to one's
                           nation."
```

✅ The leaf carries no other text, so there is nothing further on it to teach. ❌ Nothing is guessed at, described or supplied from elsewhere.

✅ Two things can nevertheless be said safely. ✅ The chapter supplies a definition of the **national movement**: the self-organisation of a community with **national consciousness** whose **territory is occupied by a colonial or imperial power**, for **the struggle of independence**. ✅ And the closing formula — **highest importance to one's loyalty and commitment to one's nation** — restates the sentiment limb and so closes the section where it opened.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter's nationalism is too benign. ⚠️ It lists egalitarian entitlement claims and generic symbols, records that nationhood may be invented without criticising the invention, and nowhere confronts nationalism's exclusionary and aggressive potential — which it had itself attributed to **nationality** at Lesson 19.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter does carry the critical material, but places it elsewhere. ✅ At Lesson 19 feelings of nationality **separate one set of people from other such sets**, **sometimes** with **a sense of one's own superiority, or a sense of disdain for others**, leading to **tensions, wars and other disastrous consequences**. ✅ At Lesson 18 the idealist elevation of **especially the nation-state** as **the march of God on earth** is explicitly condemned. ⚠️ So the critique exists in the chapter; it is distributed rather than absent.

⚠️ **Residual.** Distribution is not the same as integration. ⚠️ The chapter never brings the nationality critique to bear on nationalism, and p.165 carries only the two sentences quoted above, so it does not do so there either. ⚠️ The honest position is that this page presents nationalism's claims and that the chapter's critical resources for assessing them lie in other sections.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The sentiment/ideology distinction and the two postulates are the core of any nationalism question.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *What is nationalism?* — sentiment, ideology, two postulates, three entitlement claims, invented symbols.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating nationalism and nationality as the same thing. ✅ Nationality is a basis of unity; nationalism is an ideology about how states should be constituted.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the invention paragraph to show that national identity is actively constructed; then qualify it with the chapter's own ordering — the self-identification comes first.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The concept of nation-state is the focus of two conflicting ideologies, viz. nationalism and internationalism.**
2. ✅ **Nationalism denotes a sentiment as well as an ideology.**
3. ✅ As a sentiment it **involves an individual's attachment to his nation**, and a nationalist **accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests**.
4. ✅ As an ideology it **postulates that the structure of a state or its political organization should be founded on nationhood**.
5. ✅ Postulate 1: **each nation should constitute an independent state**.
6. ✅ Postulate 2: **each state should represent a particular nation**.
7. ✅ Members of such a community **must live under a political system of their own choice**.
8. ✅ **They should enjoy an equal status with other nations in the world community.**
9. ✅ **No nation should be kept under the domination or supremacy of any other nation.**
10. ✅ A community regarding itself as a nation **can create or invent some elements of nationhood in order to strengthen their national sentiment**.
11. ✅ The examples include **'national language'**, **'national flag'**, **'national anthem'**, **national tree, flower, fruit, bird, animal, river**, **national festivals**, memorials of **'national heroes'**, and **popular songs, paintings and statues depicting heroic episodes of their national history**.
12. ⚠️ Self-identification comes first; the symbols **strengthen** a sentiment that already exists.
13. ✅ A community with national consciousness whose **territory is occupied by a colonial or imperial power** can organise **the struggle of independence**, which **is called 'national movement'**.
14. ✅ **In short, nationalism pays highest importance to one's loyalty and commitment to one's nation.**
15. ⚠️ PDF p.165 is an unnumbered overflow leaf carrying only the text quoted above; ✅ continuity with pp.164 and 166 is verified and ❌ nothing is reconstructed.

### Concept check

**Question:** Nationalism as an ideology makes two postulates. State them separately and explain why keeping them separate matters.

**Model answer:** ✅ Nationalism **postulates that the structure of a state or its political organization should be founded on nationhood**, and this resolves into two claims: **each nation should constitute an independent state**, and **each state should represent a particular nation**. ⚠️ They are different in subject and in what would falsify them. ⚠️ The first is a claim about nations and fails wherever a nation has no state of its own; the chapter's own instance is that **the Kurds are scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey although they recognize themselves as one nation**. ⚠️ The second is a claim about states and fails wherever a state contains peoples who **have not been able to assimilate themselves into one nation**, as with **Lebanon** and **Cyprus**. ⚠️ Keeping them separate matters because a single state can satisfy one and violate the other, so a candidate who runs them together cannot explain which postulate a given case refutes. 🔑 Together they demand strict congruence of nation and state.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read the chapter as saying that national symbols create nations. ⚠️ Its order is the reverse: **if the members of a community regard themselves as a nation, they can create or invent some elements of nationhood in order to strengthen their national sentiment** — the self-identification is prior, and the symbols reinforce it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss nationalism as a sentiment and as an ideology. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Nationalism denotes a sentiment as well as an ideology.** ✅ As a sentiment it **involves an individual's attachment to his nation**; the nationalist **accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests**. ✅ As an ideology it **postulates that the structure of a state... should be founded on nationhood**, in two demands: **each nation should constitute an independent state; and each state should represent a particular nation**. ✅ Such communities **must live under a political system of their own choice** and **enjoy an equal status with other nations**, with **no nation... under the domination or supremacy of any other nation**. ✅ Nationhood may be furnished: a community **can create or invent some elements of nationhood** — **'national language'**, **'national flag'**, **'national anthem'**, memorials of **'national heroes'** — to **strengthen their national sentiment**. ✅ **In short, nationalism pays highest importance to one's loyalty and commitment to one's nation.**

**Rubric [10]:** the sentiment limb with the primacy clause **2** · the ideology limb with both postulates stated separately **3** · at least two of the three entitlement claims **2** · the create-or-invent paragraph with at least three symbol types **2** · the closing formula on loyalty and commitment **1**.

## Lesson 30 — Internationalism I: international communism

Progress: 30 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part VIII — nationalism and internationalism

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §6, with the Advanced companion's §6, read against the opening of the internationalism passage (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 = printed 150), which supplies the two streams, the rejection of the nation-state as centre and the Communist Manifesto material.
CA search: "international communism workers of the world unite Communist Manifesto 1848 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current item bears on the chapter's exposition of a nineteenth-century doctrine. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What internationalism denies

✅ The chapter defines internationalism negatively before defining it positively:

> On the other hand, internationalism does not regard nation-state as the centre of political organization, nor it accepts nation as the object of individual's undivided loyalty. It insists that mankind should evolve larger organizations in order to achieve some great objectives.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

```text
   INTERNATIONALISM DENIES TWO THINGS
     (1) that the NATION-STATE is the CENTRE of
         political organization
     (2) that the NATION is the object of the
         individual's UNDIVIDED LOYALTY
                 |
   AND ASSERTS ONE
     mankind should evolve LARGER ORGANIZATIONS
     in order to achieve SOME GREAT OBJECTIVES
```

⚠️ Denial (2) is the direct negation of Lesson 20's definition of the nation, where citizens owe **their undivided allegiance to that state**. ✅ The chapter has constructed a genuine opposition: the same phrase is affirmed in one section and denied in another, and the clash is the substance of this Part.

### 2. The two streams

✅ **Broadly speaking, we may discern two streams of internationalism: (a) international communism; and (b) a principle of international organization.**

| ✅ Stream | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|
| **(a) International communism** | This lesson |
| **(b) A principle of international organization** | Lesson 31 |

⚠️ The lettering is the chapter's own. ✅ Keeping the two apart is essential, because they have different grounds, different agents and different relationships to sovereignty.

### 3. International communism

✅ The chapter's exposition:

> International communism is associated with the teachings of Marx (1818-83) and Engels (1820-95). In *Communist Manifesto* (1848) they observed that workers have no country of their own. They exhorted workers of the world to unite to bring about socialist revolution all over the world. In their view, socialist revolution was necessary for the emancipation of humanity. This could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

| ✅ Element | ✅ Detail |
|---|---|
| Authors | **Marx (1818-83)** and **Engels (1820-95)** |
| Work and year | ***Communist Manifesto* (1848)** |
| Premise | **Workers have no country of their own** |
| Exhortation | **Workers of the world to unite** |
| Objective | **To bring about socialist revolution all over the world** |
| Justification | **Socialist revolution was necessary for the emancipation of humanity** |
| Scope conclusion | **This could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation** |

```text
   WORKERS HAVE NO COUNTRY OF THEIR OWN
              |  (premise)
   the unit of political identity is CLASS, not NATION
              |
   WORKERS OF THE WORLD, UNITE
              |
   SOCIALIST REVOLUTION ALL OVER THE WORLD
              |
   necessary for THE EMANCIPATION OF HUMANITY
              |
   therefore CANNOT BE CONFINED to the boundaries
   of a particular nation
```

🔑 The argument is tight and should be reproduced as an argument. ⚠️ The premise is sociological — a claim about what workers actually have. ⚠️ The conclusion is strategic — a claim about where the revolution must occur. ⚠️ The bridge is normative — the revolution serves **the emancipation of humanity**, and humanity is not national.

⚠️ Note that this is the chapter's second use of **the emancipation of humanity** style of reasoning. ✅ The first was at Lesson 28, where neo-colonial exploitation had to be tackled **in order to save humanity from mounting exploitation and injustice**. ✅ Both arguments move from a universal beneficiary to a supra-national remedy, and that shared structure is what makes the two streams of internationalism recognisably one family.

### 4. The outcome the chapter has already recorded

⚠️ This lesson must be read against Lesson 27, because the chapter has already told us how international communism fared:

| ✅ Doctrine (p.166) | ✅ Outcome (p.163) |
|---|---|
| **Workers have no country of their own** | ⚠️ **Harsh realities of human nature and practical necessity forced the people of the socialist states to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states** |
| **Socialist revolution all over the world** | ⚠️ Revolutions occurred **first in Russia (1917), and later in China (1949) and other countries** — ⚠️ in states, not across them |
| **Could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation** | ⚠️ **A large number of socialist states have now relinquished socialism and are now heading towards liberalization as independent nation-states** |

🔑 Setting the two pages side by side is the single strongest move available on this lesson. ✅ The chapter presents a doctrine that denies the nation-state and then reports that its adherents became nation-states — and it does so without comment. ⚠️ Making that juxtaposition explicit is analysis the chapter leaves to the reader.

### 5. Comparison: the two streams, previewed

| Axis | ✅ (a) International communism | ✅ (b) International organisation (L31) |
|---|---|---|
| Agent | **Workers of the world** | ⚠️ **Peoples of different nation-states** |
| Ground | ⚠️ Class position — **workers have no country** | ⚠️ Interdependence — the nation-state is **no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members** |
| Method | **Socialist revolution all over the world** | ⚠️ **Larger international organizations** and **cooperation** |
| Attitude to the state | ⚠️ Transcended by revolution | ⚠️ Retained, but accepting **reasonable restraints on its sovereignty** |
| Beneficiary | **The emancipation of humanity** | ⚠️ **The common interest of mankind** |

⚠️ The decisive difference is in the fourth row. ✅ Stream (a) is revolutionary and dispenses with the nation-state; ✅ stream (b) is reformist and keeps it under restraint. ⚠️ Only stream (b) survives into the chapter's own conclusions at Lessons 33 and 34.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter's exposition of international communism is bare. ⚠️ It reports a premise, an exhortation and a scope claim, without any of the argument that connects them — no account of why class position should override national attachment, and no engagement with the historical fact that workers have repeatedly fought national wars against one another.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's purpose here is classificatory: it is identifying one of **two streams** of internationalism so that stream (b) can be distinguished from it. ✅ The substantive doctrine is routed elsewhere, as the chapter's own structure implies — ➡️ **Topic 04** owns historical materialism and the class analysis, and ➡️ **Topic 05** owns the socialist tradition. ⚠️ Within this chapter, what is needed is the *shape* of the position, and that is supplied.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply explains the brevity and not the silence. ⚠️ The chapter records at Lesson 27 that socialist states became nation-states, and nowhere asks what that implies for the doctrine. ⚠️ Its only offered explanation — **harsh realities of human nature** — is precisely the kind of unargued appeal the doctrine would reject. ⚠️ That gap is real and should be stated.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The two-stream division is the chapter's own and gives any internationalism answer its structure.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *What is internationalism?* — the two denials, the one assertion, then the two streams.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating internationalism as a single position. ✅ The chapter explicitly discerns **two streams**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Close by noting that the chapter's own narrative records stream (a)'s adherents stabilising as nation-states; it is a textual observation, not an external criticism.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Internationalism **does not regard nation-state as the centre of political organization**.
2. ✅ Nor does it accept **nation as the object of individual's undivided loyalty**.
3. ✅ It insists that **mankind should evolve larger organizations in order to achieve some great objectives**.
4. ✅ There are **two streams**: **(a) international communism**; **(b) a principle of international organization**.
5. ✅ International communism is **associated with the teachings of Marx (1818-83) and Engels (1820-95)**.
6. ✅ In the ***Communist Manifesto* (1848)** they observed that **workers have no country of their own**.
7. ✅ They **exhorted workers of the world to unite to bring about socialist revolution all over the world**.
8. ✅ **Socialist revolution was necessary for the emancipation of humanity.**
9. ✅ It **could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation**.
10. ⚠️ Denial (2) directly negates the **undivided allegiance** of Lesson 20's definition of the nation.
11. ⚠️ The chapter has already recorded at Lesson 27 that socialist states became and remained nation-states.
12. ⚠️ Stream (a) transcends the state; stream (b) restrains it.
13. 🔑 Memorise the argument: **no country → unite → world revolution → emancipation of humanity → not confinable to one nation**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconstruct the argument of international communism as the chapter presents it, identifying the kind of claim each step makes.

**Model answer:** ✅ The premise is sociological: in the ***Communist Manifesto* (1848)**, **Marx (1818-83)** and **Engels (1820-95)** observed that **workers have no country of their own** — a claim about what workers actually possess, implying that their political identity rests on class rather than nation. ✅ The exhortation is strategic: they **exhorted workers of the world to unite to bring about socialist revolution all over the world**. ✅ The justification is normative: **socialist revolution was necessary for the emancipation of humanity**. ✅ The conclusion is a scope claim: it **could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation**, because its beneficiary is humanity, which is not national. ⚠️ The argument therefore moves from a fact about workers, through a universal beneficiary, to a supra-national method. 🔑 It is the same structure the chapter uses at p.164 about neo-colonialism, where problems must be tackled **in order to save humanity from mounting exploitation and injustice**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat internationalism as a single doctrine. ⚠️ The chapter explicitly discerns **two streams** — **international communism** and **a principle of international organization** — and they differ on the decisive point: the first dispenses with the nation-state, while the second retains it subject to **reasonable restraints on its sovereignty**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine international communism as a form of internationalism. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Internationalism **does not regard nation-state as the centre of political organization, nor... accepts nation as the object of individual's undivided loyalty**, insisting instead that **mankind should evolve larger organizations**. ✅ Of its **two streams**, the first is **international communism**, **associated with the teachings of Marx (1818-83) and Engels (1820-95)**. ✅ In the ***Communist Manifesto* (1848)** they observed that **workers have no country of their own**, and **exhorted workers of the world to unite to bring about socialist revolution all over the world**, since **socialist revolution was necessary for the emancipation of humanity** and **could not be confined to the boundaries of a particular nation**. ⚠️ Its difficulty is recorded by the chapter itself: **practical necessity forced the people of the socialist states to accept and perpetuate their position as nation-states**, and many later **relinquished socialism**. ⚠️ The doctrine denied the nation-state; its adherents became nation-states.

**Rubric [10]:** both denials and the one assertion of internationalism **2** · the two streams named **1** · Marx and Engels with dates and the 1848 work **2** · the no-country premise and the unite exhortation **2** · the emancipation-of-humanity justification and the non-confinement conclusion **2** · the chapter's own record of the outcome **1**.

## Lesson 31 — Internationalism II: the principle of international organisation

Progress: 31 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part VIII — nationalism and internationalism

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §6, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150), which supplies the interdependence premise, the precedence rule, the repudiation of 'my country, right or wrong', the restraints clause, the earlier-version contrast and the three named global evils.
CA search: "international cooperation environmental pollution terrorism drug trafficking global dimensions 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ the single dated anchor in this file is placed at Lesson 39; ❌ no second linkage is carried, and nothing current is recorded here.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The premise: a capacity claim

✅ The second stream rests on a factual premise rather than a class one:

> As a principle of international politics internationalism implies that in view of the growing contact between different parts of the world, a nation-state is no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members, not to speak of mankind as such.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

```text
   GROWING CONTACT between different parts of the world
            |
   a nation-state is NO LONGER CAPABLE of fulfilling
   ALL THE NEEDS OF ITS MEMBERS
            |
   ... NOT TO SPEAK OF MANKIND AS SUCH
            |
   therefore: peoples should FORM LARGER INTERNATIONAL
   ORGANIZATIONS and COOPERATE to serve THE COMMON
   INTEREST OF MANKIND
```

⚠️ Two steps, in ascending order. ✅ The nation-state fails *its own members* first; ✅ only then does the argument extend to **mankind as such**. 🔑 That ordering matters because it makes internationalism an argument from the nation-state's own purposes rather than from cosmopolitan sentiment — the state cannot do what it exists to do.

⚠️ Compare with Lesson 8: government exists for **the provision of common services**. ⚠️ If some needs now exceed the state's reach, then the element of government is partly disabled by scale. ✅ Lessons 33 and 34 supply the specific needs — security, environment, communication.

### 2. The precedence rule, and what it repudiates

✅ The normative core:

> In the case of a clash between national interest and universal human interest, human interest should be given precedence. In this sense, internationalism repudiates the idea of 'my country, right or wrong'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

| ✅ Rule | ⚠️ What it is directed against |
|---|---|
| **In the case of a clash... human interest should be given precedence** | ⚠️ Nationalism's sentiment limb, where the nationalist **accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests** |
| ✅ It **repudiates the idea of 'my country, right or wrong'** | ⚠️ Unconditional patriotic loyalty |

🔑 The two ideologies collide on exactly one word: **primacy**. ✅ Lesson 29's nationalist **accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests**; the internationalist gives precedence to **universal human interest**. ⚠️ A complete answer quotes both formulations and states that the disagreement is about ordering, not about whether national interest matters.

⚠️ Note the conditional **in the case of a clash**. ✅ Internationalism, on this account, does not deny national interest; it ranks it. ❌ Presenting it as hostility to one's own country misreports the chapter.

### 3. The restraints clause

✅ The institutional demand:

> It insists that each nation-state should accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty in the interests of world peace, collective security and observance of international law.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **Each nation-state should accept** | ⚠️ Acceptance, not imposition — consistent with sovereignty as derived authority |
| **Reasonable restraints** | ⚠️ **Reasonable** is undefined by the chapter |
| Three purposes | ✅ **World peace**, **collective security**, **observance of international law** |

⚠️ The word **accept** is doing real work and resolves an apparent conflict with Lesson 9. ✅ Sovereignty is **final authority of the state over its population and its territory**; a restraint the state itself accepts is an exercise of that authority, not a denial of it. 🔑 Internationalism in this stream is therefore *compatible* with sovereignty, which is precisely why it, and not stream (a), survives into the chapter's conclusions.

⚠️ The undefined **reasonable** is the weak point. ⚠️ The chapter supplies no criterion for distinguishing a reasonable restraint from an unreasonable one, and ➡️ the sustained treatment of sovereignty under international pressure belongs to **Topic 12**.

### 4. The two versions of internationalism, and the three global evils

✅ A historical contrast follows:

| | ✅ **Earlier version** | ✅ **The United Nations' call** |
|---|---|---|
| Fields | **International cooperation in military, commercial, educational and cultural spheres** | ✅ Cooperation **in order to maintain world peace** and **to eradicate hunger, disease, illiteracy and superstition from various parts of the world** |
| Basis | **In the mutual interest of the nations concerned** | ⚠️ Human welfare as such |

⚠️ The shift is from **mutual interest** to **eradication**. ✅ The earlier version is transactional — states cooperate where it pays them; the UN version is purposive — states cooperate to remove conditions anywhere. ✅ That is the precedence rule institutionalised.

✅ And the contemporary extension:

> In the contemporary world, the problems of environmental pollution, terrorism and drug-trafficking have assumed global dimensions. Internationalists should come forward to muster vigorous international cooperation to fight against these evils on the global scale.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

```text
   THREE PROBLEMS OF GLOBAL DIMENSIONS
     ENVIRONMENTAL POLLUTION
     TERRORISM
     DRUG-TRAFFICKING
   => require VIGOROUS INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION
      ON THE GLOBAL SCALE
```

⚠️ All three reappear later in the chapter: pollution at Lesson 33, and terrorism and drug-trafficking at Lessons 34 and 37. ✅ The chapter is consistent, and an answer that tracks one of these themes across the chapter demonstrates command of the whole.

⚠️ ❌ No contemporary instance, treaty, agency or figure is named here. ✅ The chapter names none, and this file adds none.

### 5. Comparison: how the two ideologies actually differ

| Question | ✅ Nationalism (L29) | ✅ Internationalism (L30-31) |
|---|---|---|
| Centre of political organisation | ⚠️ The nation-state | ❌ Not the nation-state |
| Object of loyalty | ⚠️ The nation, **undivided** | ❌ Not the nation undivided |
| Ordering of interests | **Primacy** to **national interest over all other interests** | **Human interest should be given precedence** in a clash |
| Attitude to sovereignty | ⚠️ Each nation an **independent state** | ✅ **Reasonable restraints** accepted by the state |
| Slogan rejected | — | ✅ **'My country, right or wrong'** |
| Where they agree | ✅ Both affirm **equal status** among nations and reject the **domination or supremacy of any other nation** | ✅ Same |

🔑 The last row is the one most answers miss. ⚠️ Nationalism's third entitlement claim — **no nation should be kept under the domination or supremacy of any other nation** — is an internationalist principle in content. ✅ The two ideologies are **conflicting** on ordering and on the centre of organisation; ❌ they are not opposed at every point.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The precedence rule is unworkable. ⚠️ Who determines what **universal human interest** requires, and by what procedure? ⚠️ In practice the determination is made by states, and the most powerful states are best placed to present their own interests as humanity's — which is precisely the **garb** the chapter identified at Lesson 26.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's stream (b) does not entrust the determination to a single authority; it works through **larger international organizations** in which states **cooperate**, and it requires each state to **accept** restraints rather than have them imposed. ✅ The anchoring purposes — **world peace, collective security and observance of international law** — are also thin and widely shared, which limits the scope for a powerful state to substitute its own agenda.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply reduces the risk without removing it, and the chapter offers no account of how **reasonable** is to be determined or of what happens when a powerful state refuses. ⚠️ Its own later sentence at Lesson 34 — that the nation-state **should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty** — is equally undefined. ⚠️ The honest statement is that the chapter establishes the *need* for restraint and leaves its *measure* open.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The precedence rule and the restraints clause are the two sentences every internationalism answer should contain.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Compare nationalism and internationalism.* — use the six-row table above rather than two separate descriptions.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting internationalism as the abolition of the nation-state. ✅ Stream (b) retains it and restrains it; only stream (a) transcends it.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Close with the shared ground — equal status and non-domination — to show that the conflict is about ordering, not about everything.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **In view of the growing contact between different parts of the world, a nation-state is no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members, not to speak of mankind as such.**
2. ✅ Hence peoples **should form larger international organizations and cooperate to serve the common interest of mankind**.
3. ✅ **In the case of a clash between national interest and universal human interest, human interest should be given precedence.**
4. ✅ Internationalism **repudiates the idea of 'my country, right or wrong'**.
5. ✅ **Each nation-state should accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty.**
6. ✅ The three purposes: **world peace, collective security and observance of international law**.
7. ⚠️ **Accept** preserves sovereignty as derived and self-binding; ⚠️ **reasonable** is left undefined.
8. ✅ An **earlier version** believed in cooperation in **military, commercial, educational and cultural spheres in the mutual interest of the nations concerned**.
9. ✅ **The United Nations called for international cooperation in order to maintain world peace and to eradicate hunger, disease, illiteracy and superstition.**
10. ✅ Contemporary problems of **global dimensions**: **environmental pollution, terrorism and drug-trafficking**.
11. ✅ **Internationalists should come forward to muster vigorous international cooperation to fight against these evils on the global scale.**
12. ⚠️ Nationalism and internationalism agree that no nation should be dominated by another; they differ on primacy and on the centre of organisation.
13. 🔑 Mnemonic for the three purposes: **Peace · Security · Law**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the restraints clause of internationalism can be reconciled with the chapter's own account of sovereignty.

**Model answer:** ✅ Through the verb **accept**. ✅ Internationalism **insists that each nation-state should accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty in the interests of world peace, collective security and observance of international law**. ⚠️ A restraint that the state itself accepts is an exercise of its own final authority, not a subtraction from it. ✅ The chapter's definition supports this: sovereignty is the **final authority of the state over its population and its territory**, which **may be exercised by the government of the day** but **essentially belongs to the state from which it is derived**. ⚠️ Since the authority is original in the state, the state can bind itself without ceasing to be its source. ⚠️ The unresolved part is the word **reasonable**, for which the chapter supplies no criterion, and the same vagueness returns when it later says the nation-state must **concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty**. 🔑 The compatibility is real; the measure of restraint is left open.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present internationalism as denying that national interest matters. ⚠️ The precedence rule is conditional — **in the case of a clash** — so it ranks interests rather than eliminating one of them, and it is directed specifically at the slogan **'my country, right or wrong'**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Compare nationalism and internationalism as responses to the nation-state. *(Compare — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **The concept of nation-state is the focus of two conflicting ideologies.** ✅ Nationalism treats the nation as the unit: it **accords primacy to... national interest over all other interests**, and demands that **each nation should constitute an independent state; and each state should represent a particular nation**. ✅ Internationalism **does not regard nation-state as the centre of political organization, nor... accepts nation as the object of individual's undivided loyalty**, since **a nation-state is no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members**. ✅ Hence **in the case of a clash between national interest and universal human interest, human interest should be given precedence**; it **repudiates... 'my country, right or wrong'** and insists that each state **accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty in the interests of world peace, collective security and observance of international law**. ⚠️ Yet both affirm equal status among nations.

**Rubric [10]:** nationalism's primacy claim and at least one postulate **2** · internationalism's two denials **2** · the capacity premise **1** · the precedence rule and the repudiated slogan **2** · the restraints clause with all three purposes **2** · the shared ground on equality and non-domination **1**.

## Lesson 32 — The verdict of history, and the nation/state mismatch

Progress: 32 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part VIII — nationalism and internationalism
