Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 and §9, with the Advanced companion's §7 and §12, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.166-167 (printed 150-151), which supply the historical survey of state systems, the medieval-empire parallel, the uncertainty about the future, the Kurds, the unassimilated states and the diaspora case.
CA search: "stateless nations ethnic conflict state disintegration political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current conflicts the chapter does not address and that would require independent verification; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson, and no contemporary case is named.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The historical argument

✅ The chapter tests the nation-state against its predecessors:

> In the history of mankind various types of state systems were evolved in different ages, but none of them could provide for enduring peace and security to people.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.166 (printed 150)

| ✅ System | ✅ Its specific failure |
|---|---|
| **Greek city-states** | **Were fighting against each other** |
| **Roman empire** | **Was able to control peoples of different races but it failed to arouse a sense of social solidarity among them** |
| **The medieval age** | **The Pope and the Emperor wanted to create a universal system on the basis of religious unity, but when different countries became aware of their national identities, the medieval empire was dissolved** |

```text
   CITY-STATES   -> control without PEACE  (mutual war)
   ROMAN EMPIRE  -> control without SOLIDARITY
   MEDIEVAL      -> universality destroyed by the rise of
                    NATIONAL IDENTITIES
                          |
   TODAY: "nation-state is also facing the similar problem"
```

🔑 The three failures are of three different kinds — external peace, internal cohesion, and durability against a rising principle — and the chapter's claim is that the nation-state faces **the similar problem** as the last of them.

⚠️ The medieval parallel deserves emphasis because it is an argument about *mechanism*. ✅ The medieval empire fell because a new principle of identification — **national identities** — outgrew it. ⚠️ By analogy, a nation-state would fall to whatever principle of identification outgrows nationality. ✅ The chapter does not say what that would be, and ❌ this file does not supply one.

### 2. The verdict, and the honest uncertainty

✅ The chapter's assessment is balanced:

> Today nation-state is also facing the similar problem. It has proved more efficient than the medieval empire to provide mankind with security and happiness, but under the changed conditions it seems unable to perform its role. With the beginning of the twenty-first century, search for a new form of organization has begun which should be able to fulfil hopes and aspirations of humanity. But there is no certainty about the future form of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.166-167 (printed 150-151)

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ Strength |
|---|---|
| **More efficient than the medieval empire to provide mankind with security and happiness** | ✅ A comparative credit |
| **Under the changed conditions it seems unable to perform its role** | ⚠️ **Seems** — a hedged judgment |
| **Search for a new form of organization has begun** | ⚠️ A search, not a discovery |
| **There is no certainty about the future form of the state** | ✅ An explicit admission of uncertainty |

🔑 The final sentence is the single most quotable line for any question about the future of the state, precisely because it refuses prediction. ⚠️ Note how it qualifies the section heading of Lesson 25, **RISE AND GROWTH OF THE MODERN NATION-STATE**: the sequence that looked teleological is here declared open-ended.

### 3. The nation/state mismatch, in three forms

✅ **In the modern age nation and state are generally regarded as concomitant, but many examples will prove otherwise.**

⚠️ The chapter then gives three distinct kinds of mismatch, and they map onto Lesson 29's two postulates.

```text
   MISMATCH A — A NATION WITHOUT A STATE
     communities scattered over different parts of the world
     entertaining a sense of unity on the basis of common
     race, culture, language and religion, professing
     NATIONAL CONSCIOUSNESS, yet UNABLE TO ORGANIZE
     THEMSELVES AS A SINGLE STATE
     => "the Kurds are scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey
         although they recognize themselves as one nation"
     => BREAKS POSTULATE 1
            |
   MISMATCH B — A STATE WITHOUT ONE NATION
     states in which DIFFERENT ETHNIC GROUPS LIVE TOGETHER
     but HAVE NOT BEEN ABLE TO ASSIMILATE THEMSELVES INTO
     ONE NATION
     => "Countries like Lebanon and Cyprus remained afflicted
         with civil wars for very long"
     => "Former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia
         have been divided into many parts due to such
         disruptive tendencies"
     => BREAKS POSTULATE 2
            |
   MISMATCH C — ALLEGIANCE ACROSS STATES
     people MIGRATED in search of opportunities for
     advancement, NATURALIZED CITIZENS of those countries,
     yet SO DEEPLY ATTACHED to the language, culture, faith
     and customs of the countries of their origin that they
     are PREPARED TO MAKE SACRIFICES FOR THE WELFARE OF
     THOSE COUNTRIES
     => "the Chinese who have settled abroad are still making
         substantial contribution to the advancement of China"
     => BREAKS 'UNDIVIDED ALLEGIANCE' (L20)
```

⚠️ Three cautions on handling these examples.

- ⚠️ They are **book-period illustrations** from a **2009** text. ✅ Use them as the chapter's examples; ❌ do not update them into present-day geopolitical claims, and ❌ do not add cases the chapter does not name.
- ✅ Mismatch C is the most neglected and the most interesting, because it contradicts the chapter's own definition. ⚠️ At Lesson 20 nationhood required **undivided allegiance**; here naturalised citizens' allegiance is openly divided and the chapter offers no reconciliation.
- ⚠️ Note the chapter's own framing word for Mismatch B: **disruptive tendencies** — the same phrase it used at Lesson 21 for post-independence states. ✅ The two passages describe one phenomenon at two scales.

### 4. Comparison: mismatch mapped to nationalism's postulates

| Mismatch | ✅ Chapter's instance | ⚠️ Which nationalist postulate it breaks |
|---|---|---|
| A — nation without state | **The Kurds**, **scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey** | **Each nation should constitute an independent state** |
| B — state without one nation | **Lebanon**, **Cyprus**; **former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia** | **Each state should represent a particular nation** |
| C — divided allegiance | **The Chinese who have settled abroad** | ⚠️ The **undivided allegiance** of the chapter's own definition of the nation |

🔑 This table is the most efficient way to show that the chapter's examples are not a list but a systematic refutation. ✅ Nationalism's demand for congruence fails in both directions and its presupposition about loyalty fails too.

### 5. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter diagnoses a crisis of the nation-state and then names no successor. ⚠️ **There is no certainty about the future form of the state** is an evasion dressed as modesty; a theory that identifies a form as failing owes an account of what replaces it.

✅ **Reply.** The admission is a virtue given the chapter's own method. ✅ Its historical sections explain each form by the conditions that produced it — geography for the city-state, conquest for Rome, industrial change for feudalism's end — so it cannot name a successor before the conditions that would generate one are visible. ⚠️ Predicting the next form would require exactly the speculative method the chapter avoids elsewhere.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply explains the silence and does not fill it. ⚠️ The chapter also leaves Mismatch C unreconciled with its own definition of nationhood, and it does not ask whether the three mismatches are evidence that the nation-state is failing or evidence that nationalism's congruence demand was always unrealistic. ⚠️ Those are different conclusions, and the chapter draws neither.

### 6. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The three mismatches are the chapter's empirical case against the one-nation-one-state formula.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Are nation and state coterminous?* — the three mismatches with their instances, mapped to the two postulates.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reporting the chapter's examples as current geopolitical facts. ✅ They are 2009 illustrations and must be labelled as such.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Close with the **no certainty about the future form of the state** sentence; it is a qualified conclusion that examiners reward.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Various types of state systems were evolved in different ages, but none of them could provide for enduring peace and security to people.**
2. ✅ **Greek city-states were fighting against each other.**
3. ✅ The **Roman empire** controlled peoples of different races but **failed to arouse a sense of social solidarity among them**.
4. ✅ In the medieval age **the Pope and the Emperor wanted to create a universal system on the basis of religious unity**, dissolved when **different countries became aware of their national identities**.
5. ✅ **Today nation-state is also facing the similar problem.**
6. ✅ It has **proved more efficient than the medieval empire to provide mankind with security and happiness**.
7. ✅ **But under the changed conditions it seems unable to perform its role.**
8. ✅ **With the beginning of the twenty-first century, search for a new form of organization has begun.**
9. ✅ **But there is no certainty about the future form of the state.**
10. ✅ **In the modern age nation and state are generally regarded as concomitant, but many examples will prove otherwise.**
11. ✅ **The Kurds are scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey although they recognize themselves as one nation.**
12. ✅ **Countries like Lebanon and Cyprus remained afflicted with civil wars for very long.**
13. ✅ **Former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia have been divided into many parts due to such disruptive tendencies.**
14. ✅ Naturalised citizens may remain **so deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin** that they make sacrifices for them — **the Chinese who have settled abroad**.
15. 🔑 Three mismatches: **nation without state · state without one nation · allegiance across states**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the chapter's three examples of nation/state mismatch refute the two postulates of nationalism, and identify the one that also embarrasses the chapter's own definition of the nation.

**Model answer:** ✅ Postulate 1 — **each nation should constitute an independent state** — is refuted by communities that **profess national consciousness** yet are **unable to organize themselves as a single state**: **the Kurds are scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey although they recognize themselves as one nation**. ✅ Postulate 2 — **each state should represent a particular nation** — is refuted by states in which **different ethnic groups live together but they have not been able to assimilate themselves into one nation**, such as **Lebanon** and **Cyprus**, and by **former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia**, **divided into many parts due to such disruptive tendencies**. ⚠️ The third case embarrasses the chapter itself: naturalised citizens remain **so deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin** that they will sacrifice for them — yet Lesson 20 defined the nation by **undivided allegiance**. 🔑 The chapter supplies the counter-example to its own definition and does not reconcile it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the named countries as current descriptions. ⚠️ They are illustrations from a **2009** edition, offered to make conceptual points about mismatch; the chapter's own companion material warns against quoting such instances as present geopolitical status without fresh verification.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the claim that nation and state are coterminous. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **In the modern age nation and state are generally regarded as concomitant, but many examples will prove otherwise.** ✅ A nation may lack a state: communities professing **national consciousness** may be **unable to organize themselves as a single state**, as with **the Kurds**, **scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey although they recognize themselves as one nation**. ✅ A state may contain no single nation: **different ethnic groups live together but they have not been able to assimilate themselves into one nation**, as in **Lebanon** and **Cyprus**, while **former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia have been divided into many parts due to such disruptive tendencies**. ✅ Allegiance may even cross states, since naturalised citizens may remain **deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin**. ⚠️ These are **2009** illustrations, used conceptually.

**Rubric [10]:** the concomitance claim stated and then qualified **1** · the stateless-nation case with the Kurds **2** · the unassimilated-state case with at least two named instances **3** · the divided-allegiance case **2** · an explicit label that the instances are book-period **1** · the no-certainty conclusion **1**.

## Lesson 33 — Security and environment: two pressures that cross borders

Progress: 33 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part IX — pressures on the nation-state

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §6 and §12, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.167 (printed 151), which supplies the security-function claim, the lethal-weapons argument, the industrial-waste point, the fuel-consumption claim and the soil-erosion chain ending in global warming.
CA search: "transboundary pollution global warming nation state capacity security 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current climate and security material governed by instruments the chapter does not cite and requiring independent verification; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The argument's shape

⚠️ Lesson 31 asserted that the nation-state is **no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members**. ✅ This lesson and the next supply the evidence, in three instalments: security, environment and communication. ⚠️ Each follows the same two-step form.

```text
   STEP 1  the nation-state has a FUNCTION
   STEP 2  a cause of harm CROSSES ITS BOUNDARY
   ------  therefore the function cannot be discharged
           within the boundary
```

🔑 Recognising the repeated form is what makes this part of the chapter an argument rather than a list of worries.

### 2. Security

✅ The chapter's statement:

> One major function of a nation-state is to provide for security to its residents. But due to the invention of lethal weapons and probability of their use by other countries, no nation-state has remained capable of providing full security to its residents. Thus if a country resorts to a nuclear attack on others with the help of advanced missiles, it would prove disastrous to humanity far and wide. Further, it would cause so much atmospheric pollution that no part of the globe is likely to be spared by its effect.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.167 (printed 151)

| Step | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| Function | **To provide for security to its residents** |
| Disabling fact | **The invention of lethal weapons and probability of their use by other countries** |
| Verdict | **No nation-state has remained capable of providing full security to its residents** |
| Reach | **Disastrous to humanity far and wide** |
| Second-order effect | **So much atmospheric pollution that no part of the globe is likely to be spared** |

⚠️ Note the precision of the verdict: **full** security. ✅ The chapter does not claim states provide none; it claims none can provide all. ⚠️ Dropping **full** overstates the point and invites the obvious rebuttal that states do in fact defend their populations.

🔑 The last clause is structurally clever: a *military* problem is shown to produce an *environmental* one, which is how the chapter moves to its next pressure without changing subject.

⚠️ ❌ No country, weapon, treaty or episode is named. ✅ The chapter names none, and this file names none.

### 3. Environment: three mechanisms

✅ The chapter supplies three distinct environmental arguments:

```text
   MECHANISM 1 — INDUSTRIAL WASTE
     "the atmospheric pollution caused by the disposal of
      the industrial waste is NOT CONFINED TO THE
      BOUNDARIES OF NATION-STATES"
            |
   MECHANISM 2 — FUEL CONSUMPTION
     "the increasing consumption of petrol and diesel by
      the advanced nations is responsible for causing
      immense harm to the global atmosphere"
            |
   MECHANISM 3 — LAND USE
     cutting of FORESTS AND MOUNTAINS for FUEL, TIMBER,
     LIME STONE or HYDRO-ELECTRIC POWER PROJECTS
     IN ANY COUNTRY causes SOIL EROSION
            |
     "its cumulative effect results in DISTORTION OF
      WEATHER CYCLE and consequent danger of GLOBAL WARMING"
```

| Mechanism | ✅ Who causes it, on the chapter's account | ⚠️ Why it defeats the boundary |
|---|---|---|
| **Industrial waste** | ⚠️ Unattributed | **Not confined to the boundaries of nation-states** |
| **Petrol and diesel consumption** | ✅ **The advanced nations** | ⚠️ Harm is to **the global atmosphere**, not to the consumer |
| **Deforestation and quarrying** | ✅ **In any country** | ⚠️ **Cumulative effect** across countries |

⚠️ Three analytical observations.

- ⚠️ **The attribution differs by mechanism.** ✅ Only mechanism 2 names a responsible party — **the advanced nations** — which connects the environmental argument to Lesson 28's neo-colonialism: the same nations that take **cheap labour and raw materials** also impose a disproportionate atmospheric cost.
- ⚠️ **Mechanism 3 is explicitly cumulative and explicitly universal**: **in any country**, with effects arising from the **cumulative effect**. ✅ That is the structure of a collective-action problem, though the chapter does not use the term.
- ⚠️ **The named named four land uses** — **fuel, timber, lime stone** and **hydro-electric power projects** — are the chapter's own. ✅ They are worth reproducing because they show that the harm arises from ordinary development activity, not from negligence.

🔑 Answer line: the chapter's environmental case is that harm is produced by lawful activity inside one jurisdiction and suffered in all of them — which is exactly the condition that no sovereign, territorially bounded authority can address.

⚠️ ❌ No figure, target, instrument or dated commitment is introduced here. ✅ The chapter supplies none, and this file adds none.

### 4. Why this is a problem about sovereignty, not about policy

⚠️ The chapter does not spell this out, so the connection is marked as a reading. ✅ Sovereignty, at Lesson 9, is **the final authority of the state over its population and its territory**, and territory, at Lesson 7, **symbolizes the sphere of sovereignty**. ⚠️ Both the security and the environmental harms described here are produced outside the sphere in which the suffering state's authority is final.

| ✅ Element of the state | ⚠️ How these pressures bear on it |
|---|---|
| **Territory** | ⚠️ Harm enters across the boundary that was supposed to be the limit of jurisdiction |
| **Government** | ⚠️ Cannot provide the **common services** of defence or a safe environment by acting alone |
| **Sovereignty** | ⚠️ **Final authority** is final only over what lies inside; the causes lie outside |
| **Population** | ⚠️ Suffers harm its own state did not authorise and cannot prevent |

✅ This is why Lesson 31's conclusion follows: not that sovereignty is abolished, but that each state **should accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty** — because unilateral authority has become insufficient for the state's own purposes.

### 5. Comparison: the two pressures

| Axis | ✅ Security | ✅ Environment |
|---|---|---|
| Origin of harm | ⚠️ A deliberate act by **other countries** | ⚠️ Ordinary industrial and developmental activity |
| Intention | ⚠️ Hostile | ⚠️ Not hostile |
| Attribution | ⚠️ Clear in principle | ⚠️ Diffuse, **cumulative** |
| Remedy implied | ⚠️ **Collective security** (L31) | ⚠️ **Prevention of atmospheric pollution** through cooperation (L27, L31) |
| Shared feature | ✅ **No part of the globe is likely to be spared** | ✅ **Not confined to the boundaries of nation-states** |

⚠️ The last row is the chapter's unifying claim and the reason the two pressures belong in one lesson: in both cases, the boundary that defines the state does not define the harm.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The argument proves too much. ⚠️ Threats have always crossed boundaries — invasion, epidemic, famine — and states have nonetheless remained the units that respond. ⚠️ Showing that a state cannot provide **full** security establishes only that no political form provides perfect protection, which is true of every form the chapter has described.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's claim is comparative, not absolute, and sits inside a historical sequence. ✅ It grants that the nation-state **has proved more efficient than the medieval empire to provide mankind with security and happiness**, and argues only that **under the changed conditions it seems unable to perform its role**. ⚠️ What is new is the combination of reach and diffuseness: the environmental mechanisms arise from **any country** and operate through **cumulative effect**, so no single state's defensive capacity is relevant to them at all.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply distinguishes the new case from invasion and does not establish that a different political form would do better. ⚠️ The chapter itself declines to say what the alternative is — **there is no certainty about the future form of the state** — so its conclusion is properly limited to the demand for **reasonable restraints** and cooperation, not to the replacement of the state.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** These two pressures are the chapter's empirical case for internationalism and the bridge from Part VIII to Part IX.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is the nation-state still capable of discharging its functions?* — security first, then the three environmental mechanisms, then the restraints conclusion.
- ❌ **Trap.** Dropping the word **full** from the security verdict. ✅ The claim is about full security, not about security as such.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the two-step form — function, then boundary-crossing cause — as your paragraph structure; it makes the argument visible.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **One major function of a nation-state is to provide for security to its residents.**
2. ✅ Because of **the invention of lethal weapons and probability of their use by other countries**, **no nation-state has remained capable of providing full security to its residents**.
3. ✅ A nuclear attack with **advanced missiles** would **prove disastrous to humanity far and wide**.
4. ✅ It would also cause **so much atmospheric pollution that no part of the globe is likely to be spared by its effect**.
5. ✅ **The atmospheric pollution caused by the disposal of the industrial waste is not confined to the boundaries of nation-states.**
6. ✅ **The increasing consumption of petrol and diesel by the advanced nations is responsible for causing immense harm to the global atmosphere.**
7. ✅ **Cutting of forests and mountains for fuel, timber, lime stone or hydro-electric power projects in any country causes soil erosion.**
8. ✅ **Its cumulative effect results in distortion of weather cycle and consequent danger of global warming.**
9. ⚠️ Only the fuel mechanism attributes responsibility, and it attributes it to **the advanced nations**.
10. ⚠️ The land-use mechanism is explicitly universal — **in any country** — and **cumulative**.
11. ⚠️ Each pressure follows the same form: a state function, a cause that crosses the boundary.
12. ⚠️ The pressures bear on **territory**, **government** and **sovereignty** simultaneously.
13. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is that the state cannot provide **full** security — ❌ not that it provides none.
14. 🔑 Mnemonic for the environmental mechanisms: **Waste · Fuel · Felling**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the chapter's environmental argument bears on sovereignty rather than merely on policy.

**Model answer:** ✅ Because the harms are produced outside the sphere within which the affected state's authority is final. ✅ Sovereignty is **the final authority of the state over its population and its territory**, and **territory symbolizes the sphere of sovereignty of the state**. ⚠️ But **the atmospheric pollution caused by the disposal of the industrial waste is not confined to the boundaries of nation-states**; **the increasing consumption of petrol and diesel by the advanced nations** harms **the global atmosphere**; and the felling of **forests and mountains for fuel, timber, lime stone or hydro-electric power projects in any country** produces, by **cumulative effect**, a **distortion of weather cycle and consequent danger of global warming**. ⚠️ Each harm is lawful where it originates and injurious where it is not authorised, so no exercise of final authority within a territory can prevent it. ✅ That is why the chapter's remedy is institutional rather than regulatory: states must **accept reasonable restraints on sovereignty** and cooperate.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not state that the nation-state can no longer provide security. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is that **no nation-state has remained capable of providing full security to its residents**, and it elsewhere credits the nation-state with being **more efficient than the medieval empire to provide mankind with security and happiness**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the pressures that security and environmental problems place upon the nation-state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **One major function of a nation-state is to provide for security to its residents**, but **due to the invention of lethal weapons... no nation-state has remained capable of providing full security**; a nuclear attack would be **disastrous to humanity far and wide**, causing **so much atmospheric pollution that no part of the globe is likely to be spared**. ✅ Environmental harm works the same way: **the atmospheric pollution caused by the disposal of the industrial waste is not confined to the boundaries of nation-states**; **the increasing consumption of petrol and diesel by the advanced nations** harms **the global atmosphere**; and felling **for fuel, timber, lime stone or hydro-electric power projects in any country** causes erosion whose **cumulative effect results in distortion of weather cycle and consequent danger of global warming**. ⚠️ The causes lie outside the territory where authority is final; the remedy must be cooperative.

**Rubric [10]:** the security function and the full-security qualification **2** · the double effect of a nuclear attack **1** · all three environmental mechanisms **3** · the attribution to advanced nations in the fuel mechanism **1** · the cumulative, any-country structure **1** · the conclusion linking boundary-crossing harm to the need for cooperation **2**.

### 1. ⚠️ The second overflow leaf, disclosed before the teaching

⚠️ ⚠️ **This lesson stands at the chapter's second unnumbered overflow leaf.**

✅ The communication argument begins at the foot of PDF p.167 (printed 151) and continues onto PDF p.168. ⚠️ PDF p.168 carries **no folio**, and the whole of its text is a single narrow column set in an otherwise blank page:

> message or a copy of any document or picture from one corner of the world to another at the speed of light. With the help of satellite, television programmes can be transmitted and watched in all
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.168, ✅ the leaf's entire text

✅ The clause runs straight on into the top of PDF p.169 (printed 152) — **parts of the world simultaneously** — so the sentence is complete across the three pages and ✅ no text is missing at this leaf. ❌ **Nothing is reconstructed, inferred or supplied anywhere across the join.** ⚠️ The offset changes from 16 to 17 after this leaf, which completes the three-band pagination recorded in the Source Ledger.

⚠️ What follows is taught from PDF p.167 in full, the whole text of p.168, and PDF p.169 in full.

### 2. The communication argument

✅ From PDF p.167:

> Then information revolution all over the world has practically eliminated the distance between different countries in the matter of communication. Telephone, internet, e-mail, fax, etc. enable us to send a
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.167 (printed 151)

✅ Continuing through the whole text of p.168 and onto p.169:

| ✅ Capability | ✅ Source page |
|---|---|
| **Telephone, internet, e-mail, fax** | p.167 |
| Send **a message or a copy of any document or picture from one corner of the world to another at the speed of light** | ⚠️ p.168 strip |
| **With the help of satellite, television programmes can be transmitted and watched in all parts of the world simultaneously** | ⚠️ p.168 strip + p.169 |
| **Any important event occurring in any part of the world is reported so fast and so vividly in all other parts that nothing remains unknown** | p.169 |

✅ The conclusion:

> The increasing intimacy of the people all over the world has converted it into a 'global village'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.169 (printed 152)

```text
   INFORMATION REVOLUTION
     has PRACTICALLY ELIMINATED THE DISTANCE between
     different countries IN THE MATTER OF COMMUNICATION
                |
   messages / documents / pictures AT THE SPEED OF LIGHT
   satellite television WATCHED SIMULTANEOUSLY
   any important event REPORTED SO FAST AND SO VIVIDLY
   that NOTHING REMAINS UNKNOWN
                |
   INCREASING INTIMACY OF THE PEOPLE ALL OVER THE WORLD
                |
          => a 'GLOBAL VILLAGE'
```

🔑 Note the precision of **in the matter of communication**. ✅ The chapter does not claim distance has been eliminated; it claims communication no longer tracks it. ⚠️ That qualification matters, because territory remains an element of the state while ceasing to be a barrier to information.

⚠️ The phrase **'global village'** carries the chapter's own inverted commas. ❌ No author is credited for it on this page, and ❌ none should be supplied.

### 3. From communication to economy — and to crime

✅ The chapter moves immediately to two consequences:

> Unprecedented advancement of the means of transport and communication has paved the way for globalization of economy. Even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means of transport and communication.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.169 (printed 152)

| ✅ Consequence | ⚠️ Character |
|---|---|
| **Globalization of economy** | ⚠️ Structural; ➡️ owned in full by **Topic 12** |
| **Criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists** making **full use of the advanced means** | ⚠️ Adversarial |

🔑 The pairing is the chapter's sharpest move in this passage: the same infrastructure serves commerce and its enemies. ⚠️ Capability is neutral between users, so a development that strengthens the world economy simultaneously strengthens those who attack it. ✅ Two of the three actors named — **drug traffickers** and **terrorists** — are from the list of global evils at Lesson 31, closing that loop.

### 4. The conclusion: a new role, and conceded authority

✅ The chapter's conclusion to the whole of Part IX:

> The situation calls for a global effort to deal with the global problems. In this scenario, nation-state will have to play a new role for which it should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.169 (printed 152)

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **A global effort to deal with the global problems** | ⚠️ The remedy matches the scale of the problem |
| **Nation-state will have to play a new role** | ⚠️ A new role, ❌ not abolition |
| **It should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty** | ⚠️ **Concede** — the state is the agent of its own adjustment |

```text
   PRESSURES (L33-34)
     security · environment · information · economy · crime
                |
   "a GLOBAL EFFORT to deal with the GLOBAL PROBLEMS"
                |
   the NATION-STATE will have to play A NEW ROLE
                |
   it should CONCEDE NECESSARY ADJUSTMENT in its
   AUTHORITY AND SOVEREIGNTY
```

🔑 This sentence is the chapter's final word on the nation-state, and its verbs are carefully chosen. ✅ **Play a new role** preserves the state as an actor; ✅ **concede** makes the adjustment voluntary, matching Lesson 31's **accept reasonable restraints**. ❌ The chapter nowhere says the state is superseded.

⚠️ The weak point is **necessary**, which, like **reasonable** at Lesson 31, is left undefined. ⚠️ The chapter establishes that adjustment is required and gives no measure of how much. ➡️ **Topic 12** carries the sustained analysis.

### 5. Comparison: three pressures, one conclusion

| Pressure | ✅ The chapter's key phrase | ⚠️ What it disables |
|---|---|---|
| Security (L33) | **No nation-state has remained capable of providing full security** | ⚠️ The protective function |
| Environment (L33) | **Not confined to the boundaries of nation-states** | ⚠️ Territorial jurisdiction as a remedy |
| Information and economy (L34) | **Practically eliminated the distance between different countries** | ⚠️ The boundary as a filter |
| ✅ Common conclusion | **Concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty** | — |

⚠️ Read downward: each pressure attacks a different element — government's services, territory's jurisdiction, the boundary's filtering function — and all three converge on sovereignty. ✅ That convergence is why the chapter's conclusion is stated in terms of **authority and sovereignty** rather than of policy.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The passage is descriptive and dated. ⚠️ A list of communication technologies and a borrowed phrase about a **'global village'** do not constitute an argument about sovereignty, and the demand for **necessary adjustment** is too vague to guide anything.

✅ **Reply.** The argument is not in the technology list but in the pairing that follows it. ✅ The chapter shows that the same infrastructure that produces **globalization of economy** also serves **criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists** — so the capability cannot be welcomed selectively, and a state cannot obtain the benefits while excluding the harms by territorial means. ⚠️ That is a genuine argument about jurisdiction, and it is what licenses the move to **a global effort**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The vagueness objection stands. ⚠️ The chapter supplies no criterion for **necessary**, no account of which powers should be adjusted, and no institutional design. ⚠️ It also does not consider that states may respond by *strengthening* control over information flows rather than conceding authority. ⚠️ Both gaps are real and are recorded rather than filled.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the chapter's bridge to globalisation and the source of its most quotable conclusion about sovereignty.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How has the information revolution affected the nation-state?* — elimination of communicative distance, global village, the double use of the same means, the conceded adjustment.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that the chapter predicts the end of the nation-state. ✅ It says the state **will have to play a new role**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the commerce-and-crime pairing as the analytical core; it converts a technology list into an argument.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The **information revolution all over the world has practically eliminated the distance between different countries in the matter of communication**.
2. ✅ The named means: **telephone, internet, e-mail, fax**.
3. ✅ They enable **a message or a copy of any document or picture** to travel **from one corner of the world to another at the speed of light**.
4. ✅ **With the help of satellite, television programmes can be transmitted and watched in all parts of the world simultaneously.**
5. ✅ **Any important event occurring in any part of the world is reported so fast and so vividly in all other parts that nothing remains unknown.**
6. ✅ **The increasing intimacy of the people all over the world has converted it into a 'global village'.**
7. ✅ **Unprecedented advancement of the means of transport and communication has paved the way for globalization of economy.**
8. ✅ **Even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means of transport and communication.**
9. ✅ **The situation calls for a global effort to deal with the global problems.**
10. ✅ **Nation-state will have to play a new role for which it should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty.**
11. ⚠️ **Concede** and **new role** preserve the state as an actor; ❌ the chapter does not announce its end.
12. ⚠️ **Necessary** is undefined, as **reasonable** was at Lesson 31.
13. ⚠️ PDF p.168 is an unnumbered overflow leaf carrying only the text quoted above; ✅ the sentence completes at the top of p.169 and ❌ nothing is reconstructed.
14. 🔑 Memorise the conclusion verbatim; it is the chapter's final verdict on the nation-state.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter pair the globalisation of the economy with the activity of criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists, and what does the pairing establish?

**Model answer:** ✅ Because both run on the same infrastructure. ✅ The chapter states that **unprecedented advancement of the means of transport and communication has paved the way for globalization of economy**, and immediately that **even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means of transport and communication**. ⚠️ The pairing establishes that the capability is neutral between users: a state cannot accept the economic benefit of frictionless movement while excluding its hostile uses by territorial means, because the same reduction of distance produces both. ⚠️ Since **the information revolution... has practically eliminated the distance between different countries in the matter of communication**, the boundary no longer filters what crosses it. ✅ Hence the conclusion: **the situation calls for a global effort to deal with the global problems**, and **nation-state will have to play a new role for which it should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty**. 🔑 The argument is about jurisdiction, not about technology.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read the chapter as predicting the supersession of the nation-state. ⚠️ Its verbs are deliberately conservative — the state **will have to play a new role** and should **concede necessary adjustment** — which places the state as the agent of its own adaptation, exactly as Lesson 31's **accept reasonable restraints** does.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss the impact of the information revolution on the nation-state. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The **information revolution... has practically eliminated the distance between different countries in... communication**: **telephone, internet, e-mail, fax** send **a message or a copy of any document or picture from one corner of the world to another at the speed of light**, and **with the help of satellite, television programmes can be transmitted and watched in all parts of the world simultaneously**, so **nothing remains unknown**. ✅ **The increasing intimacy of the people all over the world has converted it into a 'global village'.** ✅ It **has paved the way for globalization of economy**; and **even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means**. ⚠️ The same infrastructure serves commerce and its enemies. ✅ Hence **a global effort to deal with the global problems**, with the **nation-state... to play a new role** and **concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty**.

**Rubric [10]:** the elimination-of-distance claim with the qualifier about communication **2** · at least three named means and one capability **2** · the global-village conclusion **1** · the globalisation-of-economy consequence **1** · the criminal-use pairing **2** · the final conclusion quoted in both its limbs **2**.

## Lesson 35 — Three worlds, the cold war and the North-South reframing

Progress: 35 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part X — contemporary trends

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 and §10, with the Advanced companion's §12, read against the section headed **CONTEMPORARY TRENDS IN ADVANCED INDUSTRIAL COUNTRIES AND THE THIRD WORLD** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.169-170 = printed 152-153), which supplies the three-world division, the cold-war definition, non-alignment, the 1991 dissolution and the economic reframing.

CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current commentary that does not bear on the chapter's 2009 categories and would require independent verification; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### 1. ⚠️ A note on the section heading

⚠️ The section heading in the scan is numbered — **VI. CONTEMPORARY TRENDS IN ADVANCED INDUSTRIAL COUNTRIES AND THE THIRD WORLD** — although no other section of this chapter carries a numeral. ⚠️ The numbering is recorded here as an observed feature of the scan; ❌ no inference is drawn from it about the chapter's structure, and ❌ no numbering is supplied for any other section.

### 2. The political division of the world

✅ The chapter's three-way division is precise and must be learnt with its parentheses:

| ✅ World | ✅ Membership as the chapter gives it |
|---|---|
| **The first world** | **Western capitalist countries plus Japan, Australia and New Zealand; often including Israel and South Africa** |
| **The second world** | **Communist states of Eastern and Central Europe, led by the former Soviet Union** |
| **The third world** | **The newly independent, developing countries of Asia, Africa and Latin America** |

```text
   FIRST WORLD  <--- 'COLD WAR' --->  SECOND WORLD
   western capitalist                 communist states of
   + Japan, Australia,                Eastern and Central
     New Zealand                      Europe, led by the
   (often + Israel,                   former Soviet Union
    South Africa)
            \                               /
             \                             /
              \                           /
                      THIRD WORLD
              preferred to remain 'NON-ALIGNED'
              in order to FOCUS ON DEVELOPMENT
              and to CONTAIN THE INTENSITY of
              the prevailing 'cold war'
```

⚠️ Note the hedge **often including** for Israel and South Africa. ✅ The chapter marks these as contested members of the first world rather than settled ones.

### 3. The cold war, defined

✅ The chapter supplies a parenthetical definition that is easy to miss and worth quoting:

> While the first world and the second world were engaged in the 'cold war' (ideological propaganda against each other in a bid to prove their respective superiority), the third world preferred to remain 'non-aligned' in order to focus on development and also to contain the intensity of the prevailing 'cold war'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.169 (printed 152)

| ✅ Term | ✅ The chapter's gloss |
|---|---|
| **'Cold war'** | **Ideological propaganda against each other in a bid to prove their respective superiority** |
| **'Non-aligned'** | ⚠️ A preference of the third world, with **two** stated purposes |

✅ The two purposes of non-alignment: **to focus on development** and **to contain the intensity of the prevailing 'cold war'**.

🔑 The second purpose is the one candidates omit. ⚠️ Non-alignment is presented here not merely as abstention but as an active contribution to international order — a moderating role, not a neutral one. ✅ That reading connects it to Lesson 31's internationalism, where states accept restraints **in the interests of world peace**.

⚠️ The gloss on the cold war as **ideological propaganda** is narrower than most accounts. ⚠️ The chapter does not mention military alliance, deterrence or proxy conflict in this definition. ✅ Report it as the chapter's formulation; ➡️ the fuller account belongs to the **World History** and **International Relations** owners.

### 4. The dissolution, and what survived it

✅ The chapter's account of the change:

> With the dissolution of the second world around 1991, and the consequent end of the 'cold war', this division became partly outdated. However, the third world still maintained its identity with a more vigorous focus on development.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.170 (printed 153)

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **Around 1991** | ⚠️ The chapter's own hedge — **around**, not a precise date |
| **Partly outdated** | ⚠️ **Partly**: the first and second worlds lost meaning; the third did not |
| **The third world still maintained its identity** | ✅ With **a more vigorous focus on development** |

⚠️ The asymmetry is the point. ✅ Two of the three categories were defined by the conflict and dissolved with it; the third was defined by a condition — underdevelopment — that outlived the conflict.

### 5. The economic reframing

✅ The replacement division:

> Its identity was reinforced by an alternative division of the world on economic basis which was in vogue along with its political division. The economic division of the world recognized two broad categories: the developed world and the developing world, sometimes identified as North-South divide. The concept of the developing world coincided with the third world... With the increasing importance of economic power in the world politics, the economic division of the world has become more significant.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.170 (printed 153)

```text
   POLITICAL DIVISION (three worlds)
       first · second · third
             |  partly outdated after ~1991
             v
   ECONOMIC DIVISION (two categories)
       DEVELOPED WORLD  /  DEVELOPING WORLD
       sometimes identified as the NORTH-SOUTH DIVIDE
             |
   "the concept of the developing world COINCIDED with
    the third world"
             |
   "with the increasing importance of ECONOMIC POWER in
    world politics, the economic division... has become
    MORE SIGNIFICANT"
```

⚠️ Three precisions. ✅ The economic division **was in vogue along with** the political one — it is not purely a successor. ✅ **The concept of the developing world coincided with the third world** — coincided, so the two labels pick out the same set while meaning different things. ⚠️ And the reason for its growing significance is stated: **the increasing importance of economic power in the world politics**.

⚠️ The chapter adds that the aggregate of developing countries **continued to be described as the third world even in the context of economic division of the world** — a continuity of vocabulary across a change of criterion. ✅ Lesson 37 will show that the North-South geography does not hold up, which is the chapter's own qualification of the scheme it has just introduced.

### 6. Comparison: two ways of dividing the world

| Axis | ✅ Political division | ✅ Economic division |
|---|---|---|
| Criterion | ⚠️ Ideological alignment | ⚠️ Level of development |
| Categories | **First**, **second**, **third** | **Developed** and **developing**; **North-South divide** |
| Fate after 1991 | ⚠️ **Partly outdated** | ✅ **More significant** |
| Why it changed | ✅ **Dissolution of the second world** and **the end of the 'cold war'** | ✅ **The increasing importance of economic power in world politics** |
| Problem with it | ⚠️ Two categories vanished with the conflict | ⚠️ The geography fails — see Lesson 37 |

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The three-world scheme was always a classification by relation to a conflict rather than by any property of the countries classified, which is why two-thirds of it evaporated in a single year. ⚠️ And describing the cold war merely as **ideological propaganda** omits the military and strategic dimensions that gave the division its force.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter effectively concedes the first point when it reports the division as **partly outdated** and explains that only the third world **maintained its identity** — because only it was defined by an internal condition. ✅ The narrow definition of the cold war is a limitation of this page rather than of the chapter's argument, which needs only that an ideological contest existed from which the third world could stand apart.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply does not repair the definition. ⚠️ The chapter also does not explain why non-alignment required abstention from *alliance* if the conflict was merely propagandistic — a gap that its own narrow gloss creates. ⚠️ It is recorded here and ❌ not filled from outside the two authorised sources.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This page supplies the categories the chapter uses for the rest of the topic and must be labelled as book-period vocabulary.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How was the contemporary world divided, and how did the division change?* — three worlds, cold war, 1991, economic reframing.
- ❌ **Trap.** Using **first/second/third world** as current description. ✅ The chapter itself calls the scheme **partly outdated**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Give both purposes of non-alignment; the containment purpose is the discriminating detail.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The contemporary world was **politically divided into three major parts**.
2. ✅ **First world**: **Western capitalist countries plus Japan, Australia and New Zealand; often including Israel and South Africa**.
3. ✅ **Second world**: **Communist states of Eastern and Central Europe, led by the former Soviet Union**.
4. ✅ **Third world**: **the newly independent, developing countries of Asia, Africa and Latin America**.
5. ✅ **'Cold war'** is glossed as **ideological propaganda against each other in a bid to prove their respective superiority**.
6. ✅ The third world **preferred to remain 'non-aligned'**.
7. ✅ Its two purposes: **to focus on development** and **to contain the intensity of the prevailing 'cold war'**.
8. ✅ **With the dissolution of the second world around 1991, and the consequent end of the 'cold war', this division became partly outdated.**
9. ✅ **However, the third world still maintained its identity with a more vigorous focus on development.**
10. ✅ The **economic division** recognised **the developed world and the developing world, sometimes identified as North-South divide**.
11. ✅ **The concept of the developing world coincided with the third world.**
12. ✅ The aggregate **continued to be described as the third world even in the context of economic division**.
13. ✅ **With the increasing importance of economic power in the world politics, the economic division of the world has become more significant.**
14. ⚠️ These are **2009** categories; ❌ do not present them as current description.
15. 🔑 Mnemonic: **three political worlds → two economic worlds**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did only one of the three political categories survive the end of the cold war, and what replaced the scheme?

**Model answer:** ✅ Because only one of them was defined by a property of its members rather than by a relation to the conflict. ✅ The **first world** and the **second world** were constituted by their engagement in the **'cold war'** — **ideological propaganda against each other in a bid to prove their respective superiority** — so **with the dissolution of the second world around 1991, and the consequent end of the 'cold war', this division became partly outdated**. ✅ The **third world**, by contrast, was defined by being **newly independent** and **developing**, a condition unaffected by the conflict's end, so it **still maintained its identity with a more vigorous focus on development**. ✅ What replaced the scheme was **an alternative division of the world on economic basis** into **the developed world and the developing world, sometimes identified as North-South divide**, which grew in importance **with the increasing importance of economic power in the world politics**. 🔑 **The concept of the developing world coincided with the third world.**

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present non-alignment as mere neutrality. ⚠️ The chapter gives it two purposes — **to focus on development** *and* **to contain the intensity of the prevailing 'cold war'** — the second of which is an active contribution to international order rather than abstention from it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the division of the contemporary world into three worlds and its subsequent reframing. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The world was **politically divided into three major parts**: the **first world** of **Western capitalist countries plus Japan, Australia and New Zealand; often including Israel and South Africa**; the **second world** of **Communist states of Eastern and Central Europe, led by the former Soviet Union**; and the **third world** of **newly independent, developing countries of Asia, Africa and Latin America**. ✅ The first two were **engaged in the 'cold war'** — **ideological propaganda against each other in a bid to prove their respective superiority** — while the third **preferred to remain 'non-aligned' in order to focus on development and... to contain the... 'cold war'**. ✅ **With the dissolution of the second world around 1991... this division became partly outdated**, though **the third world still maintained its identity**. ✅ An **economic division** — **the developed world and the developing world, sometimes identified as North-South divide** — became **more significant**.

**Rubric [10]:** all three worlds with at least two membership details each **3** · the cold-war gloss **2** · both purposes of non-alignment **2** · the 1991 dissolution and the partly-outdated verdict **1** · the economic reframing with the North-South label **1** · the reason for its growing significance **1**.

## Lesson 36 — Trends in advanced industrial countries

Progress: 36 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part X — contemporary trends

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7, with the Advanced companion's §6 and §12, read against **TRENDS IN ADVANCED INDUSTRIAL COUNTRIES** (Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.169-171 = printed 152-154), which supplies the convergence thesis, Weber on bureaucracy, Burnham, the three economists, the two sociologists, the critics and Bell on post-industrial society.
CA search: "convergence thesis industrial society post-industrial society service sector 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ searches returned current labour-market commentary that would require independent verification and that the chapter does not address; ❌ no linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The thesis being reported

✅ The chapter states the claim it is about to survey:

> Western writers have been trying to demonstrate that the course of development in the advanced industrial countries is determined by the level of their industrialization and consequent modernization, and not by their ideological commitment. This meant that the western democracies and the communist states would follow a similar course of development.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.169-170 (printed 152-153)

```text
   CONVERGENCE THESIS
     the course of development is determined by
       LEVEL OF INDUSTRIALIZATION and consequent
       MODERNIZATION
     and NOT by IDEOLOGICAL COMMITMENT
            |
   => WESTERN DEMOCRACIES and COMMUNIST STATES
      would follow A SIMILAR COURSE OF DEVELOPMENT
```

🔑 This is the thesis the whole section documents, and it bears directly on Part VIII: if ideology does not determine development, then the ideological contest of the cold war was less consequential than it appeared, and the third world's non-alignment was better founded than it looked.

⚠️ Note the attribution — **western writers have been trying to demonstrate**. ✅ The chapter reports the thesis rather than adopting it, and it will supply the critics at the end.

### 2. The five-stage dossier

✅ The chapter assembles evidence in chronological layers:

| Layer | ✅ Author, work, year | ✅ Claim |
|---|---|---|
| Early 20th c. | **Max Weber (1864-1920)** | **All modern societies were prone to developing a 'legal-rational' model of bureaucracy** |
| Mid 20th c. | **James Burnham (1907-87)**, *Managerial Revolution*; **1942** | **The rise of a new class of managers was common to capitalist, communist and mixed economies** |
| 1960s economists | **W.W. Rostow**, *The Stages of Economic Growth*; **1960** · **J.K. Galbraith**, *The New Industrial State*; **1964** · **Simon Kuznets**, *Economic Growth and Structure*; **1965** | **Economic growth constitutes a uniform process involving definite stages of growth in all industrial economies irrespective of their adherence to diverse political ideologies** |
| Sociologists | **Marion J. Levy, Jr.**, *Modernization and the Structure of Society*; **1966** · **Talcott Parsons**, *The System of Modern Societies*; **1971** | **Universal structural constraints involved in the process of industrialization lead to the development of certain common characteristics in different types of societies** |
| Post-industrial | **Daniel Bell**, *The Coming of Post-Industrial Society; A Venture in Social Forecasting*; **1974** | ⚠️ A description of **the most highly developed states in the western world** |

✅ Burnham's striking formulation is worth preserving: **the war of 1939 (the Second World War) was the first great war of managerial society as the war of 1914 (the First World War) was the last great war of capitalist society**. ✅ The shift he describes is **the shift of power from the legal owner of the means of production to the effective manager**.

🔑 Burnham's claim is the strongest form of the convergence thesis, because it identifies a *single* new ruling group appearing in **capitalist, communist and mixed economies** alike.

### 3. The two lists of common characteristics

✅ The economists' list — **a uniform 'logic' underlying this process**:

```text
   1 the demand to UTILIZE TECHNOLOGY EFFICIENTLY
   2 the increasing SPECIALIZATION OF TASKS
   3 the necessity for a HIGHLY SKILLED AND EDUCATED MANPOWER
   4 an increasing RATIO OF WORKERS IN INDUSTRIAL AND
     SERVICE SECTORS in relation to the AGRICULTURAL sector
   5 the growing significance of MATERIAL INCENTIVES
   6 evolution of MORE COMPLEX ORGANIZATIONAL STRUCTURES
   7 the emergence of SIMILAR PATTERNS OF MANAGEMENT,
     PLANNING AND POLICY OBJECTIVES
```

✅ The sociologists' list — the effects of **universal structural constraints**:

| ✅ Characteristic |
|---|
| **Urbanization** |
| **Expanding educational systems** |
| **Similar occupational prestige and mobility patterns** |
| **Falling birth and death rates** |
| **Widespread electronic communication networks** |

✅ And a further set specific to advanced industrial societies: **dominance of scientific rationalism over superstition, universalism over particularism, individualism over collectivism, standards of achievement over nepotism**, together with **the dominance of a state that defines rights and obligations, extension of citizenship, increased political participation, and the expansion of individual liberties**.

⚠️ Notice the last cluster. ✅ It is a political claim smuggled into a sociological list: industrialisation is said to produce **extension of citizenship**, **increased political participation** and **the expansion of individual liberties**. 🔑 That is the point at which the convergence thesis becomes a claim about democracy — and it is precisely the point the critics attack.

### 4. The critics

✅ The chapter supplies the rebuttal in its own voice:

> Critics point out that most of these characteristics focus on the western model of development. The theory of universal characteristics of developed societies underplays the role of cultural and ideological factors as well as the strength of leadership prevailing in different types of political systems. It would be futile to look for these characteristics in the course of progress made in socialist countries and the third world. This theory is largely designed to project 'market society model' as a universal model of development which would work to the advantage of western democracies at the expense of other countries.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.170-171 (printed 153-154)

| # | ✅ Criticism |
|---|---|
| 1 | The characteristics **focus on the western model of development** |
| 2 | The theory **underplays the role of cultural and ideological factors** |
| 3 | It underplays **the strength of leadership prevailing in different types of political systems** |
| 4 | **It would be futile to look for these characteristics in the course of progress made in socialist countries and the third world** |
| 5 | It is **largely designed to project 'market society model' as a universal model of development** |
| 6 | Which would **work to the advantage of western democracies at the expense of other countries** |

🔑 Criticism 6 is the sharpest and connects this section to Lesson 28. ⚠️ A theory that universalises one model of development is, on this reading, an intellectual counterpart of neo-colonialism: it secures advantage by setting the standard rather than by administering territory.

⚠️ Criticism 2 is also notable for its structure: the convergence thesis claimed that ideology does not determine development, and the critics reply that ideology and culture are precisely what it leaves out. ✅ The dispute is therefore about the explanatory weight of ideas, not about the facts of industrialisation.

### 5. Bell, and the post-industrial contrast

✅ **Daniel Bell** is introduced as **a votary of the 'end of ideology' thesis**, which the chapter glosses as holding **that the character of social-economic organization of a country at the advanced stage of industrial development is determined by the level of its industrial development, and not by the prevailing ideology** — the convergence thesis restated. ➡️ The end-of-ideology debate itself is owned by **Topic 02**.

✅ The contrast Bell draws:

| Axis | ✅ **Industrial society** | ✅ **Post-industrial society** |
|---|---|---|
| Chief institution | **Corporation is the chief economic and social institution** | **Corporation has a less central place** |
| Work | **Machine-oriented**, leading to **conflict between capital and labour** | **Work relations are more people-oriented** |
| Employment | ⚠️ Industry predominates | **A lesser proportion of the labour force works in industry than in services** |
| Elites | ⚠️ Owners and managers | **Scientific and technical elites play prominent role** |
| Esteem | ⚠️ Property | **Universities, research institutes and knowledge in general are held in high esteem** |
| Stratification | ⚠️ Property | **Merit rather than property is the principle of social stratification** |
| Conflict | **Class warfare** | **Group competition replaces class warfare** |
| Economy and politics | ⚠️ — | **The economic function is subordinated to the political order because the government is responsible for planning** |
| Danger | ⚠️ — | ✅ **This trend challenges some aspects of democracy and constitutes a danger to individual rights** |

🔑 The last row is the chapter's own warning and the reason this section is not merely descriptive. ⚠️ Bell's post-industrial society is governed through **experts and the bureaucracy**, with the state intervening **for a broad variety of reasons** — and the chapter immediately identifies the cost to democracy and rights.

✅ The chapter's summary: advanced industrial societies are **characterized by the expansion of service sector**, with more people employed in **hotels, restaurants, shops, motor garages, transport and tourism, communication, entertainment and advertising jobs** than in factories; **government planning and regulation is extended to every part of public life**, which **may provide for some protection to ordinary people, but it may also involve strict vigilance on individual's life and some restriction on his freedom**.

⚠️ That final balanced sentence is the chapter's characteristic move — a benefit and a cost in the same breath — and it belongs in any answer on this section.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The chapter presents a dossier of convergence and then a list of criticisms without adjudicating. ⚠️ A reader is left with two opposed positions and no verdict, which is unhelpful in an examination where a view is required.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter does indicate a leaning, though not by assertion. ✅ It gives the critics the last word in the section, it endorses the specific warning that the post-industrial trend **challenges some aspects of democracy and constitutes a danger to individual rights**, and it closes with the two-sided judgment about **strict vigilance on individual's life**. ⚠️ That is an evaluative position: convergence describes something real and should not be universalised.

⚠️ **Residual.** The leaning is implicit and the chapter never says which parts of the convergence evidence survive the criticisms. ⚠️ Nor does it ask whether the critics' point about **cultural and ideological factors** is compatible with its own earlier claim at Lesson 27 that **practical necessity** overrode socialist internationalism. ⚠️ Both gaps are recorded.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the chapter's only sustained engagement with development theory and supplies named authorities with dates.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Does industrialisation override ideology?* — the dossier, then the six criticisms, then Bell's warning.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting the convergence thesis as the chapter's own view. ✅ It is attributed to **western writers** and then criticised.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Name three authorities with their works and years; precision here is cheap and highly visible.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Western writers** argued that development is **determined by the level of their industrialization and consequent modernization, and not by their ideological commitment**.
2. ✅ Hence **the western democracies and the communist states would follow a similar course of development**.
3. ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)** observed that **all modern societies were prone to developing a 'legal-rational' model of bureaucracy**.
4. ✅ **James Burnham (1907-87)**, *Managerial Revolution* (**1942**): **the rise of a new class of managers was common to capitalist, communist and mixed economies**.
5. ✅ This symbolised **the shift of power from the legal owner of the means of production to the effective manager**.
6. ✅ Burnham: **the war of 1939... was the first great war of managerial society as the war of 1914... was the last great war of capitalist society**.
7. ✅ **W.W. Rostow** (*The Stages of Economic Growth*; **1960**), **J.K. Galbraith** (*The New Industrial State*; **1964**) and **Simon Kuznets** (*Economic Growth and Structure*; **1965**) argued for **a uniform process involving definite stages of growth**.
8. ✅ Their common characteristics include **efficient technology use, specialization of tasks, skilled and educated manpower, a rising industrial and service ratio, material incentives, complex organizational structures** and **similar patterns of management, planning and policy objectives**.
9. ✅ **Marion J. Levy, Jr.** (*Modernization and the Structure of Society*; **1966**) and **Talcott Parsons** (*The System of Modern Societies*; **1971**) cited **universal structural constraints**.
10. ✅ Their characteristics: **urbanization, expanding educational systems, similar occupational prestige and mobility patterns, falling birth and death rates, widespread electronic communication networks**.
11. ✅ Advanced societies also show **scientific rationalism over superstition, universalism over particularism, individualism over collectivism, standards of achievement over nepotism**, plus **extension of citizenship, increased political participation, and the expansion of individual liberties**.
12. ✅ Critics: the characteristics **focus on the western model**, **underplay... cultural and ideological factors** and **the strength of leadership**, are **futile to look for... in socialist countries and the third world**, and **project 'market society model' as a universal model of development**.
13. ✅ That model would **work to the advantage of western democracies at the expense of other countries**.
14. ✅ **Daniel Bell** (*The Coming of Post-Industrial Society*; **1974**), **a votary of the 'end of ideology' thesis**, contrasts industrial and post-industrial society.
15. ✅ In post-industrial society **merit rather than property is the principle of social stratification** and **group competition replaces class warfare**, but the trend **challenges some aspects of democracy and constitutes a danger to individual rights**.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the convergence thesis and the strongest criticism of it that the chapter reports, and explain why the two are not disputes about facts.

**Model answer:** ✅ The thesis is that **the course of development in the advanced industrial countries is determined by the level of their industrialization and consequent modernization, and not by their ideological commitment**, so that **the western democracies and the communist states would follow a similar course of development**. ✅ The strongest criticism is that the theory **is largely designed to project 'market society model' as a universal model of development which would work to the advantage of western democracies at the expense of other countries**. ⚠️ The dispute is not factual, because both sides accept that industrial societies display common features such as **urbanization**, **specialization of tasks** and **expanding educational systems**. ⚠️ What is contested is their status: the thesis treats them as **universal structural constraints**, while the critics treat them as one model presented as universal, and object that the theory **underplays the role of cultural and ideological factors as well as the strength of leadership**. 🔑 The argument is about the explanatory weight of ideas, not about what industrial societies look like.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not attribute the convergence thesis to the chapter. ⚠️ It reports that **western writers have been trying to demonstrate** it, gives the critics the final word, and itself endorses the warning that the post-industrial trend **challenges some aspects of democracy and constitutes a danger to individual rights**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the claim that advanced industrial societies converge irrespective of ideology. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Western writers** held that development is **determined by the level of their industrialization and consequent modernization, and not by their ideological commitment**, so **the western democracies and the communist states would follow a similar course**. ✅ The evidence assembled is substantial: **Weber** on the **'legal-rational' model of bureaucracy**; **Burnham** (*Managerial Revolution*, **1942**) on a **new class of managers... common to capitalist, communist and mixed economies**; **Rostow** (**1960**), **Galbraith** (**1964**) and **Kuznets** (**1965**) on **a uniform process involving definite stages of growth**; and **Levy** (**1966**) and **Parsons** (**1971**) on **universal structural constraints** producing **urbanization, expanding educational systems... falling birth and death rates**. ⚠️ The dispute concerns the status of these shared features rather than their existence: convergence theorists read them as evidence of a common destination, while critics read the same features as compatible with very different regimes.

**Rubric [10]:** the thesis stated with its negative clause about ideology **2** · at least three named authorities with works or years **3** · at least three common characteristics **1** · at least three of the six criticisms **3** · a closing line locating the dispute correctly **1**.

## Lesson 37 — Trends in the Third World

Progress: 37 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part X — contemporary trends

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
