### 1. ⚠️ A standing caution for this lesson

⚠️ ⚠️ This lesson carries more dated political and economic claims than any other in the file, all of them from a **2009** edition. ✅ Every statement below is reported as the chapter's, with its own dating where the chapter supplies it. ❌ **No claim here is presented as current status**, ❌ no figure is updated, and ❌ no event after the chapter's edition is introduced.

### 2. Staggered independence, unequal development

✅ The chapter's opening:

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **The third world largely comprises of developing countries**, mostly in **Asia, Africa and Latin America** | — |
| They achieved independence **at various points of time after the Second World War (1939-45)** | — |
| **Although Latin American countries had attained formal independence in early nineteenth century** | ⚠️ The exception the chapter itself flags |
| They were at **different levels of 'underdevelopment'** at independence | ⚠️ Inverted commas are the chapter's |
| **Different countries made progress at different rate. They are now at different levels of 'development'.** | ⚠️ Heterogeneity is the theme |

🔑 The structural point of this whole section is that the third world is **not** a uniform category, and the chapter demonstrates it four separate times — in independence dates, in development levels, in political regimes and in the failure of the North-South geography.

### 3. From non-alignment to linkage

✅ **Most third world countries remained politically 'non-aligned' in the era of the 'cold war', but most of them have now been economically 'linked' with the developed world thanks to the all-pervading process of 'globalization'.**

```text
   POLITICALLY 'NON-ALIGNED'   (cold war era)
              |
   ECONOMICALLY 'LINKED'       (globalisation)
              |
   => independence of alignment did NOT survive as
      independence of economic relation
```

⚠️ The contrast is the chapter's own and is pointed. ✅ Political distance was achievable; economic distance was not. ⚠️ That is Lesson 28's neo-colonialism argument restated in the vocabulary of globalisation, and the connection is worth making explicitly.

✅ On the socialist path: some countries **had independently adopted socialist path without seeking alignment with the second world**, and **the collapse of socialism in the Eastern Europe and the former Soviet Union (1989-91) had a profound impact on their mode of thought**.

| ✅ Relinquished socialism **on their own** | ✅ Retained socialist systems, **probably with a more liberal outlook** |
|---|---|
| **Albania, Yugoslavia, Mongolia and Yemen** | **China, Vietnam, North Korea and Cuba** |

✅ Of the latter, **China—the largest and the most important among them—has shown keen interest in the process of liberalization and globalization**.

### 4. The chapter's account of China, reported as written

✅ The chapter devotes a paragraph to China, and it is reported here with its own hedges intact:

| ✅ Statement | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|
| **China gained independence in 1949 and emerged as a big communist power** | ⚠️ The chapter's wording |
| **It did not join the communist camp, nor took interest in the 'non-aligned' movement** | ⚠️ As written |
| **It always proclaimed itself to be part of the third world although non-aligned nations viewed this claim with suspicion** | ⚠️ Attribution of the suspicion is the chapter's |
| **It is true that China did not qualify as a superpower, yet as a country with one billion people—largest in any country of the world, bountiful natural resources, and nuclear hardware is, by any standard, a great power** | ⚠️ A **2009** assessment |
| **Incidentally, India and Pakistan have also become nuclear powers although Pakistan's economic condition remains deplorable** | ⚠️ A **2009** assessment |

⚠️ ❌ These are not updated, corroborated or extended here. ✅ They are the chapter's claims about the period it was written in, and an answer that uses them should date them explicitly. ➡️ Current status belongs to the **International Relations** owners.

### 5. The Brandt Commission, and the geography that fails

✅ **Economic division of the world was sought to be formalized by the Report of the Willy Brandt Commission, entitled *North-South: A Program for Survival* (1980).**

| ✅ Element | ✅ Detail |
|---|---|
| Report | ***North-South: A Program for Survival*** |
| Year | **1980** |
| Distinction | **Two hemispheres: North, which was economically more advanced; and South, which was less developed** |
| Effect | **Paved the way for evolving more meaningful relation between rich and poor countries** |
| Issues raised | **North-South dialogue and South-South cooperation** |

✅ And then the chapter's own refutation of the geography:

> However, technically this division was not found correct. There are some well-to-do nations, like Australia and New Zealand, and even South Africa, south of the equator; and some poor nations, like India and the rest of South Asia (Pakistan, Bangladesh, Nepal, Sri Lanka, Bhutan and Maldives), most of South-East Asia, the Caribbean, Central America, and the northern region of South America are in the north.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.172 (printed 155)

```text
   'NORTH' = RICH   /   'SOUTH' = POOR
              |
   BUT: well-to-do nations SOUTH of the equator
          Australia · New Zealand · even South Africa
        poor nations IN THE NORTH
          India and the rest of South Asia
            (Pakistan, Bangladesh, Nepal, Sri Lanka,
             Bhutan and Maldives)
          most of South-East Asia · the Caribbean ·
          Central America · the northern region of
          South America
              |
   => "technically this division was not found correct"
```

🔑 This is the chapter undermining its own organising vocabulary, and it is one of the most useful passages in the section. ⚠️ It shows that **North-South** is a metaphor for economic position, not a description of latitude — and a candidate who says so has handled the category rather than merely repeated it.

✅ The chapter adds a further complication: **with the disintegration of the second world, and further disintegration of some members of the former second world, like Czechoslovakia, the category of poor countries is no longer confined to the third world**; and there is **a wide variety of levels of development**, since **some Middle Eastern countries have a higher per capita income than does the United States**.

✅ The spread is illustrated at both ends: the **first generation** of newly industrialising economies — **Taiwan, the Republic of Korea (South Korea), Singapore and Hong Kong (the 'Four Dragons')** — **had penetrated Japanese and Western markets**, with a **second generation** expected to follow, while **sub-Saharan Africa is the most marginalized region of the world**. ✅ The chapter notes parenthetically that **Hong Kong has merged with China since 1997**.

### 6. Political trends: military rule and terrorism

✅ The political side, reported with the chapter's own dates:

| ✅ Trend | ✅ The chapter's instances |
|---|---|
| Military rule, then its supersession | **A large number of third world countries came under military rule because of their political, administrative and economic instability**; **it has been superseded by popular rule in most of those countries** |
| Latin America | **At one time, most of Latin American countries were under military rule. But in late 1990s democracy had been established in the entire region. However, Ecuador experienced military coup in 2000.** |
| Africa | **In Africa, Nigeria had a pretty long history of military rule. It returned to civilian rule in 1999.** |
| Asia | **In Asia, Pakistan returned to military rule in 1999 followed by democratic elections in 2002.** |
| Myanmar | **Military rulers in Myanmar (Burma) have been constantly evading handing over power to people's elected representatives.** |
| Terrorism | **Ethnic bias and fundamentalist attitude, combined with the widespread frustration of the youth on economic front**, have **given rise to terrorism at a large scale, threatening many parts of the world** |
| Named event | **Destruction of the World Trade Centre at New York in 2001, involving hundreds of casualties, was one of the most heinous acts of terrorism** |

⚠️ ❌ All of these are **2009** statements and are not updated here. ✅ The analytically durable part is the chapter's causal claim about terrorism: it identifies three contributing conditions — **ethnic bias**, **fundamentalist attitude** and **the widespread frustration of the youth on economic front** — and the third is economic, consistent with the chapter's method throughout.

✅ The closing demand: **third world countries will not only have to solve their economic problems but also ensure an end to exploitation of vulnerable sections of people both at national and international levels.**

🔑 Note **both at national and international levels**. ⚠️ Once again the chapter refuses to externalise the problem: domestic exploitation is named alongside international exploitation, exactly as at Lesson 27.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The section is a catalogue of dated instances held together by a category — the third world — that the chapter itself shows to be incoherent. ⚠️ If independence dates, development levels, political regimes and geography all vary within it, the category classifies nothing.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter is aware of this and says so: the division **became partly outdated**, the geography **was not found correct**, and poverty **is no longer confined to the third world**. ⚠️ What holds the category together, on its own account, is not a shared condition but a shared history — **independence from colonial domination** — and a shared task, **a more vigorous focus on development**. ✅ That is a weaker but defensible basis.

⚠️ **Residual.** The weaker basis is not stated by the chapter in those terms, so the reply is a reconstruction. ⚠️ And the section's reliance on dated instances means that its illustrative content ages faster than its analysis. ⚠️ The honest course, adopted here, is to keep the analysis and date the instances.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The failure of the North-South geography is the chapter's best demonstration that development categories are economic rather than spatial.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is the third world a useful category?* — heterogeneity four times over, then the shared-history defence.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting the chapter's country instances as current fact. ✅ Date them.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the Brandt Commission with its exact title and year, then immediately the chapter's refutation of its geography; the pairing is distinctive.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The third world achieved independence **at various points of time after the Second World War (1939-45)**, though **Latin American countries had attained formal independence in early nineteenth century**; they were at **different levels of 'underdevelopment'** and are **now at different levels of 'development'**.
2. ✅ Most remained **politically 'non-aligned'** but are **now economically 'linked' with the developed world** through **'globalization'**.
3. ✅ **The collapse of socialism in the Eastern Europe and the former Soviet Union (1989-91)** affected their **mode of thought**.
4. ✅ **Albania, Yugoslavia, Mongolia and Yemen** relinquished socialist systems **on their own**.
5. ✅ **China, Vietnam, North Korea and Cuba** retained them, **probably with a more liberal outlook**.
6. ✅ **China... has shown keen interest in the process of liberalization and globalization.**
7. ✅ **Report of the Willy Brandt Commission**, ***North-South: A Program for Survival* (1980)**, distinguished **North, which was economically more advanced** from **South, which was less developed**.
8. ✅ It raised **North-South dialogue and South-South cooperation**.
9. ✅ **However, technically this division was not found correct** — **Australia and New Zealand, and even South Africa** lie south; **India and the rest of South Asia**, **most of South-East Asia**, **the Caribbean**, **Central America** and **the northern region of South America** lie north.
10. ✅ **The category of poor countries is no longer confined to the third world**, and **some Middle Eastern countries have a higher per capita income than does the United States**.
11. ✅ The **'Four Dragons'** — **Taiwan, the Republic of Korea (South Korea), Singapore and Hong Kong** — **had penetrated Japanese and Western markets**; **Hong Kong has merged with China since 1997**.
12. ✅ **Sub-Saharan Africa is the most marginalized region of the world.**
13. ✅ Military rule was widespread and **has been superseded by popular rule in most of those countries**; the chapter dates **Ecuador 2000**, **Nigeria 1999**, **Pakistan 1999 and 2002**.
14. ✅ **Ethnic bias and fundamentalist attitude, combined with the widespread frustration of the youth on economic front**, produced **terrorism at a large scale**; the chapter names the **destruction of the World Trade Centre at New York in 2001**.
15. ✅ Third world countries must end **exploitation of vulnerable sections of people both at national and international levels**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter introduces the North-South division and then rejects it. Explain the rejection and what it implies about how such categories should be used.

**Model answer:** ✅ The division was formalised by the **Report of the Willy Brandt Commission**, ***North-South: A Program for Survival* (1980)**, distinguishing **North, which was economically more advanced** from **South, which was less developed**, and it usefully generated **North-South dialogue and South-South cooperation**. ✅ The chapter nonetheless states that **technically this division was not found correct**, because **well-to-do nations, like Australia and New Zealand, and even South Africa** lie **south of the equator**, while **poor nations, like India and the rest of South Asia**, **most of South-East Asia, the Caribbean, Central America, and the northern region of South America are in the north**. ⚠️ The implication is that **North** and **South** function as names for economic position, not for latitude. ⚠️ The chapter reinforces the point by noting that **the category of poor countries is no longer confined to the third world** and that **some Middle Eastern countries have a higher per capita income than does the United States**. 🔑 Such categories should be used as shorthand for economic relations and never as geographical descriptions.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the third world as an economically uniform bloc. ⚠️ The chapter documents **a wide variety of levels of development**, from the **'Four Dragons'** that **had penetrated Japanese and Western markets** to **sub-Saharan Africa**, described as **the most marginalized region of the world**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss the trends in the third world after the end of the cold war, as the chapter describes them. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Most third world countries **remained politically 'non-aligned' in the era of the 'cold war', but... have now been economically 'linked' with the developed world** through **'globalization'**. ✅ **The collapse of socialism in the Eastern Europe and the former Soviet Union (1989-91)** led **Albania, Yugoslavia, Mongolia and Yemen** to relinquish it, while **China, Vietnam, North Korea and Cuba** retained it **probably with a more liberal outlook**. ✅ The **Brandt Commission**'s ***North-South: A Program for Survival* (1980)** formalised an economic division, though **technically this division was not found correct**. ✅ Development is uneven — the **'Four Dragons'** at one end, **sub-Saharan Africa**, **the most marginalized region**, at the other. ✅ Politically, military rule **has been superseded by popular rule in most of those countries**, while **ethnic bias and fundamentalist attitude, combined with the widespread frustration of the youth**, produced large-scale terrorism. ⚠️ All instances are **2009** statements.

**Rubric [10]:** the non-aligned-to-linked contrast **2** · the socialist retreats with both lists **2** · the Brandt Commission with exact title and year **2** · the rejection of the geography with at least two counter-instances **2** · the development spread from Four Dragons to sub-Saharan Africa **1** · the political trends with a dated qualifier **1**.

## Lesson 38 — Civil society returns: two senses, and Tocqueville

Progress: 38 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part XI — civil society in contemporary discourse

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6, §8 and §13, with the Advanced companion's §5 and §8, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.173-174 (printed 156-157), which supply the two contemporary senses, Tocqueville's diagnosis and remedy, the five functions of voluntary associations, the two quotation panels and the Held definition.
CA search: "voluntary associations schools of democracy civic participation Tocqueville 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ the single dated anchor in this file is placed at Lesson 39, where the chapter's own demand for associational independence arises; ❌ nothing is recorded here.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The two contemporary senses

✅ The chapter returns to civil society after thirty pages, and divides the contemporary usage in two:

| ✅ Sense | ✅ What it comprises | ✅ Relation to the state |
|---|---|---|
| **One** | **Social institutions like school, church and peer groups of citizens which serve as structures of legitimation of the state** | **These institutions largely lend support to the state**; ✅ **corresponds to Gramsci's view of its role in sustaining the capitalist system** |
| **Two** | **A set of public interest organizations set up by some conscious citizens which make various demands on the state or launch social movements to mobilize ordinary citizens on the way to social reform** | ⚠️ Demand-making; **the state must respond promptly to their demands in order to ensure smooth functioning of society** |

```text
   CIVIL SOCIETY (contemporary)
        |
   +----+--------------------------+
   |                               |
 SENSE 1: LEGITIMATION        SENSE 2: DEMAND-MAKING
 school · church · peer       public interest organizations
 groups                       set up by CONSCIOUS CITIZENS
 "largely LEND SUPPORT to     make DEMANDS on the state
  the state"                  launch SOCIAL MOVEMENTS
 = Gramsci's view             mobilize citizens toward
                              SOCIAL REFORM
        \                            /
         \                          /
   THE SAME TERM, TWO OPPOSITE POLITICAL FUNCTIONS
```

🔑 This is the single most important distinction in the civil-society sequence, and it is the chapter's own. ⚠️ It means that no general claim about civil society — that it supports freedom, or that it reproduces domination — can be true of the whole. ✅ The chapter adds that **the role of civil society in this sense**, the second, **has assumed special significance in recent years**.

⚠️ Note that Sense 1 is explicitly identified with Gramsci, whose account was taught at Lesson 15. ✅ The chapter is therefore not abandoning the critical reading when it turns to the celebratory one; it is placing them side by side as two senses of one term.

### 2. Tocqueville's diagnosis

✅ **Present-day concept of civil society closely corresponds to Tocqueville's view on the role of 'intermediate voluntary associations'.**

✅ **Alexis de Tocqueville (1805-59)**, a **French philosopher**, in **Democracy in America (1835-40)**, argued:

| Step | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| Premise | **With the dissolution of aristocracy in Europe an alternative for the pluralist dispersion of power was urgently needed** |
| The old order | **In the medieval Europe, there were three centres of power: Clergy, Nobility and Commoners** |
| How it worked | **Clergy and Nobility enjoyed substantial powers in their respective fields**; **Commoners could also make their voice heard at the decision-making level because of their large numbers** |
| The change | **With the coming of democracy, old centres of power had been destroyed** |
| The danger | **Power was now concentrating in the hands of majority. This led to the danger of tyranny of majority.** |

```text
   MEDIEVAL: three centres of power
     CLERGY · NOBILITY · COMMONERS
        -> power DISPERSED
              |
   DEMOCRACY: old centres DESTROYED
        -> power CONCENTRATING IN THE HANDS OF MAJORITY
              |
        => DANGER OF TYRANNY OF MAJORITY
              |
   REMEDY: a vigorous system of VOLUNTARY ASSOCIATIONS
           as COUNTERWEIGHTS TO THE STATE POWER
```

⚠️ The argument is counter-intuitive and that is its value. ✅ Tocqueville's worry is not that democracy gives too little power to the people but that it removes the *intermediate* bodies that formerly stood between ruler and ruled. 🔑 Civil society is therefore proposed as a **functional replacement for aristocracy** — a dispersal of power achieved by voluntary means rather than by birth.

✅ The chapter supports this with a quotation panel: **When a nation abolishes aristocracy, centralization follows as a matter of course.** — **Alexis de Tocqueville, *The Ancien Regime* (1856)**.

⚠️ Note that the chapter cites two different Tocqueville works, with different dates: ***Democracy in America* (1835-40)** and ***The Ancien Regime* (1856)**. ✅ Reproducing both accurately is a mark of care.

### 3. The five functions of voluntary associations

✅ Tocqueville's remedy is specified:

| # | ✅ Function |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Act as counterweights to the state power** |
| 2 | **Crystallize and publicize opinions and interests which would otherwise go unheard** |
| 3 | **Stimulate collective self-help rather than reliance on state initiative** |
| 4 | **Draw people into cooperative ventures, breaking down their social isolation and making them aware of their wider social responsibilities** |
| 5 | **Function as 'schools of democracy', instilling habits of civic virtue and public spirit into their members** |

✅ In short, these associations **would serve as an effective instrument of defence of individual liberty and encourage close cooperation between the citizens to solve their common problems**.

🔑 Function 5 — **'schools of democracy'** — is the phrase to memorise, and it is in the chapter's own inverted commas. ⚠️ Note that it is *educative*: associations do not merely check power, they produce the dispositions a democracy needs.

⚠️ Function 3 deserves attention because it cuts against a common assumption. ✅ Tocqueville's associations **stimulate collective self-help rather than reliance on state initiative** — so civil society here reduces demands on the state rather than multiplying them. ⚠️ That is the opposite of Sense 2 above, where civil society **make[s] various demands on the state**. ✅ The chapter contains both and does not reconcile them, which is further evidence that the term covers genuinely different things.

✅ The chapter adds Tocqueville's preferences: he was **an ardent champion of freedom of association** and **earnestly hoped that free political parties and a free press would prove to be most effective among these voluntary associations**. ✅ And it updates the list: **in the contemporary context, various interest groups and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) could be added**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the only place in the chapter where NGOs are mentioned**, and they appear as a late addition to a list headed by **free political parties** and **a free press**. ❌ The common equation of civil society with NGOs is therefore a distortion of the chapter's own emphasis.

✅ A second quotation panel follows: **What is understood by republican government in the United States is the slow and quiet action of society upon itself.** — **Alexis de Tocqueville, *Democracy in America* (1835-40)**.

### 4. Civil society as an organ of democracy

✅ The chapter's own summary of the contemporary position:

| ✅ Claim |
|---|
| Civil society **includes a wide range of associations and social movements** |
| These **provide ample opportunities to the citizens to develop their capacities and express their varying interests and diverse identities** |
| It **creates an atmosphere where the citizens are able to enjoy some level of autonomy or independence from government control or influence** |
| It **promotes a moral sense of obligation among the citizens and motivates them to participate in civic causes** |
| It **discourages their dependence on the government for the solution of their common problems** |
| ✅ **Thus it serves as the true source of democratization** |

⚠️ The phrase **diverse identities** is worth flagging. 🧩 It is the only point at which the chapter's civil-society discussion touches the ground the second source occupies at Lessons 43-45, where recognition of **cultural difference** becomes the central question. ✅ The chapter does not develop it.

### 5. Held's definition

✅ The chapter supplies a set-off definition:

> Civil society connotes those areas of social life—the domestic world, the economic sphere, cultural activities and political interaction—which are organized by private or voluntary arrangements between individuals and groups outside the direct control of the state.
>
> — David Held, *Political Theory and the Modern State* (1989), as quoted at Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.174 (printed 157)

| ✅ Four areas named | ⚠️ Note |
|---|---|
| **The domestic world** | ⚠️ Includes the family — contrast Hegel at Lesson 14, who placed the family *outside* civil society |
| **The economic sphere** | ⚠️ Retains the Hegelian-Marxian content |
| **Cultural activities** | ⚠️ Retains the Gramscian content |
| **Political interaction** | ⚠️ Outside the state, yet political |

✅ The organising criterion: **private or voluntary arrangements between individuals and groups outside the direct control of the state**.

🔑 Held's definition is the broadest in the chapter, and it is broad by design: it is defined by a *negative* criterion — outside direct state control — rather than by any positive content. ⚠️ That breadth is why it can include the family, the economy and culture at once, and why it absorbs every earlier sense except the classical one in which civil society *was* the state.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The Tocquevillian case is a non-sequitur. ⚠️ Associations formed voluntarily by the energetic and articulate are not a substitute for aristocracy's dispersal of power, because they reproduce existing advantages: those with time, money and education associate more effectively. ⚠️ A civil society of this kind may concentrate influence rather than disperse it.

✅ **Reply.** Tocqueville's own functions anticipate part of this. ✅ Associations are said to **crystallize and publicize opinions and interests which would otherwise go unheard** — the whole point being to give voice to those without it — and to **break down their social isolation**, which addresses exclusion directly. ✅ Function 5 adds that they are **'schools of democracy'**, so participation is supposed to create capacity rather than presuppose it.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply states aspirations, not safeguards. ⚠️ The chapter supplies no account of unequal associational capacity, and its own later material at Lesson 40 concedes that **some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies** — which is the objection conceded in the chapter's own words, two pages later.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The two-sense division and Tocqueville's five functions are the core of any contemporary civil-society answer.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Discuss civil society as a condition of democracy.* — two senses, Tocqueville's diagnosis, the five functions, Held's definition.
- ❌ **Trap.** Equating civil society with NGOs. ✅ They are a late addition to a list headed by **free political parties** and **a free press**.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the tyranny-of-majority diagnosis; it shows why the remedy is associational rather than constitutional.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Sense one: **social institutions like school, church and peer groups of citizens which serve as structures of legitimation of the state**, which **largely lend support to the state** and **correspond[] to Gramsci's view of its role in sustaining the capitalist system**.
2. ✅ Sense two: **a set of public interest organizations set up by some conscious citizens which make various demands on the state or launch social movements**.
3. ✅ **The state must respond promptly to their demands in order to ensure smooth functioning of society.**
4. ✅ **Alexis de Tocqueville (1805-59)**, *Democracy in America* (**1835-40**), held that **with the dissolution of aristocracy in Europe an alternative for the pluralist dispersion of power was urgently needed**.
5. ✅ **In the medieval Europe, there were three centres of power: Clergy, Nobility and Commoners.**
6. ✅ **With the coming of democracy, old centres of power had been destroyed**, and **power was now concentrating in the hands of majority**, creating **the danger of tyranny of majority**.
7. ✅ The remedy: **a vigorous system of voluntary associations could act as counterweights to the state power**.
8. ✅ They **crystallize and publicize opinions and interests which would otherwise go unheard**.
9. ✅ They **stimulate collective self-help rather than reliance on state initiative**.
10. ✅ They **draw people into cooperative ventures, breaking down their social isolation**.
11. ✅ They **function as 'schools of democracy', instilling habits of civic virtue and public spirit**.
12. ✅ Tocqueville **earnestly hoped that free political parties and a free press would prove to be most effective**; **interest groups and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) could be added** in the contemporary context.
13. ✅ Panel: **When a nation abolishes aristocracy, centralization follows as a matter of course** — *The Ancien Regime* (**1856**).
14. ✅ **David Held**, *Political Theory and the Modern State* (**1989**): civil society connotes **the domestic world, the economic sphere, cultural activities and political interaction** organised by **private or voluntary arrangements... outside the direct control of the state**.
15. 🔑 Civil society **serves as the true source of democratization**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain Tocqueville's reason for thinking that democracy *needs* voluntary associations, and why the reason is not the obvious one.

**Model answer:** ✅ His reason is that democracy destroys the intermediate bodies that formerly dispersed power. ✅ **With the dissolution of aristocracy in Europe an alternative for the pluralist dispersion of power was urgently needed**, because **in the medieval Europe, there were three centres of power: Clergy, Nobility and Commoners**, and **with the coming of democracy, old centres of power had been destroyed**, so that **power was now concentrating in the hands of majority**, producing **the danger of tyranny of majority**. ⚠️ The obvious reason would be that associations help citizens influence government; Tocqueville's reason is the opposite in direction — associations are needed to *limit* the power that democracy itself concentrates. ✅ Hence they **act as counterweights to the state power**, **crystallize and publicize opinions and interests which would otherwise go unheard**, **stimulate collective self-help rather than reliance on state initiative**, and **function as 'schools of democracy'**. 🔑 Civil society is proposed as a voluntary successor to aristocracy's dispersal of power.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat civil society as uniformly oppositional to the state. ⚠️ The chapter's first contemporary sense comprises institutions that **largely lend support to the state** and correspond to **Gramsci's view**, while the second makes **demands on the state**; and Tocqueville's third function actually reduces **reliance on state initiative** rather than increasing claims upon it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the contemporary conception of civil society and its relation to democracy. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The term carries **two senses**: institutions **like school, church and peer groups** that **serve as structures of legitimation of the state** (**Gramsci**); and **public interest organizations... which make various demands on the state or launch social movements**. ✅ The democratic case rests on **Tocqueville**: **with the dissolution of aristocracy... an alternative for the pluralist dispersion of power was urgently needed**, since democracy destroyed the medieval **Clergy, Nobility and Commoners** and concentrated power **in the hands of majority**, risking **tyranny of majority**. ✅ Voluntary associations **act as counterweights to the state power**, **crystallize and publicize opinions... which would otherwise go unheard**, **stimulate collective self-help** and **function as 'schools of democracy'**. ✅ **Held**: civil society is **the domestic world, the economic sphere, cultural activities and political interaction** organised **outside the direct control of the state**. ✅ Hence it **serves as the true source of democratization**.

**Rubric [10]:** both contemporary senses, with the Gramsci identification **3** · Tocqueville dated with the aristocracy-dispersal premise and the tyranny-of-majority danger **3** · at least three of the five functions including **'schools of democracy'** **2** · Held's definition with at least three of its four areas **1** · the true-source-of-democratization conclusion **1**.

## Lesson 39 — Held, Cohen-Arato, Hirst, Putnam — and the East European use

Progress: 39 / 45 | Stage: Advanced | Part XI — civil society in contemporary discourse

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6, §8 and §13, with the Advanced companion's §5 and §8, read against Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.175-176 (printed 158-159), which supply Cohen and Arato, Hirst, Putnam, the anti-totalitarian use of civil society, the Solidarity and Ecoglasnost instances, the 1989 revolutions and the boxed definitions of the totalitarian state and new social movements.
CA search: "civil society organisations independence from state and from trade and industry regulation of funding India"
CA found: 📰 **Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Amendment Act, 2020 — No. 33 of 2020, assented 28 September 2020, administered by the Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of India.** ✅ Carried once, as this file's single dated anchor, solely because the chapter's closing demand on this page is that associations be **independent from the state as well as from trade and industry**.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Three refinements of the concept

✅ The chapter names three theorists in sequence, each adding something the previous one lacked:

| Theorist | ✅ Work and year | ✅ Core claim |
|---|---|---|
| **Jean L. Cohen and Andrew Arato** | ***Civil Society and Political Theory*** (**1992**) | Civil society is **an area of public activity distinct from both the state and the market** |
| **Paul Hirst**, **a British academic** | ***Associative Democracy: New Forms of Economic and Social Governance*** (**1994**) | Civil society is **a set of voluntary associations which would be the primary bases of democracy** |
| **Robert Putnam**, **an American social scientist** | **'Bowling Alone: America's Declining Social Capital'**, in ***The Global Resurgence of Democracy***, edited by **Larry Diamond and Marc F. Plattner** (**1998**) | Associations create **'social capital'** |

### 2. Cohen and Arato — the third sphere

✅ Their definition is distinguished by a double exclusion: civil society is distinct **from both the state and the market**. ✅ The area **involves a range of groups and associations, including families**, and is **regarded to be essential for a healthy democratic society**, since **it permits participation and communicative interaction of individuals**.

✅ The crucial limitation: **this field of social life is designed to supplement the political institutions of representative democracy rather than serve as a replacement thereof**.

```text
   STATE  |  CIVIL SOCIETY  |  MARKET
             groups, associations,
             INCLUDING FAMILIES
                  |
          participation +
          COMMUNICATIVE INTERACTION
                  |
   ROLE: to SUPPLEMENT the political institutions of
         REPRESENTATIVE DEMOCRACY
         -- NOT to REPLACE them
```

🔑 The supplement-not-replacement clause is the most examinable sentence in the lesson, because it blocks the strongest version of the civil-society claim. ⚠️ Civil society does not displace elections, legislatures or parties; it adds a mode of participation alongside them.

⚠️ Note also that **families** are included. ✅ The chapter has now presented three different placements of the family — Hegel put it outside civil society (L14), Gramsci put it inside as an institution of legitimation (L15), and Cohen and Arato include it among the associations of a democratic third sphere. ⚠️ The chapter does not comment on the divergence; recording it is accurate reading.

🔶 **Owner extension.** The canonical owners add, beyond the chapter's page, the mediating apparatus Cohen and Arato propose — "political society" of parties and parliaments and "economic society" of regulated markets and unions — as the channels through which civil-society demands reach state and market. 🔶 It is marked as owner-supplied; ❌ the chapter's page does not carry it.

### 3. Hirst — associative democracy

✅ Hirst goes further than Cohen and Arato in one respect and agrees with them in another:

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ Direction |
|---|---|
| **Self-governing associations would perform public functions** | ⚠️ Further — associations take over governing tasks |
| This **would not only reduce the burden on the central state, but also curtail its power** | ⚠️ Further — a deliberate reduction of the state |
| **The goals of democracy would be achieved primarily through these associations rather than through a centralized state** | ⚠️ Further — primacy shifts |
| **The role of the state would be reduced to supervising and regulating the voluntary associations of civil society** | ⚠️ Further — the state becomes a regulator |
| ✅ Yet **the associations of civil society would only supplement the representative democracy rather than replace it** | ✅ Agreement with Cohen and Arato |

⚠️ The last row is important and easily missed. ✅ Hirst's model reassigns functions without abolishing representative institutions. 🔑 Both theorists therefore converge on the same limit, which is the chapter's way of saying that no serious contemporary theorist proposes civil society as a substitute for democratic government.

### 4. Putnam — social capital, and a lament

✅ **Social capital** is defined as **a set of social practices which involve civic engagement and ideas of reciprocity**. ✅ Putnam **firmly believes that such a network of civic involvement is necessary for an effective democracy**.

✅ The lament:

> However, he laments that in the past two or three decades the Americans have forgotten 'the art of pursuing in common the objects of common desires', which was recognized and admired by Alexis de Tocqueville. Putnam has pointed out that the present-day American society is characterized by a reduction in citizens' activity in the associations of civil society, with a consequent decline in the quality of American democracy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.175 (printed 158)

```text
   SOCIAL CAPITAL = civic engagement + reciprocity
              |
   NECESSARY for an EFFECTIVE DEMOCRACY
              |
   BUT: reduction in citizens' activity in associations
              |
   => CONSEQUENT DECLINE IN THE QUALITY OF
      AMERICAN DEMOCRACY
```

🔑 Putnam closes the circle opened by Tocqueville at Lesson 38: the quoted phrase, **'the art of pursuing in common the objects of common desires'**, is attributed by Putnam to Tocqueville's own admiration. ✅ The chapter has therefore arranged its contemporary theorists so that the last one returns to the first.

⚠️ Note the logical form of Putnam's claim. ✅ If associational density is **necessary** for effective democracy, then its decline is sufficient for democratic decline — which is why the lament is a political diagnosis and not merely an observation about leisure habits.

🔶 **Owner extension.** The owners add Putnam's earlier comparative study of Italian regional government and its finding that denser horizontal associational life accompanied more effective regional government, together with the standard criticisms that social capital may be bonding rather than bridging and that correlation does not settle causation. 🔶 Marked as owner-supplied; ❌ not on the chapter's page.

### 5. Civil society against the totalitarian state

✅ The chapter's final movement on these pages is historical and political:

| ✅ Stage | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| The problem | **Communist governments in Eastern Europe had demobilized civil society so that rulers could directly control the individual** |
| The realisation | **In 1960s and 1970s it was realized that the institutions of civil society could not only be used to strengthen the authority of the ruling class, but the opposition could also use this device to promote its own viewpoint** |
| The method | **Where the authority of the ruling class could not be challenged at the political level, there the hegemony of the rulers could be undermined through manipulation of education and culture** |
| The use | **In late 1970s and 1980s the device of civil society was widely used in East European socialist countries as a weapon against the all-encompassing claims of the totalitarian state** |
| Instance 1 | **The Solidarity movement in Poland sought to build up the institutions of civil society as a 'parallel society' with a view to safeguarding the interests of workers** |
| Instance 2 | **In Bulgaria, an environmental group called 'Ecoglasnost' raised the issue of the wanton destruction of natural resources and the appalling pollution in industrial centres**; since those centres **were functioning under government control**, it **proved to be an effective organ of civil society as the anti-government movement in Bulgaria** |
| Outcome | **After the successful revolutions of 1989 throughout the Eastern Europe, the concept of civil society gained immense popularity** |
| Transfer | **Western intellectuals also found the concept of civil society as instrumental to the revival of citizen participation in public affairs in democratic societies where it had recently declined** |
| Alliance | **'Civil society movement' in the recent decades has emerged as an ally of 'new social movements'** |

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the counter-hegemonic half of Gramsci's legacy, delivered twenty pages after the first half.** ✅ Lesson 15 showed civil society **sustaining** domination; here the same institutions are used against it, by exactly the mechanism Gramsci identified — **manipulation of education and culture**. ⚠️ Joining the two passages is the single strongest analytical move available on this topic.

⚠️ The Ecoglasnost case is also an instance of Lesson 33's argument: an environmental grievance becomes political precisely because the polluting centres **were functioning under government control**.

### 6. The two boxed definitions

✅ **Totalitarian State — The state that seeks to regulate and control all aspects of life of its citizens—whether public or private. In other words, it seeks to direct all political, economic, social-cultural and intellectual activities of people towards fulfilling certain aims which are determined by the state itself. No citizen has the right or opportunity to oppose or criticize the state, or to propose any new aim.**

✅ **New Social Movements — A diverse set of popular movements in the contemporary world that are characterized by a departure from the conventional methods of political organization and expression. In advanced capitalist societies, these movements have centred around feminist, ecological, peace and anti-nuclear issues. In Africa, Asia and Latin America they have focused on wider issues including women's and human rights, environmental and social issues, and uplift of the downtrodden. New social movements seek to empower the common people and mobilize the institutions of civil society toward the achievement of their common goals without involving the state.**

| ✅ Region | ✅ Issues named by the chapter |
|---|---|
| **Advanced capitalist societies** | **Feminist, ecological, peace and anti-nuclear** |
| **Africa, Asia and Latin America** | **Women's and human rights, environmental and social issues, and uplift of the downtrodden** |

⚠️ Note the closing clause of the second box: these movements act **without involving the state**. ⚠️ That is a strong claim and sits in tension with Sense 2 at Lesson 38, where public-interest organisations **make various demands on the state**. ✅ The chapter supplies both formulations and does not reconcile them.

🔑 The totalitarian-state box is also the chapter's answer to Lesson 11's second reason for distinguishing state from society: it is the precise description of what happens when the distinction collapses.

### 7. 📰 One current linkage, and its exact limits

📰 **Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Amendment Act, 2020**, **No. 33 of 2020**, which received assent on **28 September 2020**, administered by the **Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of India**.

⚠️ It is carried here, once, for one reason only. ✅ The chapter's own closing demand on this page is that **the intellectuals of today wish to establish a vast network of voluntary associations, which would be independent from the state as well as from trade and industry**. 📰 That demand describes a standing question of institutional design — how associational funding and autonomy are regulated — and the 2020 amending Act is a live statutory instrument addressing exactly that question in India.

❌ It is **not** evidence for any claim in the chapter. ❌ It is **not** presented as endorsing or refuting Tocqueville, Hirst or Putnam. ❌ No doctrine, lesson, concept check or practice item in this file depends on it. ❌ No second current linkage appears anywhere in this file, and every other lesson records a truthful nil in its pre-teach checklist. ➡️ Analysis of the Act's provisions and effects belongs to the **Polity** and **Governance** owners, not to this topic.

### 8. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The East European narrative proves less than it appears. ⚠️ Civil society there succeeded against a regime that had **demobilized** it — an unusually weak opponent in associational terms — and its later export to established democracies, where **it had recently declined**, is an argument by analogy rather than by evidence.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's claim is about a *mechanism*, not about a success rate. ✅ What the East European cases show is that **the institutions of civil society could not only be used to strengthen the authority of the ruling class, but the opposition could also use this device** — that the same terrain is available to both sides. ✅ That mechanism is general even if its outcomes are contingent, and the Bulgarian instance illustrates it precisely: an environmental grievance becomes a political weapon because the polluter is the government.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply secures the mechanism and leaves the transfer unargued. ⚠️ The chapter does not explain why a device effective against a state that monopolised organisation should also be effective in a democracy where associations compete freely. ⚠️ Nor does it reconcile the new-social-movements box's **without involving the state** with the demand-making sense of Lesson 38. ⚠️ Both gaps are recorded.

### 9. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** These three theorists plus the East European cases are the chapter's complete contemporary apparatus.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Civil society supplements representative democracy; it does not replace it. Discuss.* — the stem is almost Cohen and Arato's own sentence.
- ❌ **Trap.** Claiming that civil society replaces the state. ✅ Both Cohen-Arato and Hirst explicitly deny it.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Join Lesson 15 to this lesson — legitimation and counter-hegemony — and the answer becomes an argument rather than a survey.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Cohen and Arato**, *Civil Society and Political Theory* (**1992**): civil society is **an area of public activity distinct from both the state and the market**, which **involves a range of groups and associations, including families**, and **permits participation and communicative interaction of individuals**.
2. ✅ It is **designed to supplement the political institutions of representative democracy rather than serve as a replacement thereof**.
3. ✅ **Paul Hirst**, *Associative Democracy* (**1994**): **self-governing associations would perform public functions**, which would **reduce the burden on the central state, but also curtail its power**.
4. ✅ **The goals of democracy would be achieved primarily through these associations rather than through a centralized state.**
5. ✅ **The role of the state would be reduced to supervising and regulating the voluntary associations.**
6. ✅ Hirst too holds that associations **only supplement the representative democracy rather than replace it**.
7. ✅ **Robert Putnam**, **'Bowling Alone: America's Declining Social Capital'** in *The Global Resurgence of Democracy*, eds **Larry Diamond and Marc F. Plattner** (**1998**).
8. ✅ **Social capital** is **a set of social practices which involve civic engagement and ideas of reciprocity**, **necessary for an effective democracy**.
9. ✅ Putnam laments that Americans have forgotten **'the art of pursuing in common the objects of common desires'**, admired by **Tocqueville**, with a **consequent decline in the quality of American democracy**.
10. ✅ **Communist governments in Eastern Europe had demobilized civil society so that rulers could directly control the individual.**
11. ✅ In the **1960s and 1970s** it was realised that **the opposition could also use this device**, undermining **the hegemony of the rulers... through manipulation of education and culture**.
12. ✅ In the **late 1970s and 1980s** civil society became **a weapon against the all-encompassing claims of the totalitarian state**.
13. ✅ **Solidarity** in **Poland** built **a 'parallel society'**; **'Ecoglasnost'** in **Bulgaria** became **the anti-government movement**; **after the successful revolutions of 1989 throughout the Eastern Europe, the concept of civil society gained immense popularity**.
14. ✅ Boxes: the **totalitarian state** seeks **to regulate and control all aspects of life of its citizens—whether public or private**; **new social movements** seek **to empower the common people... without involving the state**.
15. ✅ The chapter's demand: a **vast network of voluntary associations, which would be independent from the state as well as from trade and industry**, able to flourish **only in a democratic set-up where people have complete freedom of speech, association and assembly**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter shows civil society sustaining domination in one place and undermining it in another. Reconstruct both and identify the mechanism common to them.

**Model answer:** ✅ Sustaining: **Gramsci** held that **civil society includes family, school and church which transmit capitalist value-system to the new generation**, embodying **'structures of legitimation'**, so that **capitalist society largely depends on the efficiency of the institutions of civil society for its stability**. ✅ Undermining: **in 1960s and 1970s it was realized that the institutions of civil society could not only be used to strengthen the authority of the ruling class, but the opposition could also use this device to promote its own viewpoint**, since **where the authority of the ruling class could not be challenged at the political level, there the hegemony of the rulers could be undermined through manipulation of education and culture**. ⚠️ The common mechanism is control of the formative institutions — education and culture — through which consent is produced. ✅ Hence **Solidarity** could build **a 'parallel society'** in **Poland**, and **'Ecoglasnost'** could become **the anti-government movement** in **Bulgaria**. 🔑 Civil society is a terrain, not a side.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conclude from Hirst's model that civil society replaces the state or representative institutions. ⚠️ He reduces the state's role **to supervising and regulating the voluntary associations**, yet insists, with Cohen and Arato, that associations **only supplement the representative democracy rather than replace it**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the contribution of recent theorists to the concept of civil society. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ **Cohen and Arato** (*Civil Society and Political Theory*, **1992**) define it as **an area of public activity distinct from both the state and the market**, involving **groups and associations, including families**, which **permits participation and communicative interaction** and is **designed to supplement the political institutions of representative democracy rather than serve as a replacement**. ✅ **Paul Hirst** (*Associative Democracy*, **1994**) proposes that **self-governing associations would perform public functions**, which would **reduce the burden on the central state, but also curtail its power**, leaving the state **to supervising and regulating** them, yet still only **supplement**ing it. ✅ **Robert Putnam** (**1998**) holds that associations create **'social capital'** — **a set of social practices which involve civic engagement and ideas of reciprocity**, **necessary for an effective democracy** — and laments a **reduction in citizens' activity** and **consequent decline in the quality of American democracy**. ⚠️ All three keep representative institutions in place.

**Rubric [10]:** Cohen and Arato with the double exclusion and the supplement clause **3** · Hirst's four reassignments **2** · Hirst's agreement on supplementation **1** · Putnam with the definition of social capital and the necessity claim **2** · Putnam's lament and its democratic consequence **1** · a closing line noting the common limit **1**.

### 1. ⚠️ A disclosure about where this lesson's evidence comes from

⚠️ ⚠️ **Part of this lesson is taught from pages that lie outside the range the canonical owners declare for this topic.**

| ✅ Fact | ✅ Evidence |
|---|---|
| Both canonical owners and the subject's topic map declare this chapter as PDF pp.**147-176** | ⚠️ Owner front matter |
| The chapter in fact continues to PDF p.**178** | ✅ PDF pp.177-178 carry folios **160** and **161**; ✅ p.178 is the recto, with the running head **Concept of the State**, and p.177 the verso, with **An Introduction to Political Theory** |
| Chapter 7 begins at PDF p.**179** | ✅ It carries the chapter number and the title **Concept of Sovereignty** |

⚠️ PDF pp.177-178 therefore contain chapter material that the owners' declared window excludes: the **Comparative Study of the State and Civil Society** table, the section headed **A CRITICAL APPRAISAL**, and the boxed definitions of **Elitism** and **Oligarchy**. ✅ They are taught here because they belong to the chapter. ⚠️ The range discrepancy is disclosed at the head of this file, in this lesson and in the Source Ledger; ❌ no owner file, sibling topic file or index is edited to reconcile it.

### 2. The demand on bureaucracy

✅ From PDF p.177 (printed 160):

> Civil society movement demands that the conduct of public affairs should not be allowed to be concentrated in the hands of bureaucracy. Instead the citizen participation in these affairs should be fully encouraged. Civil society alone can provide the people with a forum where they can articulate their criticism of government policy and action. Thus civil society would prove to be an effective instrument of removing economic inequalities and securing social justice.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.177 (printed 160)

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ Strength |
|---|---|
| Public affairs should not be **concentrated in the hands of bureaucracy** | ⚠️ A demand |
| **Citizen participation... should be fully encouraged** | ⚠️ A demand |
| **Civil society alone can provide the people with a forum where they can articulate their criticism of government policy and action** | ⚠️ **Alone** — a strong exclusivity claim |
| It **would prove to be an effective instrument of removing economic inequalities and securing social justice** | ⚠️ A prediction |

🔑 The word **alone** is the chapter at its most expansive, and it is exactly the claim the next page will qualify. ⚠️ The structure of pp.177-178 is therefore a strong claim followed immediately by the chapter's own appraisal of it — which is why the two pages must be taught together.

⚠️ The anti-bureaucratic demand also connects back to Lesson 36, where Bell's post-industrial society was governed through **experts and the bureaucracy** in a way that **challenges some aspects of democracy and constitutes a danger to individual rights**. ✅ Civil society is offered here as the counterweight to exactly that trend.

### 3. The comparative table, reconstructed

✅ The chapter prints a table headed **Comparative Study of the State and Civil Society**, with six issues. ⚠️ Its columns are interleaved in the extraction; the content is reproduced here in the chapter's own order and wording, and the reconstruction of column alignment is catalogued in the Source Ledger.

| ✅ The Issue | ✅ The State | ✅ Civil Society |
|---|---|---|
| **Structure** | **Formal, unified structure comprising identifiable organs of power: legislature, executive, judiciary** | **Comprises loose organizations of citizens voluntarily pursuing public interest** |
| **Authority** | **Armed with supreme legal authority, i.e. sovereignty** | **Enjoys no formal, legal authority; at best, it enjoys moral authority over citizens** |
| **Jurisdiction** | **Compulsory jurisdiction over its citizens and territory** | **No compulsory jurisdiction anywhere; it largely depends on its ability of motivation and inspiration** |
| **Functions** | **Maintenance of law and order; protection of citizens from internal and external offences, natural and other calamities** | **Protection of the common interest of citizens by mobilizing them, pooling their resources, and exerting influence on government organs** |
| **Resources** | **Huge resources at its disposal—ownership of national wealth, taxation, public debt, etc.** | **No definite assets or sources of income; it has to create its own resources through mobilizing subscriptions, grants and donations, etc.** |
| **Occurrence** | **Universal; some form of political organization is found in all societies** | **It appears only in relatively advanced societies where citizens have become adequately conscious of their rights, duties and the common interest** |

🔑 This table is the most complete single statement in the chapter of what civil society is **not**, and four of its rows restate material taught much earlier.

```text
   ROW -> EARLIER LESSON
   Authority     -> L9  sovereignty as the state's exclusive prerogative
   Jurisdiction  -> L4  Laski on compulsory membership by residence
   Occurrence    -> L6  political organisation is universal, statehood is not
   Resources     -> L8  the state is entitled to levy taxes
```

⚠️ The **Occurrence** row is the most consequential and the least noticed. ✅ The state is **universal**; civil society **appears only in relatively advanced societies where citizens have become adequately conscious of their rights, duties and the common interest**. ⚠️ That is a developmental condition, and it sits beside the chapter's other condition at Lesson 39 — that such associations **can flourish only in a democratic set-up**. ⚠️ Together they mean civil society is an *achievement*, not a constant.

⚠️ The **Authority** row is equally sharp: civil society **enjoys no formal, legal authority; at best, it enjoys moral authority over citizens**. ✅ Its power is therefore wholly dependent on **its ability of motivation and inspiration** — which explains why Lesson 39's independence condition matters so much. ⚠️ An association that loses credibility loses everything it has.

### 4. The critical appraisal: the case for

✅ The section headed **A CRITICAL APPRAISAL** opens by restating the favourable view:

| ✅ Claim |
|---|
| Civil society is **an area where citizens are able to enjoy some level of autonomy or independence from the government** |
| It is **expected to promote an active citizenry to adopt a moral sense of obligation and participate in civic causes** |
| Forming **multiple voluntary associations** gives people **the opportunity to express themselves along with the competing viewpoints and perspectives** |
| They **can also find effective solution of their common problems through these associations** |

✅ Two instances are supplied:

> For example, after the 1985 Mexico City earthquake, the urban poor worked through neighbourhood organizations to force the government and World Bank to alter their recovery plans. Similar civic action groups have been formed to fight crime and corruption in Mexico and Nigeria. In Nigeria, groups of youngmen known as 'area boys' have played an active role in handling civic problems. They have a widespread approval from the people who are fed up with government inaction.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF pp.177-178 (printed 160-161)

| ✅ Instance | ✅ What it demonstrates |
|---|---|
| **The 1985 Mexico City earthquake** — the **urban poor worked through neighbourhood organizations to force the government and World Bank to alter their recovery plans** | ⚠️ Associational pressure can change the decisions of a state **and** an international institution |
| **Civic action groups... to fight crime and corruption in Mexico and Nigeria**; in Nigeria, **'area boys'** handling civic problems | ⚠️ Associations substitute where there is **government inaction** |

⚠️ Two cautions. ⚠️ These are **book-period** instances from a **2009** edition; ❌ they are not updated here. ⚠️ And the second instance is double-edged on the chapter's own terms: **widespread approval from the people who are fed up with government inaction** describes legitimacy derived from state failure, which is precisely the condition in which the next paragraph's **uncivil** forms arise.

### 5. The critical appraisal: the case against

✅ The chapter then turns:

> However, civil society movement is not an unmixed blessing. Does it promote democracy at all levels? Some observers point out that some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies. Then some manifestations of civil society itself can take highly 'uncivil' forms.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.178 (printed 161)

```text
   "NOT AN UNMIXED BLESSING"
            |
   +--------+---------------------------+
   |                                    |
 DEFECT 1                            DEFECT 2
 some organs are themselves          some manifestations take
 afflicted by ELITIST or             highly 'UNCIVIL' FORMS
 OLIGARCHIC TENDENCIES                      |
   -> civil society may               'UNCIVIL MOVEMENTS' that
      reproduce the concentration     PROMOTE EXCLUSION AND
      it was meant to disperse        VIOLENCE as a means of
                                      COMPETING FOR AND GAINING
                                      POWER WITHIN A DEMOCRATIC
                                      SYSTEM
                                            |
                                      they USE DEMOCRATIC LANGUAGE,
                                      INSTITUTIONS AND STRATEGIES
                                      FOR ACHIEVING THEIR
                                      UNDEMOCRATIC ENDS
```

✅ The chapter names two instances of the second defect: **activities of the groups like Carapintada military leaders in Argentina, and the Inkatha in South Africa, indicate the proliferation of 'uncivil movements'**. ⚠️ Both are **2009** instances and are reported as such; ❌ neither is updated or extended.

🔑 The definition of an uncivil movement is the highest-yield formulation on these pages: groups that **use democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends**. ⚠️ Note that the defect is not illegality but *instrumentalisation* — democratic means in the service of undemocratic ends.

✅ The chapter's conclusion is a double duty:

> In short, the enlightened groups within the civil society should not only keep a vigil over the state and its bureaucracy, they should also ensure that any group trying to fulfil its sinister designs by pretending to be the part of civil society, is not allowed to destroy the spirit of democracy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.6, PDF p.178 (printed 161)

| ✅ Duty one | ✅ Duty two |
|---|---|
| **Keep a vigil over the state and its bureaucracy** | **Ensure that any group trying to fulfil its sinister designs by pretending to be the part of civil society, is not allowed to destroy the spirit of democracy** |

🔑 Civil society is thus assigned a policing role **over itself** as well as over the state. ⚠️ That is the chapter's answer to the objection raised at Lesson 16, where it was asked how one tells an independent association from a captured one: the answer is that the enlightened part of civil society must make the distinction — which is honest, and plainly insufficient as a test.

### 6. The two closing boxes

✅ **Elitism — A theory of organization which holds that the chosen few—endowed with the qualities of leadership—should exercise all decision-making power.**

✅ **Oligarchy — A pattern of organization in which the chosen few exercise all power, largely by means of their manipulative skills.**

| Axis | ✅ **Elitism** | ✅ **Oligarchy** |
|---|---|---|
| Kind of thing | **A theory of organization** | **A pattern of organization** |
| Who rules | **The chosen few—endowed with the qualities of leadership** | **The chosen few** |
| Justification | ⚠️ **Should** exercise all decision-making power — normative | ❌ None — descriptive |
| Means | ⚠️ Leadership qualities | **Largely by means of their manipulative skills** |

⚠️ The pairing is exact and worth reproducing: elitism is a **theory** that says the few **should** rule because they are qualified; oligarchy is a **pattern** in which the few **do** rule by **manipulative skills**. ✅ The chapter places them here because defect 1 — **elitist or oligarchic tendencies** inside civil society — needs both terms, and they mean different things. ➡️ The full elitist-pluralist debate belongs to **Topics 22 and 23**.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

⚠️ **Objection.** The appraisal is self-undermining. ⚠️ Civil society is recommended because it disperses power and resists domination, and is then conceded to be **afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies** and capable of **'uncivil'** forms. ⚠️ If the remedy exhibits the disease, the recommendation collapses.

✅ **Reply.** It does not collapse, because the chapter never claimed that civil society is good as such. ✅ Its claim has always been structural: a **distinction** between state and civil society **must be maintained in order to prevent authoritarianism**, and associations supply **counterweights** to concentration. ⚠️ A counterweight need not be virtuous to be useful; what it needs is independence. ✅ And the two defects named are precisely failures of independence — capture by an elite within, or by sinister designs disguised as civil society.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply protects the structural claim and leaves the practical one exposed. ⚠️ The chapter's only safeguard is that **the enlightened groups within the civil society** should police the rest, which presupposes that enlightenment can be identified without a criterion — the same gap recorded at Lesson 16. ⚠️ And the strong claim that **civil society alone can provide the people with a forum** is never reconciled with the admission that some of its organs are oligarchic. ⚠️ Both are recorded as open.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The six-row table and the uncivil-movements definition are the two most distinctive pieces of this topic's contemporary section.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Is civil society an unmixed blessing?* — the chapter asks the question itself and answers it.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting civil society as inherently democratic. ✅ The chapter names two defects and demands internal vigilance.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the **Occurrence** row as your qualification: civil society **appears only in relatively advanced societies**, so it is an achievement rather than a given.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ PDF pp.177-178 lie **beyond** the range declared by the canonical owners; the discrepancy is disclosed and ❌ no owner file is edited.
2. ✅ Civil society movement demands that public affairs **not be allowed to be concentrated in the hands of bureaucracy**, because **civil society alone can provide the people with a forum where they can articulate their criticism of government policy and action**.
3. ✅ It would be **an effective instrument of removing economic inequalities and securing social justice**.
4. ✅ Table, **Structure**: the state has a **formal, unified structure... legislature, executive, judiciary**; civil society **comprises loose organizations of citizens voluntarily pursuing public interest**.
5. ✅ **Authority**: the state is **armed with supreme legal authority, i.e. sovereignty**; civil society **enjoys no formal, legal authority; at best... moral authority**.
6. ✅ **Jurisdiction**: the state has **compulsory jurisdiction**; civil society has **no compulsory jurisdiction anywhere** and depends on **motivation and inspiration**.
7. ✅ **Functions**: the state maintains **law and order** and protects from **internal and external offences, natural and other calamities**; civil society protects **the common interest of citizens by mobilizing them, pooling their resources, and exerting influence on government organs**.
8. ✅ **Resources**: the state has **ownership of national wealth, taxation, public debt**; civil society has **no definite assets** and relies on **subscriptions, grants and donations**.
9. ✅ **Occurrence**: the state is **universal**; civil society **appears only in relatively advanced societies where citizens have become adequately conscious of their rights, duties and the common interest**.
10. ✅ Instance: after the **1985 Mexico City earthquake**, the **urban poor worked through neighbourhood organizations to force the government and World Bank to alter their recovery plans**.
11. ✅ Instance: civic action groups against **crime and corruption in Mexico and Nigeria**; **'area boys'** in Nigeria, approved by people **fed up with government inaction**.
12. ✅ **However, civil society movement is not an unmixed blessing.** Defect 1: **some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies**.
13. ✅ Defect 2: **'uncivil movements'** that **promote exclusion and violence as a means of competing for and gaining power within a democratic system**, using **democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends** — instanced by **Carapintada military leaders in Argentina** and **the Inkatha in South Africa**.
14. ✅ The double duty: **keep a vigil over the state and its bureaucracy**, and prevent any group **pretending to be the part of civil society** from destroying **the spirit of democracy**.
15. ✅ Boxes: **elitism** is **a theory of organization**; **oligarchy** is **a pattern of organization** in which the few rule **largely by means of their manipulative skills**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Define an "uncivil movement" in the chapter's terms and explain why it is a harder problem than ordinary lawbreaking.

**Model answer:** ✅ Uncivil movements are **manifestations of civil society** that **promote exclusion and violence as a means of competing for and gaining power within a democratic system**, and whose distinguishing feature is that **such groups use democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends**; the chapter instances **Carapintada military leaders in Argentina, and the Inkatha in South Africa**. ⚠️ They are harder than ordinary lawbreaking because the problem is not conduct outside the rules but conduct that uses the rules. ⚠️ A movement that organises, speaks, associates and competes is doing exactly what civil society is praised for doing, so the usual indicators of civic virtue are present. ⚠️ Since civil society **enjoys no formal, legal authority** and operates by **motivation and inspiration**, it has no procedure for excluding such groups. ✅ The chapter's only remedy is internal: **the enlightened groups within the civil society** must ensure that any group **pretending to be the part of civil society** does not **destroy the spirit of democracy**. 🔑 Means are democratic, ends are not, and no formal test separates them.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat civil society as universally present. ⚠️ The chapter's **Occurrence** row states that the state is **universal** while civil society **appears only in relatively advanced societies where citizens have become adequately conscious of their rights, duties and the common interest** — which makes it a condition to be achieved rather than a feature of every society.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine whether civil society is an unmixed blessing. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The case for is strong: civil society is **an area where citizens... enjoy some level of autonomy or independence from the government**, promotes **an active citizenry** and **competing viewpoints** — as when, after the **1985 Mexico City earthquake**, **the urban poor worked through neighbourhood organizations to force the government and World Bank to alter their recovery plans**. ⚠️ **However, civil society movement is not an unmixed blessing.** ✅ **Some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies**, and some take **highly 'uncivil' forms**, **promoting exclusion and violence as a means of... gaining power within a democratic system**, such groups **us[ing] democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends**. ✅ Hence the double duty: **keep a vigil over the state and its bureaucracy**, and prevent groups **pretending to be the part of civil society** from destroying **the spirit of democracy**.

**Rubric [10]:** at least two favourable claims with one named instance **2** · the not-an-unmixed-blessing turn **1** · the elitist-or-oligarchic defect **2** · the uncivil-movements definition including the democratic-means clause **3** · the double duty as conclusion **2**.
