# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Eight cumulative checks. ⚠️ Each requires material from at least three lessons, which is the skill an examiner is actually testing when a stem spans the whole topic.

---

## Check A — the five collapses the chapter exists to prevent

**Prompt:** Easton listed five incompatible senses of "state" and concluded that the word should be abandoned. Show that four of them are collapses of a distinction, name the lesson that restores each, and state what is lost politically in each case.

**Worked response:** ✅ Easton's senses are the state as **the embodiment of the moral spirit**, as **the instrument of exploitation used by one class against others**, as **simply an aspect of society**, as **simply a synonym for government**, and as **a separate and unique association among a large number of other associations**. ⚠️ Four are collapses. ✅ *State = society* is restored at Lessons 11-12, where the distinction is needed **not only for scientific precision but also for saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule**; the loss is that every social obligation becomes an enforceable command. ✅ *State = government* is restored at Lesson 17, where **the organization is greater than the organ** and **government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty**; the loss is the ground of criticising rulers. ✅ *State = the ideal state* is the idealist position of Lesson 18, which **demands complete subordination of man** **without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the image of the ideal state**; the loss is accountability. ✅ *State = class instrument* is the Marxist position of Lesson 18, which **attributes any imperfection of government to the state itself**; the loss is reform within the existing order. 🔑 The fifth sense, the state as one association among many, is the one the chapter partly keeps, at Lesson 4.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat Easton's list as a survey of opinions. ⚠️ It is an argument from ambiguity, and the chapter's reply is not to deny the ambiguity but to resolve it pair by pair.

---

## Check B — the four elements, and what each one's absence does

**Prompt:** For each of the four elements of the state, state what its loss produces on the chapter's own account, and identify the one element with a two-level structure.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Population** is essential because **the state is a human institution**, and it must be united by **interdependence, consciousness of common interest, and general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions**. ✅ **Territory** is essential because **a state comes into existence only when its population is settled in a fixed territory**; a community with **authority**, **government with custom-based law** and even a **'political system'** but no fixed territory is not a state. ✅ **Sovereignty**'s loss, through **internal revolt or external aggression**, produces **anarchy and disappearance of the state as such**. ⚠️ **Government** is the element with two levels. ✅ Individual governments **may rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state**, so long as they are formed **according to the established custom, procedure or constitution**; but when **an alien power** suspends **the established procedure of forming a government**, the state is **reduced to a colony of the imperial power**. 🔑 Loss of sovereignty ends the state; loss of the government-forming procedure degrades it.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not add international recognition as a fifth element. ⚠️ It is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence**, and is needed only so that a state may **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**.

---

## Check C — the full history of one word

**Prompt:** Trace the concept of civil society through every stage the chapter gives it, and identify the single move that produces the modern meaning.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Originally the terms 'civil society' and 'political society' were used as coterminous**, so that civil society **was applied synonymously with 'state'**. ✅ **Cicero (106-43 B.C.)** used it for a community whose members **lived together as citizens, abided by civil laws and led a civilized, cultured and dignified life**, excluding **barbarian communities**. ✅ **Locke** had people enter it on leaving **the state of nature** to protect **'life, liberty and property'**; ✅ **Rousseau** found in it **legal equality of all citizens... in spite of their natural differences**. ✅ **Hegel** split the term, opposing **'universal egoism'** to **'universal altruism'** and locating civil society in **economic activities**. ✅ **Marx accepted Hegel's description... but he did not accept Hegel's distinction**, holding that **in actual practice civil society represented the state itself**. ✅ **Gramsci** relocated it to **family, school and church**, embodying **'structures of legitimation'**. ✅ **Marcuse** made the distinction diagnostic of modern unfreedom in both directions. ⚠️ The decisive move is Gramsci's: the shift from an **economic** civil society to a **cultural** one is what produces the contemporary sense of **'intermediate' associations between individual (or family) and the state**. 🔑 Two reversals in one history — from identity with the state to distinction from it, and from economy to culture.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not say that civil society has always meant the non-state sphere. ⚠️ The chapter's first sentence on the subject denies it.

---

## Check D — nation, nationality and state, and the three mismatches

**Prompt:** Define all three terms in the chapter's words, then map its three mismatch cases to the claims each one refutes.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Nationality** rests on **common race, language, religion, culture, geographical compactness, common political aspirations and historical development**, **most of these factors** being **based on birth**, so that feelings of nationality **separate one set of people from other such sets** and grow from **a relatively narrow base**. ✅ A **nation grows on a much wider base**: **people living in a defined territory, inspired by a sense of unity, common political aspirations, common interests, common history and common destiny though they may belong to different nationalities**, since **nationhood transcends the conditions of birth and extends to the permanent residents of a state**. ✅ The **state** is population, territory, government and sovereignty. ✅ Mismatch one — **the Kurds are scattered over Iraq, Iran and Turkey although they recognize themselves as one nation** — refutes nationalism's first postulate, that **each nation should constitute an independent state**. ✅ Mismatch two — **Lebanon** and **Cyprus**, and **former USSR, Yugoslavia, Czechoslovakia and Ethiopia** — refutes the second postulate, that **each state should represent a particular nation**. ✅ Mismatch three — naturalised citizens **so deeply attached to the language, culture, faith and customs of the countries of their origin** — embarrasses the chapter's own requirement of **undivided allegiance**. 🔑 Two postulates refuted and one definition strained, all from the chapter's own pages.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not quote the named countries as current description. ⚠️ They are **2009** illustrations used to make conceptual points.

---

## Check E — one argument stated at four levels

**Prompt:** The chapter argues that the nation-state can no longer discharge its functions. Assemble the argument from every place it appears, and state the conclusion in the chapter's exact words.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Level one, capacity.** ✅ **In view of the growing contact between different parts of the world, a nation-state is no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members, not to speak of mankind as such.** ⚠️ **Level two, security.** ✅ **One major function of a nation-state is to provide for security to its residents**, yet **no nation-state has remained capable of providing full security**, and a nuclear attack would be **disastrous to humanity far and wide**. ⚠️ **Level three, environment.** ✅ Pollution from **industrial waste** is **not confined to the boundaries of nation-states**; **petrol and diesel** consumption by **the advanced nations** harms **the global atmosphere**; felling **in any country** produces, by **cumulative effect**, **distortion of weather cycle and consequent danger of global warming**. ⚠️ **Level four, information and economy.** ✅ The information revolution has **practically eliminated the distance between different countries in the matter of communication**, converting the world into a **'global village'** and paving the way for **globalization of economy**, while **even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means**. ✅ The conclusion: **the situation calls for a global effort to deal with the global problems. In this scenario, nation-state will have to play a new role for which it should concede necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty.** 🔑 Four levels, one structure: a state function, and a cause that crosses the boundary.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not report the chapter as predicting the end of the nation-state. ⚠️ It says the state **will have to play a new role** and should **concede** adjustment, and it adds that **there is no certainty about the future form of the state**.

---

## Check F — civil society as a terrain rather than a side

**Prompt:** Assemble every verdict the chapter passes on civil society and show that they are consistent.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The verdicts look contradictory. ✅ Civil society **embodies 'structures of legitimation'**, and **capitalist society largely depends on the efficiency of the institutions of civil society for its stability**. ✅ Yet **the opposition could also use this device to promote its own viewpoint**, so that **the hegemony of the rulers could be undermined through manipulation of education and culture** — as with **Solidarity** in **Poland** and **'Ecoglasnost'** in **Bulgaria**. ✅ It is **the bedrock of civil liberties** and **the true source of democratization**; it is also **not an unmixed blessing**, since **some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies** and some take **'uncivil' forms** that **use democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends**. ⚠️ They are consistent because civil society is a **terrain**, not an agent: the same institutions of socialisation serve whoever controls them. ✅ The chapter's own condition confirms the reading — associations must be **independent from the state as well as from trade and industry**, and can **flourish only in a democratic set-up**. 🔑 Independence, not virtue, is what makes civil society democratic.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the celebratory and the critical passages as the chapter changing its mind. ⚠️ Its two contemporary senses are stated explicitly, one corresponding to **Gramsci's view** and the other to demand-making.

---

## Check G — two sources, two questions about difference

**Prompt:** Show how the supplement's secularism and multiculturalism entries answer a question the chapter raises but does not answer.

**Worked response:** ✅ The chapter raises it at Lesson 5: the population of a state **need not belong to a single race, religion, language or culture**, **a homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state**, and the modern state **claims to reconcile the interests of various groups of its citizens**. ⚠️ The verb **claims** leaves the question open — *how*? ✅ 🧩 Secularism answers the religious half: all secular states are **neither theocratic nor do they establish religion**, and the Indian model adds **equal focus on intra-religious and inter-religious domination**, **principled distance**, and **principled state intervention**. ✅ 🧩 Multiculturalism answers the cultural half: **equality is equal rights of all cultures to maintain its differential identity**, implemented through Kymlicka's **self-government rights, poly-ethnic rights and representative rights**. ⚠️ Both answers carry the same unresolved cost, named by the feminist critique: recognition can **preserve and legitimize patriarchal and traditional beliefs that systematically disadvantage women**. 🔑 The chapter asserts that plural statehood is possible; the supplement shows how, and at what price.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not merge the two sources. ⚠️ Gauba's chapter says nothing about secularism or multiculturalism, and every claim about them is marked 🧩.

---

## Check H — the five denials that carry the most marks

**Prompt:** Name the five congruences the sources expressly deny, quote the denial in each case, and say what each denial protects.

**Worked response:** ✅ **One.** State is not government: **the organization is greater than the organ**, and the denial protects the citizen's standing to **criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state**. ✅ **Two.** Nation is not nationality: **a nation grows on a much wider base**, including people of **different races, with different religions, languages and cultures**, and the denial protects plural statehood against the demand for **a separate state for each nationality**, which is **no longer held valid**. ✅ **Three.** Civil society is not the NGO sector: the chapter's contemporary sense is **'intermediate' associations between individual (or family) and the state**, headed by **free political parties and a free press**, and the denial protects the concept's critical variants. ✅ **Four.** 🧩 Secularism is not equal respect for all religions: **Indian secularism cannot be captured by the phrase "equal respects for all religions"**, and the denial protects **principled state intervention** against **institutionalized religious domination**. ✅ **Five.** 🧩 Multiculturalism is not cultural determinism: **modern multiculturalists rarely sympathize with this form of crude cultural determinism**, and the denial protects the doctrine from the liberal charge that it **subordinates the rights and needs of the individual**. 🔑 Five denials, five scorable sentences, and each one is the difference between a reproduced definition and an argued position.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not state any of the five denials without its protective purpose. ⚠️ A denial without a reason reads as pedantry; with the reason it reads as analysis.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six original questions — two at each weight — distinct from the forty-five lesson-local questions above. ⚠️ Ceilings observed: **10-mark models within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models within 250 words.** ⚠️ Each rubric is quantified and unique.

---

## Question 1 [10 marks]

**Question:** "The distinction between state and government is a condition of political criticism." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter supports the claim. ✅ The state is **the organization, of which government is the administrative organ**, since **the organization is greater than the organ**: a state has **a constitution, a code of laws, a way of setting up its government, a body of citizens**. ✅ Because the two are distinct, **government commands our obedience; the state commands our loyalty**, and **we may criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state**. ⚠️ Collapse the distinction and criticism becomes disloyalty — the idealist failure, since that theory **demands complete subordination of man** **without ensuring whether the actual government... conforms to the ideal state**. ✅ Hence the chapter's test: **any theory which does not provide for a concrete control mechanism over the government is bound to have disastrous consequences**. ⚠️ The condition holds only where the **established procedure** for replacing a government survives.

**Rubric [10]:** the organ/organization formula with the greater-than principle **2** · at least two items of the state inventory **1** · the obedience/loyalty sentence **2** · the criticise-and-still-acclaim consequence **2** · the idealist failure used as the counter-case **2** · a qualification about the established procedure **1**.

---

## Question 2 [10 marks]

**Question:** Discuss why international recognition is not treated as an element of the state. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ **Some writers regard 'international recognition' as an essential element of the state**, but the chapter refuses. ⚠️ Conceptually, recognition is **formal recognition of the sovereignty of the state over a given territory and population by other states** — it presupposes the sovereignty it is supposed to create, and so is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence**. ✅ Historically, when **Bangladesh** appeared, some states recognised it **immediately** while others withheld recognition **for quite a long time**, since **much depends on the foreign policy of a state**; the **USA** withheld recognition of the **USSR** and **People's Republic of China** for **decades**, **but they did exist as states**. ⚠️ Recognition remains necessary to **enjoy its rights and perform its duties as a member of the comity of nations**. 🔑 Necessary for participation, not for existence.

**Rubric [10]:** the fifth-element thesis named as the position tested **1** · recognition correctly defined as recognition of sovereignty by other states **2** · the presupposition argument **2** · the Bangladesh illustration with the foreign-policy clause **2** · the USSR and PRC illustration with **decades** **2** · the participation/existence reconciliation **1**.

---

## Question 3 [15 marks]

**Question:** "Civil society is neither inherently democratic nor inherently repressive; what matters is its independence." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter supports both halves. ✅ On the repressive side, **Gramsci** placed **family, school and church** in civil society as institutions that **transmit capitalist value-system to the new generation**, embodying **'structures of legitimation'** which, with political society's **'structures of coercion'**, form **'structures of domination'**; indeed **capitalist society largely depends on the efficiency of the institutions of civil society for its stability**. ✅ On the democratic side, **Tocqueville's** voluntary associations **act as counterweights to the state power**, **crystallize and publicize opinions and interests which would otherwise go unheard**, and **function as 'schools of democracy'**; **Cohen and Arato** make civil society **essential for a healthy democratic society**; **Putnam** finds **social capital** **necessary for an effective democracy**.

⚠️ The chapter reconciles them: **the institutions of civil society could not only be used to strengthen the authority of the ruling class, but the opposition could also use this device**, the hegemony of rulers being undermined **through manipulation of education and culture** — as **Solidarity** and **'Ecoglasnost'** showed.

✅ Independence is therefore the operative variable, and the chapter names both capture-routes when it demands associations **independent from the state as well as from trade and industry**, able to flourish **only in a democratic set-up**. ⚠️ The qualification is that independence is asserted and not measured: the chapter concedes that **some organs of civil society are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies**, and its only safeguard is that **the enlightened groups within the civil society** police the rest. 🔑 The proposition is right; its criterion is unoperationalised.

**Rubric [15]:** the Gramscian case with at least two quoted terms **3** · the democratic case with at least two named theorists **3** · the chapter's own both-sides sentence about ruling class and opposition **3** · at least one East European instance **1** · the independence condition with both capture-routes **3** · a residual naming the absence of a test for independence **2**.

---

## Question 4 [15 marks]

**Question:** Examine the claim that secularism and multiculturalism answer two different questions about difference. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ 🧩 They do, and the difference is in the object. ✅ 🧩 Secularism concerns the relation between religion and state power: all secular states are **neither theocratic nor do they establish religion**; the Western model treats **separation of religion and state... as mutually exclusive**, so that **religion is a private affair, not a matter of state policy or law**; the Indian model rejects that exclusion, insists that **the idea of inter-religious equality is more crucial**, and produces **equal focus on intra-religious and inter-religious domination**, operating by **principled distance** and **principled state intervention**.

✅ 🧩 Multiculturalism concerns the relation between cultural groups and common citizenship. ✅ 🧩 Descriptively it records **cultural diversity that arises from the existence within a society of two or more groups whose beliefs and practices generate a distinctive sense of collective identity**; normatively it is **a positive endorsement or acceptance, even celebration, of communal diversity**, implemented through Kymlicka's **self-government rights, poly-ethnic rights and representative rights**, on the principle that **equality is equal rights of all cultures to maintain its differential identity**.

⚠️ The questions nonetheless converge at one point, and that convergence is where both are tested. ✅ 🧩 Indian secularism is concerned with **intra-religious domination** — **the oppression of the dalits and women within Hinduism** — while the feminist critique of multiculturalism warns that recognition may **preserve and legitimize patriarchal and traditional beliefs that systematically disadvantage women**. 🔑 Both doctrines must therefore decide how much internal domination a group's autonomy may contain, and neither source supplies a principle for deciding it.

**Rubric [15]:** the common secular floor **1** · the Western model with mutual exclusion and privatisation **3** · the Indian model with the two-axis formulation and principled distance **4** · the descriptive and normative senses of multiculturalism **3** · Kymlicka's three categories or the equality-as-difference formula **2** · the convergence on internal domination stated as the shared unresolved point **2**.

---

## Question 5 [20 marks]

**Question:** "The modern state has been defined by what it monopolises and limited by what crosses its borders." Critically analyse. *(Critically analyse — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The first half is definitional. ✅ **Machiavelli** described **the power which has authority over men**, describing **the nature of the state, not the end**; ✅ **Weber** completed it, holding that **sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends** and defining it as **a human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory**. ✅ The monopoly is specified: **the right to use physical force is ascribed to other institutions or to individuals only to the extent to which the state permits it**, so **legitimate coercion in its own right is the exclusive prerogative of the state**, exercised over a **territory where its authority is accepted without dispute or challenge**.

✅ The second half is its conclusion. ✅ **No nation-state has remained capable of providing full security to its residents**; pollution from **industrial waste** is **not confined to the boundaries of nation-states**; felling **in any country** produces, by **cumulative effect**, **global warming**; the information revolution has **practically eliminated the distance between different countries in the matter of communication**, producing a **'global village'** used equally by commerce and by **criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists**.

✅ Hence **the situation calls for a global effort**, the state **conced[ing] necessary adjustment in its authority and sovereignty**. ⚠️ Two qualifications. ⚠️ The adjustment is **conceded**, so sovereignty remains the source of its own limits. ⚠️ And the measure is undefined — **reasonable** and **necessary** are never specified — closing with **no certainty about the future form of the state**. 🔑 Monopoly defines; porosity constrains.

**Rubric [20]:** Machiavelli's nature-not-end move **2** · Weber's two-step definition with at least five clauses **4** · the permitted-force gloss on monopoly **2** · the territorial condition quoted **2** · at least three boundary-crossing pressures with their exact formulations **5** · the concede-adjustment conclusion **3** · both qualifications, self-binding and undefined measure **2**.

---

## Question 6 [20 marks]

**Question:** "Nationalism and internationalism disagree about ordering, not about everything." Discuss with reference to the nation-state. *(Discuss — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ **The concept of nation-state is the focus of two conflicting ideologies**, and the conflict is narrower than it appears. ✅ Nationalism is **a sentiment as well as an ideology**: as sentiment the nationalist **accords primacy to his national interest over all other interests**; as ideology it demands that **each nation should constitute an independent state; and each state should represent a particular nation**. ✅ Internationalism **does not regard nation-state as the centre of political organization, nor it accepts nation as the object of individual's undivided loyalty**, holding that **a nation-state is no longer capable of fulfilling all the needs of its members**, so that **in the case of a clash between national interest and universal human interest, human interest should be given precedence** and **'my country, right or wrong'** is repudiated.

⚠️ Yet the agreement is substantial. ✅ Nationalism itself claims that such communities **should enjoy an equal status with other nations in the world community** and that **no nation should be kept under the domination or supremacy of any other nation** — which is an internationalist principle in content. ✅ The chapter adds that **a logical outcome of the idea of a nation postulates equality among nations, their co-existence and cooperation**.

⚠️ The genuine disagreements are two: the **centre** of political organisation, and the **ordering** of interests in a clash. ✅ Even on sovereignty the gap narrows, since internationalism asks states to **accept reasonable restraints**, which presupposes the authority it restrains. 🔑 Two ideologies, one political form, and a dispute about precedence rather than about worth.

**Rubric [20]:** both limbs of nationalism with the primacy clause **4** · both nationalist postulates **2** · both internationalist denials **3** · the precedence rule and the repudiated slogan **3** · at least two points of agreement, including the non-domination claim **4** · the restraints clause read as presupposing sovereignty **2** · a closing verdict naming ordering and centre as the real disagreements **2**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Eighty-seven errors, grouped by where they arise. ⚠️ Each row states the error as a candidate would actually commit it, and the correction in the sources' own terms wherever they supply them.

## Set 1 — the concept of the state and its definitions (Lessons 1-4)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ❌ "Behaviouralists rejected the state because it was old-fashioned." | ✅ Three lettered grounds: a **formal concept** while politics **transcends the formal organization**; conception by **'ends'** dragging enquiry into **moral philosophy**; and a **particular type of organization** that generates **'pre-state'** and **'stateless'** societies |
| 2 | ❌ "Easton said the term should be used carefully." | ✅ He concluded it **'ought to be abandoned entirely'** |
| 3 | ❌ "The whole discipline abandoned the term." | ✅ The distaste was **confined to some exponents of liberal political theory, especially to some American political scientists** |
| 4 | ❌ "Skocpol refuted Easton." | ⚠️ No confrontation is reported; **in the 1980s attention swung back**, and the state was **redefined as an active agent of shaping and reshaping society** |
| 5 | ❌ "The 1980s restored the old concept." | ✅ The redefinition is explicitly **in contrast to the earlier concept of the state as an institutional structure** |
| 6 | ❌ "Gettel said politics begins and ends with the state." | ✅ That is **Garner** (**1928**); **Gettel** (**1949**) called political science **'the science of the state'** |
| 7 | ❌ "States are a modern invention." | ✅ **Political organization has existed since ancient times**; it is **the concept of the 'state' as such** that is **comparatively modern** |
| 8 | ❌ "Machiavelli's contribution was political realism." | ✅ In this chapter it is that his formulation **describes the nature of the state, not the end of the state** |
| 9 | ❌ "Weber said the state has a monopoly of force." | ✅ **A human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory** — four droppable words, four lost marks |
| 10 | ❌ "Weber defined the state by its purposes." | ✅ **Sociologically, the state cannot be defined in terms of its ends** |
| 11 | ❌ "Laski held that the state is supreme over all associations." | ⚠️ The quoted passage is about **compulsory membership through residence**; Laski is the leading **pluralist**, and the monist reading is a misattribution |
| 12 | ❌ "The four elements follow logically from the definitions." | ✅ The chapter says only that it is **customary** to identify the state by them |

## Set 2 — the four elements (Lessons 5-10)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 13 | ❌ "Population just means people." | ✅ It must be united by **interdependence**, **consciousness of common interest**, and **general regard for a set of common rules of behaviour and institutions** |
| 14 | ❌ "Economic self-sufficiency is an element of the state." | ✅ It is **essential for the stability of a state** — ❌ not for its existence |
| 15 | ❌ "Foreign aid is ruled out." | ✅ **The possibility of foreign aid, as a temporary measure, cannot be ruled out** |
| 16 | ❌ "A modern state needs a homogeneous population." | ✅ **A homogeneous population is no longer considered an essential feature of the modern state** |
| 17 | ❌ "The state reconciles the interests of its groups." | ⚠️ The chapter says it **claims to reconcile** — the verb is deliberate |
| 18 | ❌ "Nomadic tribes have no government." | ✅ They **do have the institution of authority, or even government with custom-based law, but not a state** |
| 19 | ❌ "Seeley's view is accepted." | ✅ It is **no longer held valid** |
| 20 | ❌ "Political organisation is coextensive with statehood." | ✅ Political sociologists **concede the existence of a 'political system'** where there is no state |
| 21 | ❌ "Territorial waters extend twelve miles." | ⚠️ The chapter says **usually** **three miles**, extendable **in times of war**; ❌ do not update a 2009 illustration |
| 22 | ❌ "Territory is just land." | ✅ **Land, water and air-space within its boundary** |
| 23 | ❌ "Patriotism is attachment to one's nationality." | ✅ Patriotism is **the sense of belonging to a state**, binding people of **different races, with different religions, languages and cultures** |
| 24 | ❌ "Boundaries are natural facts." | ✅ Sometimes demarcation is **mainly on a political basis**, where **the peoples' sense of identification** becomes the basis |
| 25 | ❌ "Government means the ruling party." | ✅ **The agency or machinery through which common policies are determined and by which common affairs are regulated and common interests promoted** |
| 26 | ❌ "A change of government changes the state." | ✅ Governments **rise and fall without disturbing identity of the state**, if formed **according to the established custom, procedure or constitution** |
| 27 | ❌ "A dictatorship reduces a state to a colony." | ⚠️ The chapter's condition is suppression **by an alien power** so that **the established procedure of forming a government is also suspended**; ❌ the domestic case is not addressed |
| 28 | ❌ "Without government there is no society." | ✅ The claim is narrower — no **common aims, common interests or a common organization**; **men can live without a state, but not without society** |
| 29 | ❌ "Sovereignty belongs to the government." | ✅ It **may be exercised by the government of the day, but it essentially belongs to the state from which it is derived** |
| 30 | ❌ "Only the state uses force." | ✅ Force is permitted to others **only to the extent to which the state permits it**; the state is **the sole source of the 'right' to use violence** |
| 31 | ❌ "Loss of sovereignty makes a state a colony." | ✅ It produces **anarchy and disappearance of the state as such**; colonial status arises from the suspended government-forming procedure |
| 32 | ❌ "Recognition is a fifth element." | ✅ It is **the outcome of the sovereignty of the state, not a condition of its existence** |
| 33 | ❌ "The chapter contradicts itself on recognition." | ⚠️ p.150 concerns **participation** in the **comity of nations**; p.152 concerns **existence** |

## Set 3 — society, civil society and government (Lessons 11-18)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 34 | ❌ "The state/society distinction is merely academic." | ✅ It is needed **not only for scientific precision but also for saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule** |
| 35 | ❌ "Political and social relations work the same way." | ✅ Social relations run on **necessity, custom, courtesy, morality, mutual understanding, agreement or even contract**; political relations **mainly** on **command and obedience** |
| 36 | ❌ "Society and state always coincide." | ✅ Four configurations — coincide, within, beyond, without — and **that is not always the case** |
| 37 | ❌ "Society depends on the state." | ✅ **The state depends on society for its existence, not vice versa** |
| 38 | ❌ "The state creates social institutions." | ✅ **MacIver**: social **forms** owe it **neither their origin nor their inspiration**; social **forces** it **may protect or modify, but certainly does not create** |
| 39 | ❌ "Civil society has always meant the non-state sphere." | ✅ **Originally the terms 'civil society' and 'political society' were used as coterminous** |
| 40 | ❌ "Cicero used civil society to mean the state's machinery." | ✅ **It was not merely the external structure of the state** but a life of **citizens** who **abided by civil laws** and were **civilized, cultured and dignified** |
| 41 | ❌ "The state of nature is a historical period." | ✅ Box: **the hypothetical condition in which people live before the formation of the state** |
| 42 | ❌ "Marx rejected Hegel's account of civil society." | ✅ He **accepted Hegel's description** and **did not accept Hegel's distinction** |
| 43 | ❌ "Hegel included the family in civil society." | ✅ The individual deals with others **apart from the members of his family** |
| 44 | ❌ "Gramsci identified civil society with the economy." | ✅ He located it in **family, school and church** — a cultural relocation |
| 45 | ❌ "Gramsci's civil society only reproduces domination." | ✅ The chapter later records that **the opposition could also use this device** |
| 46 | ❌ "Marcuse said civil society should defeat the state." | ✅ **We must secure independence of both institutions** |
| 47 | ❌ "Civil liberties protect against the state." | ✅ The box says **from arbitrary acts of government** |
| 48 | ❌ "Loyalty to the state is unconditional." | ✅ Obligations **should be determined by the character of the government it creates**, and a discredited government must be replaced or **the credibility of the state itself will be eroded** |
| 49 | ❌ "Idealism and Marxism make the same error." | ⚠️ Both refuse the distinction, in opposite directions and with opposite costs |
| 50 | ❌ "Marxism seeks to abolish authority." | ✅ Withering away **restore[s] 'authority' to a classless society** |

## Set 4 — nation, nationalism and internationalism (Lessons 19-21, 29-32)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 51 | ❌ "Nation and nationality are the same." | ✅ Nationality rests **mostly** on factors **based on birth** and **separates**; a nation **grows on a much wider base** and includes **different nationalities** |
| 52 | ❌ "Nationality causes war." | ⚠️ Superiority and disdain occur **sometimes**, and it is through them that **tensions, wars** follow |
| 53 | ❌ "Each nationality should have a state." | ✅ That view is **no longer held valid** |
| 54 | ❌ "Self-determination is only about decolonisation." | ✅ **Wilson** conceived it **primarily as a criterion for the break-up of the empires defeated in the war**; **Lenin** gave it the colonial scope |
| 55 | ❌ "Self-determination means independence." | ✅ Two limbs — **to be independent** *and* **to choose a suitable form of government for itself** |
| 56 | ❌ "Nation-building is a cultural task." | ✅ It is **bound to remain elusive** unless states **contain their widening economic disparities** and escape **the foul play of neo-colonial powers** |
| 57 | ❌ "Nationalism is only an ideology." | ✅ It **denotes a sentiment as well as an ideology** |
| 58 | ❌ "National symbols create nations." | ✅ A community that already **regard[s] themselves as a nation** may **create or invent some elements of nationhood** to **strengthen their national sentiment** |
| 59 | ❌ "Internationalism is a single doctrine." | ✅ **Two streams**: **international communism** and **a principle of international organization** |
| 60 | ❌ "Internationalism denies national interest." | ✅ Only **in the case of a clash** does **human interest** take precedence |
| 61 | ❌ "Internationalism abolishes sovereignty." | ✅ Each state should **accept reasonable restraints on its sovereignty** |
| 62 | ❌ "The chapter predicts the end of the nation-state." | ✅ It says the state **will have to play a new role** and that **there is no certainty about the future form of the state** |

## Set 5 — contemporary trends and civil society today (Lessons 33-40)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 63 | ❌ "The nation-state can no longer provide security." | ✅ The claim is that none can provide **full** security |
| 64 | ❌ "The chapter blames developing countries for pollution." | ✅ The fuel mechanism names **the advanced nations**; the felling mechanism applies **in any country** |
| 65 | ❌ "Globalisation only benefits commerce." | ✅ **Even criminals, drug traffickers and terrorists are making full use of the advanced means** |
| 66 | ❌ "The three-world division still holds." | ✅ It became **partly outdated** after the dissolution **around 1991** |
| 67 | ❌ "Non-alignment meant neutrality." | ✅ Two purposes: **to focus on development** and **to contain the intensity of the prevailing 'cold war'** |
| 68 | ❌ "North-South is a geographical division." | ✅ **Technically this division was not found correct** — **Australia and New Zealand** lie south, **India and the rest of South Asia** in the north |
| 69 | ❌ "The convergence thesis is the chapter's own view." | ⚠️ It is attributed to **western writers** and then criticised for projecting the **'market society model' as a universal model of development** |
| 70 | ❌ "Civil society means NGOs." | ✅ NGOs are added last to a list headed by **free political parties and a free press** |
| 71 | ❌ "Civil society can replace representative democracy." | ✅ **Cohen and Arato** and **Hirst** both hold that it **supplement[s]** representative democracy rather than replacing it |
| 72 | ❌ "Civil society is an unmixed blessing." | ✅ **Some organs... are themselves afflicted by elitist or oligarchic tendencies**, and **'uncivil movements'** **use democratic language, institutions and strategies for achieving their undemocratic ends** |

## Set 6 — 🧩 secularism and multiculturalism (Lessons 41-45)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 73 | ❌ "Secularism means rejecting religion." | ✅ 🧩 It is a philosophy that **forms its ethics without reference to religious dogmas**; secular states are **neither theocratic nor do they establish religion** |
| 74 | ❌ "All secular states separate religion and state identically." | ✅ 🧩 Mutual exclusion is **not the defining feature of all secular states** |
| 75 | ❌ "The Western model can reform religious practice." | ✅ 🧩 **If a religious institution forbids a woman from becoming a priest, then the state can do little about it** |
| 76 | ❌ "Indian secularism copies the West." | ✅ 🧩 It is **fundamentally different**, arising from **deep religious diversity that predated the advent of Western modern ideas** |
| 77 | ❌ "Indian secularism means equal respect for all religions." | ✅ 🧩 It **cannot be captured by** that phrase; it **allows equal disrespect for some aspects of organized religions** |
| 78 | ❌ "Principled distance means constant intervention." | ✅ 🧩 It **also allows for non interference**, and **interference need not automatically mean coercive or forceful intervention** |
| 79 | ❌ "Secularism threatens religious identity." | ✅ 🧩 It **protects religious identity**, though it **does undermine some forms** — **dogmatic, violent, fanatical, exclusivist** |
| 80 | ❌ "Descriptive multiculturalism is neutral reporting." | ✅ 🧩 It is **invariably reserved** for **racial, ethnic and language differences** that **generate a distinctive sense of collective identity** |
| 81 | ❌ "Normative multiculturalism has one ground." | ✅ 🧩 Two — **the right... to respect and recognition** and **the alleged benefits to the larger society** |
| 82 | ❌ "Multiculturalists are cultural determinists." | ✅ 🧩 **Modern multiculturalists rarely sympathize with this form of crude cultural determinism** |
| 83 | ❌ "Minority rights are one kind of claim." | ✅ 🧩 **Self-government rights, poly-ethnic rights and representative rights**, differing in holder and content |
| 84 | ❌ "Multiculturalism respects formal equality." | ✅ 🧩 It **is prepared to violate the principal of formal equality** in order to **redress social injustice** |
| 85 | ❌ "There is one model of multiculturalism." | ✅ 🧩 **Liberal**, **pluralist**, **particularist** and **cosmopolitan** |
| 86 | ❌ "Particularist multiculturalism is the strongest form." | ✅ 🧩 It **may thus be an example of 'plural monoculturalism' rather than a form of multiculturalism** |
| 87 | ❌ "The critiques come from one direction." | ✅ 🧩 Liberal, conservative, feminist and social-reformist, with incompatible premises |

---

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

⚠️ Seven maps. ⚠️ Each assembles material that is distributed across many lessons and cannot be seen from any one of them.

## Map 1 — The chapter's single argument, end to end

```text
   STARTING CONDITION
   traditional theory: "political science begins and ends
   with the state" (Garner 1928)
            |
   CHALLENGE  three behavioural objections (a) formal
   (b) ends -> moral philosophy  (c) excludes pre-state and
   stateless societies   => Easton 1953: "ought to be
   abandoned entirely"
            |
   SURVIVAL  Marxist vocabulary · 'state-building' ·
   the 1980s swing · REDEFINITION as "an active agent of
   shaping and reshaping society"
            |
   DEFINITION  Machiavelli: NATURE not END
               Weber 1920: human community that
               (successfully) CLAIMS the MONOPOLY of the
               LEGITIMATE use of PHYSICAL FORCE within a
               GIVEN TERRITORY
            |
   DECOMPOSITION  population · territory · government ·
   sovereignty     [recognition = INCIDENTAL]
            |
   DIFFERENTIATION  five neighbours, five distinctions
     state / society       -> saves the individual from
                              absolutist rule
     state / civil society -> prevents authoritarianism
     state / government    -> permits criticism of rulers
     nation / nationality  -> permits plural statehood
     nation / state        -> admits the mismatches
            |
   EVOLUTION  tribal -> Oriental empire -> Greek city-state
   -> Roman world empire -> feudal state -> MODERN NATION-STATE
            |
   PARADOX  "liberty, equality and rule of law at home,
   and imperialistic exploitation abroad"
            |
   UNIVERSALISATION  1917 · 1949 · decolonisation ·
   socialist states and new nations both STABILIZE AS
   NATION-STATES
            |
   TWO IDEOLOGIES  nationalism (primacy to national interest;
   congruence of nation and state)  vs  internationalism
   (human interest first; reasonable restraints on sovereignty)
            |
   PRESSURES  full security impossible · pollution crosses
   boundaries · distance eliminated in communication ·
   economy globalised · crime globalised
            |
   VERDICT  "nation-state will have to play a NEW ROLE for
   which it should CONCEDE NECESSARY ADJUSTMENT in its
   AUTHORITY AND SOVEREIGNTY"
   and  "there is NO CERTAINTY about the future form of
   the state"
            |
   RETURN OF CIVIL SOCIETY  two senses · Tocqueville ·
   Held · Cohen-Arato · Hirst · Putnam · Solidarity ·
   Ecoglasnost · 1989
            |
   CRITICAL APPRAISAL  "not an unmixed blessing":
   elitist/oligarchic organs · UNCIVIL MOVEMENTS using
   democratic means for undemocratic ends
            |
   🧩 THE SUPPLEMENT ANSWERS WHAT THE CHAPTER LEFT OPEN
   how a single state holds together people who differ
   SECULARISM (religion and state power)
   MULTICULTURALISM (groups and common citizenship)
```

## Map 3 — The five distinctions, side by side

| Pair | ✅ What is shared | ✅ What separates them | ✅ What the distinction protects | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **State / society** | The same people | ⚠️ Scope — **all their needs of life—from cradle to grave** vs **their particular need of political organization**; mode — seven social determinants vs **command and obedience** | ⚠️ **Saving individual from absolutist, authoritarian and totalitarian rule** | 11-12 |
| **State / civil society** | ⚠️ Both are forms of association | ⚠️ Authority — **supreme legal authority, i.e. sovereignty** vs **no formal, legal authority; at best... moral authority**; jurisdiction — **compulsory** vs **none anywhere** | ⚠️ Preventing **authoritarianism**; **the bedrock of civil liberties** | 13-16, 40 |
| **State / government** | ⚠️ Government acts for the state | ⚠️ **The organization is greater than the organ**; one is **abstract**, the other **concrete** | ⚠️ The standing to **criticize or condemn the government, and still acclaim the greatness of our state** | 8, 17-18 |
| **Nation / nationality** | ⚠️ Shared history and aspirations appear in both | ⚠️ Nationality is **mostly based on birth** and **separates**; the nation includes **different nationalities** | ⚠️ Plural statehood; rejection of **a separate state for each nationality** | 19-20 |
| **Nation / state** | ⚠️ Generally regarded as **concomitant** | ⚠️ The nation needs a **sense of unity** and **common destiny** — attitudes, not institutions | ⚠️ Honesty about the three mismatches | 20, 32 |

## Map 4 — The civil-society sequence, as a single argument

```text
   STAGE 1  CIVIL SOCIETY = THE STATE

                        |

                        |
   STAGE 3  THE RELOCATION (economy -> culture)

               'structures of legitimation'
               + political society = 'structures of coercion'
               = 'structures of domination'
     Marcuse : capitalism -> civil society eclipses the state
               communism  -> the state eclipses civil society
               => secure INDEPENDENCE OF BOTH
                        |
   STAGE 4  THE CONTEMPORARY SENSE
     'intermediate' associations between individual

     product of FREEDOM OF ASSOCIATION · bedrock of CIVIL
     LIBERTIES · channel of communication · shock-absorber
                        |
   STAGE 5  THE DEMOCRATIC THEORY
     Tocqueville : counterweight to the tyranny of majority
                   'schools of democracy'
     Held        : domestic · economic · cultural · political,
                   outside direct state control
     Cohen-Arato : distinct from state AND market;
                   SUPPLEMENT, not replacement

                        |
   STAGE 7  THE APPRAISAL
     "not an unmixed blessing"
     elitist/oligarchic organs · UNCIVIL MOVEMENTS
```

## Map 5 — The two sources, and what each can and cannot answer

| Question | ✅ Gauba, Ch.6 | ✅ 🧩 *Socio-Political Philosophy*, pp.144-159 |
|---|---|---|
| What is a state? | ✅ Fully | ❌ Not addressed |
| What are its elements? | ✅ Fully | ❌ Not addressed |
| How does it differ from society, civil society, government, nation? | ✅ Fully | ❌ Not addressed |
| How did it evolve? | ✅ Fully | ❌ Not addressed |
| Nationalism and internationalism? | ✅ Fully | ⚠️ Only as a comparison with multiculturalism |
| Civil society? | ✅ Fully | ❌ Not addressed |
| How should a state treat religious difference? | ⚠️ Only that population **need not belong to a single... religion** and that the state **claims to reconcile** | ✅ Fully — both models, six features, six critiques |
| How should a state treat cultural difference? | ⚠️ Only **diverse identities** in passing, and the nation that includes **different nationalities** | ✅ Fully — descriptive/normative, four models, minority rights, nine critiques |
| What is the future of the nation-state? | ✅ **No certainty about the future form of the state** | ✅ 🧩 Successor ideology, retreat to nationalism, or genuine cosmopolitanism |

⚠️ Read the table downward: the two sources are complementary and barely overlap. ✅ That is why this topic needs both, and why ❌ neither can be substituted for the other.

## Map 6 — The objection-and-reply spine of the whole topic

| # | ⚠️ Objection | ✅ Reply | ⚠️ Residual |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The concept of the state is formal, moralised and parochial | Weber defines by **means**; nomadic communities are conceded a **political system** but not a state | ⚠️ The formalism objection — politics beyond the state — is never fully answered |
| 2 | The state's survival is argued from usage, not validity | The definitional work of pp.148-149 is the substantive reply | ⚠️ Route 4's agent-concept is asserted and never developed |
| 3 | **Legitimate** smuggles evaluation back into Weber's definition | Read sociologically as acknowledged entitlement, supported by the permitted-force passage | ⚠️ The chapter never says which sense it uses; **the state commands our loyalty** leans the other way |
| 4 | Government is an element and yet not co-terminous with the state | The element is the standing **procedure**; the incumbents change | ⚠️ The domestic suspension of that procedure is not addressed |
| 5 | Society's priority proves nothing about authority | The claim is **dependence**, verified by stateless societies | ⚠️ Modern society is partly constituted by legal categories the state supplies |
| 6 | Gramsci's reading of family, school and church is reductive | The claim is functional, not exhaustive | ⚠️ Whether legitimation predominates is never tested; MacIver's contrary account stands unreconciled |
| 7 | Civil society's benign functions are asserted | They depend on independence, which Marcuse's remedy demands | ⚠️ No test for independence; **uncivil movements** show the cost |
| 8 | The nation is defined so inclusively that it collapses into the state | The nation adds a **subjective** element — unity and destiny | ⚠️ No account of how that sense is produced, except by invention |
| 9 | Self-determination cannot identify its beneficiaries | Tied to **a defined territory** and to alien rule in practice | ⚠️ The post-colonial secession case is untouched |
| 10 | The six-form typology is unilinear and Eurocentric | Attributed to **sociologists**; transitions explained by named causes | ⚠️ The section's title still names one destination |
| 11 | Colonialism is explained monocausally | The claim concerns **the obvious intention**, not every occasion | ⚠️ Strategic and ideological motives are absent |
| 12 | Socialist states became nation-states for no stated reason | **Practical necessity** carries the explanation | ⚠️ **Harsh realities of human nature** is unargued and un-Marxian |
| 13 | The precedence rule is unworkable in practice | Operated through cooperation and acceptance, not imposition | ⚠️ **Reasonable** and **necessary** are undefined |
| 14 | Convergence theory and its critics are left unadjudicated | The chapter gives the critics the last word and endorses Bell's warning | ⚠️ Which evidence survives the criticism is never said |
| 15 | The third world is an incoherent category | Held together by shared history and a shared task | ⚠️ That basis is a reconstruction, not the chapter's statement |
| 16 | 🧩 The Western model protects injustice inside communities | The prohibition that protects also prevents favouring | ⚠️ **The broad limits set by the law of the land** is undefined |
| 17 | 🧩 **Principled distance** permits anything | Governed by **peace, freedom and equality** and aimed at **institutionalized religious domination** | ⚠️ No procedure, and no account of who decides |
| 18 | 🧩 Group rights bind unchosen members | Formal equality is itself a settlement; representation rights enfranchise | ⚠️ The dissenting member's position is left open — **the right to opt out** |
| 19 | 🧩 Four models and nine critiques yield no position | All are testable against the shared aim, to **reconcile cultural diversity with civic cohesion** | ⚠️ The internal-minorities conflict lies *within* the aim and is unresolved |
